Then the actors, splendidly gowned, would make the customary wishes for the Imperial Peace, Prosperity, Longevity, after which there would be a posture-play in costume, and then the plays for the day would begin. On Theater days Their Majesties would lunch and dine together in the Imperial loge. They did not sit at the ends of the great table, but at right angles to each other﻿—the Emperor at the head of the table, and the Empress Dowager at his left. His Majesty was not much of an epicure. He ate fast, and apparently did not care what it was. When he finished, he would stand up near Her Majesty, or walk around the Throne-room until she had finished.

===Chinese===
每逢初一、十五，御用戏班便在戏楼演戏。这几日，皇上不回自己的宫中，而是随皇太后和女官们从觐见殿一同到戏楼去。二位陛下进戏楼庭院之时奏国乐，待他们入了御用包厢，戏班诸伶便一齐上台叩头。然后，华服盛装的伶人们便照例祝颂皇上太平、昌盛、万寿无疆，接着演一出扮相戏，再后才是当日正戏开场。有戏之日，二位陛下一并在御用包厢用午膳和晚膳。他们并不坐在大长桌的两端，而是互成直角而坐——皇上坐在上首，皇太后坐在他的左手边。皇上并不算是个讲究吃食之人。他吃得极快，似乎并不在乎吃的是什么。他吃完之后，便在皇太后近旁站着，或在宝座殿里来回踱步，等她用毕。

===Original===
The Empress Dowager was very rigorous in the observance of all fasts, as well as feasts, prescribed by the rites. On fast-days, no meat nor fish was eaten at her table. The meals consisted entirely of vegetables, bread, and rice; but there was always a great variety of these dishes, and they were temptingly prepared. Meat dishes and fish were always prepared for me when I was invited to eat at the Imperial table on fast-days, until I learned that the Empress Dowager and the Ladies were fasting, when I asked to eat only what was prepared for them when I dined with them at Her Majesty’s table.

===Chinese===
凡礼制所定的斋日和节令，无论斋戒还是庆贺，皇太后都遵守得一丝不苟。斋日她在桌上不用任何肉食和鱼。膳食全用蔬菜、面食和米饭；但这类菜肴花色总是极多，且烹制得十分诱人。我受邀在斋日到御桌上同膳之时，他们一向另为预备荤菜和鱼，直到后来我得知皇太后与女官们都在持斋，便请他们以后我与她们同在皇太后桌上用膳时，只备与她们相同的斋菜即可。

===Original===
On Festivals and Theater days, Princesses of the Imperial Family, wives of Manchu Nobles, and high Officials were invited to spend the day at the Palace. Sometimes their children would accompany them, little girls and boys under twelve. I never saw a boy over seventeen in the Palace; and only once, one sixteen years old. This was a son of Prince Ching. When these young people came to the Court, they observed the same rules of etiquette as their elders, and behaved with great decorum. Her Majesty is very fond of children, but very particular as to their manners. When a little girl did not make a graceful bow, Her Majesty would not correct her, but would ask the young Empress, an authority on etiquette and very graceful, to bow. Her Majesty would then tell the little girl to notice how the Empress bowed and try to do it in that manner. The child, or her parents, generally followed this suggestion, and the grace of the bow was improved on the next visit to Court!

===Chinese===
每逢节庆和有戏之日，皇室公主、满洲贵胄的夫人和高官们都蒙召入宫消磨一日。有时她们也带着孩子同来，都是十二岁以下的小男孩和小女孩。我在宫中从未见过十七岁以上的男孩；只有一次见过一个十六岁的。那是庆亲王（Prince Ching）之子。这些少年人到了宫廷，所遵的礼数与长辈相同，举止甚为端庄。皇太后极喜孩子，但对他们的仪态却又极其讲究。若哪个小女孩行的礼不够好看，皇太后并不亲自纠正，却要请皇后——她是礼数上的权威，举止极为优雅——行一个礼。然后皇太后便告诉那小女孩，留意皇后是怎么行的，叫她也照样去做。那孩子、或是她的父母，多半会听从这番提示，待下次再入宫时，那礼数便行得好看多了！

===Original===
On one occasion, a lady of high rank, married to a kinsman of the Empress Dowager, was invited to the Palace with her family. She had two little girls, and when the family went up to bow and repeat the salutation to Her Majesty, the younger daughter, only five years old, refused either to make the bow or repeat the salutation, but sat down on the floor and cried! The Empress Dowager waited patiently for the mother to correct the little girl, for she is very fond of children and disposed to condone their faults. The little girl would not, however, listen to reason and continued to show temper. Her Majesty could not allow such a breach of the “Proprieties,” even in a child of this age, and the high rank of the family of the little girl made it the more imperative that she should conform to the rules of Propriety and observe the etiquette of the Court. When Her Majesty saw that all efforts at bringing her to reason were fruitless, she ordered the child to be taken away. Whereupon the mother began crying, and begged her not to be offended with the little girl. She replied, “Do you think a person of superior intelligence could be offended with a baby? I send you out of the Palace to teach you a lesson, which you must teach your child. I do not blame her; I blame you and pity her; but she must suffer as well as yourself. You must teach your child that ‘it is by the rules of propriety that the character is established’ (Confucius)”; and she was inexorable. The family left the Palace and was not invited again for some time.

===Chinese===
有一次，一位身份极高的夫人——她嫁给了皇太后的本家亲戚——携家眷应召入宫。她有两个小女孩，当一家人上前向皇太后行礼请安时，那小女儿才五岁，既不肯行礼，也不肯请安，竟一屁股坐到地上大哭起来！皇太后耐心地等着那位母亲把小女孩管教好，因为她极爱孩子，原易宽宥孩子的小过。然而这小女孩却不听劝，一味地闹脾气。皇太后不能容忍这等失“礼”之事，即便在这样年纪的孩子身上也不可；况且这小女孩出身门第如此之高，便更须要她遵从礼法、恪守宫廷的规矩。皇太后见种种开导都归无效，便吩咐把孩子带走。那位母亲于是哭了起来，求她莫要怪罪小女孩。皇太后答道：“你以为一个有见识的人，会同一个小娃娃计较吗？我把你们遣出宫去，是为给你一个教训，这个教训你务必也教给你的孩子。我并不怪她；我怪的是你，也怜惜她；但她也必须同你一道受这苦。你须教导你的孩子：‘立身在于礼’（孔子）。”她言出必行，绝不改易。这一家子便出了宫，此后很长一段时候没再蒙召。

## XXV Peking—Beginning the Portrait for St. Louis / 第二十五章 北京——着手为圣路易斯画像

===Original===
The Legation quarter of Peking lies in the Tartar City, just under the walls of the Imperial City. The United States Legation, in 1904, occupied a Chinese Temple on the canal, at the left of the "Water Gate," the opening of which was exacted by the Allies in 1900. Before this time, there was no gate between the Chien-Mên and the Hata-Mên.

It was a picturesque jaunt in the early morning that I had from the United States Legation to the Palace. My cart rattled down the road, running parallel to the canal, past the splendid enclosure of the English Legation to the "Glacis," and across the Marble Bridge, that traverses it, to the narrow street under the great red walls of the Imperial City. The walls all over China are wonderful feats of architecture, the culminating point of the science of the Chinese builder. The "Great Wall," long counted one of the wonders of the world, is one of many in China, and only remarkable on account of its size and great length. Nearly every town and city in China has massive, well-constructed walls, which, with their splendid gate-towers, make them really remarkable works of architecture. Even the palaces and parks of the rich have fine walls, the monotony of their line varied by the turreted summerhouses which surmount their angles. These walls, quite overtopping the cities and houses they enclose, with their watchtowers permitting their defenders to see at great distances, must, in medieval times, have been a splendid protection against the attacks of enemies or the inroads of barbarians.

===Chinese===
北京的使馆区位于鞑靼城（内城）之内，紧贴皇城墙下。1904 年，美国使馆占用了运河边的一座中国庙宇，在"水门"左侧——这道门是 1900 年联军迫使开设的。在此以前，前门（Chien-Mên）与哈德门（Hata-Mên）之间并无门户可通。

清晨从美国使馆前往宫廷，一路景致如画。我的马车辚辚驶过那条与运河平行的道路，掠过英国使馆恢宏的院墙，来到"格拉斯斜坡"（Glacis），再越过横跨其上的汉白玉石桥，便驶入皇城高大红墙下那条狭窄的街道。中国各地的城墙都是建筑上的奇观，是中国营造之学的登峰造极之作。久被列为世界奇迹之一的"万里长城"，在中国不过是众多城墙中的一座，只因体量宏大、绵延无尽而格外著称。中国几乎每一座城镇都有厚重坚固的城墙，配上巍峨的城门楼，使其真正成为令人叹服的建筑。即便是富户的宅邸园林，也有精美的院墙，墙顶转角处常筑有小亭，打破了线条的单调。这些高墙远超其所围护的城市屋舍，墙上的瞭望楼使守者可远眺四方；在中世纪，面对敌人的进攻或蛮族的寇掠，它们必然是极佳的屏障。

===Original===
The main thoroughfares of the Tartar City are very wide, with a raised causeway, about two feet high, in the center. When Their Majesties go abroad, this is covered with yellow sand and is used as an Imperial roadway. Ordinarily any cart or chair, irrespective of the rank of the occupant, may use it. It is always kept in excellent condition, and seems to be a survival of the raised roads that Marco Polo speaks of in describing the grounds of the Palace of Kublai Khan. The lower roads on either side of this raised causeway are generally in a lamentable state. Itinerant cooks ply their odorous trade of frying greaseballs, etc.; barbers shave their clients and act as manicures and chiropodists, in full view of the passerby; venders of old iron, clothes, vegetables, etc, spread out their wares in the middle of the road, in reckless disregard of the wandering fowls, dogs, and even pigs, which roam about. Pools of stagnant water and piles of refuse add their quota to the malodorous confusion. Still the streets are not unpicturesque. The elaborately carved fronts of the shops, the graceful signs, with their red pennants, the gaily colored lanterns swinging to and fro, the great umbrellas unfurled here and there over the itinerant venders, all have a certain sort of charm.

===Chinese===
鞑靼城的主要街道极宽阔，正中是一道约两尺高的隆起御道。两宫（皇太后与皇上）出宫时，道上便铺满黄沙，充作御路。平日里，无论乘车者品秩尊卑，任何马车、轿子都可经由行走。这条道保养得极为平整，似是马可·波罗（Marco Polo）笔下忽必烈汗宫苑中那种高台道路的遗存。御道两侧的低洼道路则大多凄惨不堪。沿街的流动厨子叫卖着炸油丸之类气味浓烈的营生；剃头匠当街为顾客剃头，兼修指甲、理脚鸡眼，任凭路人围观；卖旧铁、旧衣、蔬菜的小贩，将货物摊在路中央，全然不顾四下乱窜的鸡犬乃至猪只。一汪汪死水、一堆堆垃圾，更为这腥臊纷乱的街景添了几分。然而这些街道倒并非全无画意：店铺雕镂精美的门面，飘逸的招牌配上红色幡旗，五彩灯笼摇曳其间，流动摊贩顶上随处撑开的大伞，都自有一番风韵。

===Original===
After entering the gate of the Imperial City, the roads are gay with carts, official chairs, and handsomely caparisoned horses. We sometimes met attachés of the Wai-Wu-Pu and different Yamens hurrying to and fro with despatches, or caught a glimpse, in the depths of his green chair, of one of the great ministers, the thin white-bearded face of Prince Ching, or the heavy Jewish-looking physiognomy of Na-Tung, the new minister, who seems likely to grow in favor. Sometimes we passed a bridal procession, with its gay, red-embroidered chairs, or some splendid funeral, with the great red catafalque, covered with magnificent embroideries (for red is used alike for wedding chairs and for funeral decorations)—its massive, long poles held by hundreds of red-gowned bearers and accompanied by the motley crew of figurants, who are always hired for funeral celebrations in China; the catafalque, followed by white-covered carts, carrying the mourning white-garbed women of the family. Sometimes we passed a crowd of yellow-gowned lama priests and monks returning from some celebration in the Palace—sometimes, great droves of camels laden with coal from the mountains or produce from afar. During the annual visit of the Mongolian Princes to Peking we met them with their fur-dressed and leather-booted followers, their quaintly caparisoned horses, and splendidly bedecked camels, for they were domiciled in Palaces, within the Imperial City.

All this we could see as we went on our way to the Great Gate of the Palace, itself. Within the walls and at the gate of the Empress Dowager's Palace, with the usual Chinese tolerance, the consideration of the great for the poor, beggars are allowed to come at certain times each day, to receive remnants from the Imperial kitchen. The poor are also permitted to examine the garbage of the Palace, before it is carted away. There was always a motley crew of ragged beggars around this gate, who received, apparently, kind consideration from the soldiers and guards. At least, they were allowed to ply their trade and to follow their avocations in peace.

===Chinese===
进入皇城大门之后，道上便热闹起来，马车、官轿、装饰华美的骏马络绎不绝。我们时而遇见外务部（Wai-Wu-Pu）与各衙门（Yamen）的差官捧着公文匆匆往来，时而能在绿色大轿的深处瞥见某位重臣——庆亲王（Prince Ching）清癯的白须面孔，或是新任大臣那桐（Na-Tung）那副厚重的、看似犹太人的面相，此人似乎正日渐见重。有时我们遇见迎亲的队伍，花轿艳红绣彩；有时是盛大的丧仪，巨大的红色灵辇覆以绚烂的绣幛（因红色既用于婚轿，亦用于丧仪）——粗长的杠木由数百名红袍杠夫肩起，前后簇拥着中国丧事中惯常雇来充作仪仗的杂色人等；灵辇之后，跟着覆以白帷的马车，载着家族中服丧的白衣女眷。有时我们遇见成群黄袍的喇嘛僧人，从宫中某场庆典归来——有时则遇见大队骆驼，驮着山中的煤炭或远方的物产。蒙古王公每年入京觐见时，我们也曾与他们相遇：扈从们身着皮裘、足登皮靴，马匹鞍辔古朴，骆驼披挂华美——他们都被安置在皇城以内的宫邸之中。

前往宫门正门的大道，沿途这些情景尽收眼底。在皇太后宫院的墙内宫门处，依着中国人惯有的宽厚——即尊贵者对贫苦者的体恤——每日定时容许乞丐前来领取御膳房的余食。宫中弃物运出之前，亦许穷人在其中翻检。这道宫门处总聚着一群衣衫褴褛的乞丐，看得出颇受兵丁守卫的善待——至少他们得以在此安心讨生计，无人惊扰。

===Original===
I found the light, in the magnificent hall which had now become my studio, so obscure, even in front of the great plate-glass doors, that it was almost useless to attempt to work. After trying to do so for two or three days, I told the head eunuch it was impossible. I did not wish to trouble Her Majesty with my annoyances, for she had enough of her own cares, and seemed to grow daily more and more anxious and depressed over the constantly growing rumors of war in Manchuria; but it was impossible to work longer where I was, and I decided I would have one of the ends of the hall, which projected beyond the overhanging eaves of the verandah, fitted up for my work, and in order to have sufficient light, even here, it was necessary to have the upper paper windows replaced by plate-glass. The eunuchs demurred. They said this would necessitate great changes, with heavy expense, besides establishing a precedent, as no other part of the Palace had plate-glass windows at the top! The next time the Empress Dowager came in, I told her it was impossible to work as it was. She, herself, remarked how dark it was, and noticed the reflection from the yellow roof opposite. And when she heard what I wished, she ordered it to be done at once, saying she would "speak to the Emperor" about it. An order of Her Majesty's was always promptly carried out, and two days after, to my astonishment, the plate-glass windows were placed as I wished. I had the divan that was built under the windows removed, and all the furniture taken out of this end of the hall. The eunuchs hesitated about removing an immense elephant clock of wonderful mechanism, as it had not been moved for a hundred and fifty years, but I finally accomplished even this! Even without the furniture, this end of the hall was but a small space in which to work; but I had a fairly good light, and a quiet place to paint in, for the first time since I began painting the Empress Dowager. Here I was sufficiently far away from Her Majesty's apartments, as well as from those of the Princesses and Ladies, to be able to work in quiet, without interruptions. A set of European furniture had been placed in the great hall, when it was decided to give it to me, and though this did not please me, in an artistic sense, it being absolutely out of keeping with its environment, I found the well-cushioned easy-chairs a real comfort when I wanted to rest.

===Chinese===
那座已辟为我画室的宏伟大厅，光线极为幽暗，即便站在巨大的玻璃门之前也几乎无从下笔。试了两三天之后，我告诉首领太监这样实在不行。我不愿拿这些烦扰去搅扰皇太后——她自身的烦忧已够多了，加之满洲（Manchuria）战事传闻日甚一日，她似乎日渐焦灼抑郁。但我在原地实在无法再工作下去，便决定将大厅一端——那处探出回廊飞檐之外的头——辟作我的画所；此处为了采光充足，必须把上层的纸窗换作玻璃。太监们面有难色，说这样一来工程浩大、所费不赀，而且会开一先例，因为宫中别处都没有在窗顶上装玻璃的！待皇太后下次进来，我告诉她眼下实在无法作画。她自己也说这里的确太暗，并注意到了对面黄色琉璃屋顶反射下来的阴影。听明我的请求，她当即吩咐照办，并说她去"同皇上说"。皇太后的懿旨向来雷厉风行，两天之后，玻璃窗便如我所愿装好了——实在出我意料。我又叫人把窗下原有的炕撤了，将这头大厅里的家具尽数搬出。太监们对一座机件精巧的巨大象形钟颇有顾虑，说它已有一百五十年未曾动过，但我到底连这也搬成了！即便搬空了家具，大厅这一端可作画之处也仍逼仄；但自开始为皇太后作画以来，我总算有了相当不错的光线，和一个安静的作画之所——这里距皇太后的寝宫以及诸位福晋、女眷的居处都相当远，可以不受打扰地安心作画。大厅既决定拨给我用，便置了一套洋式家具。这从艺术上讲并不合我意——与四周的环境全然不谐——但当我想要歇息时，那些软垫厚厚的安乐椅倒是实在的舒服。

===Original===
As soon as I was comfortably settled in my new studio, the Empress Dowager began to talk of having another large portrait begun—large enough to represent her with all the paraphernalia of Royalty (the ceremonial fans, the threefold screen, the nine phoenix, plants of heavenly bamboo) and pyramids of apples—all emblematic, or symbolic. I told Her Majesty it would be best to make a small study for this picture, and that the size of the portrait could be determined on after this was finished. She readily assented, and I began the small study. There were a number of beautiful thrones in the Palace, any one of which would have suited the lines of the composition. I selected one of the superb, antique thrones of red lacquer, a magnificent work of art, but the Empress Dowager did not care for this throne. It was not a matter of whether the lines or color suited the picture, the point was to have everything *Ho-shih* (proper), as the Chinese say. With the Chinese, propriety is a religion, and a thing that is "proper" must conform to tradition, for tradition and propriety are synonymous. The question of the throne was left in abeyance for the moment, as Her Majesty said there was one she would like to have painted, which she would have found before I began the big picture.

===Chinese===
我在新画室刚安顿妥当，皇太后便谈起要再画一幅大的画像——大到足以将她与全套皇家仪仗（仪仗扇、三扇屏、九凤、南天竹）以及堆成金字塔的苹果一同画出——这些都带有徽号或象征之意。我禀明皇太后，这幅画最好先作一幅小幅试笔，画像的尺寸待此画完成后再行定夺。她欣然应允，我便着手这幅小幅试笔。宫中有若干张华美的宝座，任一张都合于构图的线条。我挑中了一张华贵的古董红漆宝座，本是件绝美的艺术品，但皇太后却不喜欢这张宝座。问题不在线条或色彩是否合于画面，而在于一切都要如中国人所说"合适"（Ho-shih）。在中国人，"合宜"近乎一种信仰；所谓"合适"之事，必合于传统，因为传统与合宜本就是同义。宝座之事暂且搁下，皇太后说有一张她愿被画入其中，待我动笔画大幅之前，她会让人找出来。

### Prince Ching / 庆亲王

===Original===
I finally began the sketch. Her Majesty was dressed in one of her official winter gowns. Its fur lining rendered the already heavily embroidered satin stiffer than ever, and any stray folds that might perchance have appeared, were pulled out by a heavy fringe of pearls around the hem. She had on her famous pearl mantle over an official jacket. In her coiffure she wore her long tassel of pearls, and many curious ceremonial jewels. She had on fur-lined undersleeves, which hid half her beautiful hands. The effect of her tiny fingertips, with their long curving nails and jeweled shields, the palms not being visible, was most unfortunate. Added to this, she held them tightly together in her lap, and the lines were obscured by a large, pale-blue handkerchief in one hand.

My heart fell. Thus I would lose one of her chief beauties. I begged Lady Yu-Keng to ask her to pose her hands differently. She said she could not do such a thing; so, in my inelegant Chinese, I told Her Majesty I did not like her hands as they were. "But I like them like that," she said, looking at me with a charming expression of amused astonishment, amazed that it was possible for anyone not to like what she liked; and she kept her hands as they were, and I was obliged to begin the picture with the hands in that position.

The first sketch was quickly made, and Her Majesty expressed herself as pleased with it. Then came the discussion as to the size of the portrait. I made my measurements, and thought five feet by eight was large enough, but when she saw what size it was going to be, she thought six feet by ten would be better. The Palace carpenters were accordingly called in, and I gave them as accurate directions as I could, for making a stretcher. The Chinese workmen are clever, patient, and apt at carrying out suggestions, and the stretcher was satisfactorily made. But the canvas was to be put on this stretcher, and this they seemed to have no idea of, so I was obliged to try to do it myself. Owing to the size of the canvas, I was compelled to stand on a stool six feet high (they had no ladders), with the huge stretcher before me. An army of eunuchs stood around to assist me, presided over by a head eunuch. I used the iron pincers and pulled the canvas, myself. It was held at the corners by eunuchs, also on stools; one eunuch held the tacks, another the hammer, etc. Each order I gave was repeated in a loud voice by the head eunuch, and at every failure to comprehend my directions, the working eunuchs were rebuked and threatened with the "bamboo." Finally, I accomplished the difficult task, and the great canvas was stretched. Her Majesty was greatly exercised when she learned I had done it myself. She said that I should have made the eunuchs "stretch four or five," until they learned to do one properly. But I hadn't sufficient canvas for such experiments, and could get no more in China.

===Chinese===
我终于着手起稿。皇太后身着一件朝服冬装。皮裘的衬里使那本已绣工繁复的缎袍愈发硬挺，下摆偶然可能浮现的些许褶皱，也被下摆一重厚重的珍珠缘饰拉得平直。她在朝服马褂之外，又披上了那件有名的珍珠氅衣。发髻上缀着长长的珍珠流苏，以及许多珍奇的礼仪珠宝。她内着皮裘衬里的窄袖，掩去了一双玉手的大半。那纤纤指尖，配着长而弯曲的指甲与镶珠的甲套，又因不见掌心，效果颇为不妙。更何况她两手紧紧相握置于膝上，一只手中一方淡蓝的大手帕更遮住了线条。

我的心一下子沉了下去。如此一来，我便要失去她最动人的一处美处。我央求裕庚夫人（Lady Yu-Keng）去请她将手势改一改。她说她不能做这样的事；于是我以自己并不雅驯的中文，对皇太后说，我不喜欢她手放成那样。"可我就喜欢这样，"她说，带着一种动人而饶有兴味的惊讶神情望着我——似乎诧异至极，竟会有人不喜欢她所喜欢的。她便仍把手放在原处，我也只得就着这个手势动笔画了起来。

初稿很快完成，皇太后表示满意。接着便议起画像的尺寸。我量了量，觉得五英尺乘八英尺已足够大；但当她得知将是多大时，认为六英尺乘十英尺更好。于是传宫中的木匠进来，我尽可能精确地吩咐他们做一只画布框。中国的工匠聪敏、耐烦、善于照吩咐行事，画框做得颇为合用。然而要将画布绷到框上，他们却似乎全然不知所措，我只得自己动手。因画布太大，我不得不站在一张六英尺高的凳子上（宫中没有梯子），面前是那只巨大的画框。一群太监环立相助，由一位首领太监统领。我自己执铁钳拉拽画布。画布四角由太监们——也站在凳子上——拉住；一个太监执钉，一个执锤，各司其职。我每发一道指令，首领太监便高声复述一遍；每逢他们领会错了我的意思，干活的太监便遭申斥，并以"竹板子"相胁。终于，我完成了这桩难事，巨大的画布绷成了。皇太后得知我亲手所做，大为震动。她说我本该让太监们"绷上四五回"，直到他们学会把一只绷好为止。可我没有那么多画布供此试验，在中国也无法再弄到。

## X Peking—The Sea Palace / 第十章 北京——西苑

===Original===
The Autumnal Sacrifices to his Ancestors and His Majesty’s consequent three days’ abstinence, to prepare for them, put a stop to further festivities after the Birthday, which would have otherwise continued for several days longer. The day after the Birthday was a quiet one at the Palace. Her Majesty was feeling tired and did not care to pose, after the Audience in the morning. The visiting Princesses and Ladies were preparing to leave the Palace; the eunuchs and Her Majesty’s maids were bustling around, preparing for the moving of the Court to Peking, for Her Majesty and the Court, as well as the Emperor, were to go into one of the City Palaces the following day. Her Majesty ordered luncheon to be served in one of the beautiful summerhouses in the gardens, about a mile from the Palace, for she said a change would be good for all.

This summerhouse, or rather Palace, situated on a hill overlooking the lake, was one of Her Majesty’s favorite resorts. She often went to it, after a tiring Audience, and spent the rest of the day there, lunching and dining, and even taking her siesta there. Whenever she went to any of these Palaces inside the enclosure, she always invited all the Ladies of the Court to accompany her. It made a change in the monotony of their lives. This Palace was very luxuriously fitted up, and contained a splendid library, with thousands of volumes of the classics and Her Majesty’s favorite authors. The view from its broad verandahs and fair marble terraces was one of the finest, even of the many beautiful ones, in the grounds. We lunched on the wide verandah and drank in the beauty of the scene. No wonder Her Majesty loved this spot! Beneath lay the beautiful grounds of the Summer Palace, with its calm lake and winding streams. On an eminence beyond, the graceful seven-storied pagoda that forms so characteristic a feature in all the views of the Summer Palace, proudly reared its stately height. On the right lay the temple-crowned hills, the upturned roofs of their buildings nestling on their slopes like a flight of gigantic gaily-hued birds, with wings outspread. In the distance, beyond a soft gray undulating landscape, with fields of brilliant green here and there, lay Peking, with its walls and towers, enveloped in a golden haze.

===Chinese===
皇上须为先祖的秋祭斋戒三日，以资预备，于是皇太后万寿之后那些本可再延续数日的庆典，便就此告一段落。万寿的次日，宫中颇觉清静。皇太后觉着疲倦，清晨觐见之后，便不欲再坐班了。前来朝贺的公主和命妇们正打点着出宫；太监与侍女们忙忙碌碌，为宫廷迁往北京作准备——因为皇太后与宫眷，连同皇上，次日都要移居城内的一处宫邸。皇太后吩咐在园子里一里开外的一座精美凉亭中传午膳，说是换一换地方，于众人都有益。

这凉亭——其实更像一座宫殿——坐落在俯瞰湖面的山岗之上，是皇太后最钟爱的去处之一。每逢觐见之后疲惫不堪，她常到此处来，将一日余下的时光都在这里度过，午饭、晚膳，乃至午睡，都在这里。但凡她前往禁苑之内的任何一处宫殿，总要请宫中所有命妇随行，这总算给她们单调的日子添几分变化。此宫陈设极为奢华，内有一间极宏富的书房，藏书数千卷，经史百家以及皇太后所喜爱的作者，无不备具。从它宽阔的游廊和那洁白的大理石台榭上望出去的景色，即便在这园中那许多美景之间，也算得最为壮丽的一处。我们在宽阔的廊上进午膳，将这眼前美景一并饱览。也难怪皇太后这般喜爱此地！脚下便是颐和园那一片美丽的园囿，湖水澄澈，溪流蜿蜒。远处一座高岗之上，那座姿态轻盈的七层宝塔——它是颐和园所有景色中最具标志性的一处——傲然挺立，耸出它那端严的塔身。右边是那几座庙宇覆顶的山峦，殿宇的飞檐依偎在山坡之上，犹如一群硕大无朋的彩羽之鸟，展翅栖于其间。远处，在一片柔和的灰色起伏原野之外，时有一方一方碧绿欲滴的田畴点缀其间，北京城便卧在那里——它的城墙与城楼，尽笼罩在一层金色的薄霭之中。

### At the American Legation, Peking / 于北京美国公使馆

===Original===
After luncheon and the siesta, Her Majesty called me up and said she was to go into Peking on the morrow, and asked whether I wished the portrait to be taken in for the three days the Court was to remain in the City. She said she would be much occupied with ceremonies and sacrifices, and there would be but little time for painting, but if I wished to work she might be able to give me a short sitting! I told her I did not care to have the portrait taken into the City, for I knew it would not be possible to get a room with the same light as that in which I had begun the picture. When she found I did not care to paint in Peking, she suggested that I go to the United States Legation and spend the time of the Court’s sojourn at the Sea Palace. It had been more than two weeks since I had seen Mrs. Conger, or been in the Legation quarter, and I was delighted at Her Majesty’s kind forethought in allowing me to spend these days at the Legation. She, however, suggested that, as I had not seen the Sea Palace, where the Court was to go, I might enjoy coming there for the day﻿—and spending some of the time in seeing the Palace and grounds. She knew how I enjoyed seeing these beautiful Palaces, and this was another proof of her consideration. She said she would be much occupied with the ceremonies, but that she would map out a nice day for me, and would herself take me for a walk! She added, “This will give you a chance to study me, so your time will not be entirely spent in vain.” She said we would resume the portrait on the Court’s return to the Summer Palace.

After our return to her Throne-room, and when we had finished dinner, she told me I had better go into the room where the portrait and my materials were kept, when I was not working on it, and said I had better overlook its being put away myself. She followed me into the room, and herself aided and directed the arrangement of things. She ordered the “sacred picture” (for this is what the Chinese call a likeness of a reigning Emperor or Empress) to be attached to the wall with yellow cords and covered with a transparent yellow silk, boxlike screen, which had been especially made to protect it from dust. The portrait was treated, from its very beginning, as an almost sacred object, with the respect a reverent officiant accords the Holy Vessels of the Church. Even my painting materials seemed to be invested with a sort of semi-sacred quality. When Her Majesty felt fatigued, and indicated that the sittings were finished, my brushes and palette were taken by the eunuch from my hands, the portrait removed from the easel and reverently consigned to the room that had been set aside for it.

===Chinese===
午膳与午睡之后，皇太后把我叫到跟前，说她次日要进北京城，问我想不想趁宫廷在城内逗留的三日，把画像也搬进城去。她说她祭祀与典礼繁多，能用来作画的时间无几；然我若愿意继续动笔，她或可给我坐一会儿！我禀明她，不愿把画像搬进城——因我知道，城里断然寻不出一间与开笔之处光线相同的屋子。她见我不愿在北京作画，便提议我去美国公使馆下榻，度过宫廷驻跸西苑的这段日子。我已有两旬不曾见康格夫人，也两旬不曾到过使馆区，皇太后这一番体贴的远虑，许我到公使馆去住上几日，令我喜出望外。然她又提议道：我既未曾见过宫廷即将前往的西苑，何不前去消磨一日——抽出些时辰游览那宫苑与园囿，定会觉得有趣。她深知我多么爱看这些华美的宫殿，这又是她体恤我的另一证。她说典礼之事必将缠身，但她会替我安排一个称心之日，还要亲自领我去散一回步！她又道：「这也好叫你有机会端详端详我，你的时辰也不至于全然虚度。」她说待两宫回銮颐和园，我们再继续画像。

我们回到她的宝座殿，用罢晚膳之后，她告诉我，我不作画之时，最好亲自到那间存放画像和我画具的屋子里去，亲眼看着人把它收妥。她跟着我走进那间屋子，亲自动手帮着布置调度。她吩咐用黄绫将那张「圣容」（中国人如此称呼在位皇帝或皇太后之画像）系于墙上，再罩上一方透明的明黄丝绸——是一只专为此像避尘而特制的箱形帘子。这张画像，自打起笔之日起，便几乎被视为一件圣物，所受到的敬重，犹如虔敬的司祭敬奉教堂里的圣器一般。便是我那些画具，似乎也沾染了几分半神圣的意味。每逢皇太后觉着疲倦、示意坐班已毕，太监便从我手中接过画笔和调色板，将画像从画架上取下，恭恭敬敬地送进那间专门拨给它安放的屋子。

===Original===
The next morning early, I preceded the Court into Peking and went directly to the United States Legation, where I was warmly welcomed by my kind friends, Mr. and Mrs. Conger. The United States Legation occupied, at this time, a Chinese temple near the “Water Gate.” This building had been given to the United States Government by the Chinese after the Boxer rebellion, and was occupied temporarily by the Minister of the United States during the construction of the new Legation on Legation Street. The temple had been transformed into a comfortable American dwelling-place﻿—its Chinese individuality having been preserved wherever possible, consistent with comfort. The shaded court, filled with beautiful, growing flowers (many of them gifts from the Empress Dowager to Mrs. Conger), was a charming spot. While distinctly American as to its artistic comfort and furniture, the interior construction and decoration of the drawing-room were purely Chinese, which gave a touch of Oriental *couleur locale* to this pleasant haven of American hospitality, where Mr. and Mrs. Conger dispensed their kindly favors.

Mrs. Conger, by her own individual initiative, has done much to bring about a friendly social feeling between the Chinese and foreign ladies. It was she who first thought of entertaining the Princesses and Ladies of the Court in her own home; and the United States Legation was the first of the Legations in Peking to issue an invitation to the Ladies of the Court, or to entertain them. It is the first Legation to entertain other Chinese ladies, wives of officials or of the gentry. Several other Legations have since entertained the Ladies of the Court, but in doing so they were only following Mrs. Conger’s initiative. While doing so much to bring about friendly social relations with the Chinese, Mr. and Mrs. Conger receive all Americans, regardless of their importance or social position, with a kind cordiality. I was delighted to be in their charming family circle once more. I found my room at the Legation, with its sweet touches of homeliness, a delightful haven, and my visits to the Legation seemed always like going home.

===Chinese===
次日清晨，我先于宫廷一程进了北京城，径直前往美国公使馆，康格先生（Mr. Conger）与康格夫人这一对慈和的朋友热忱地迎我入门。此时美国公使馆所占的，是「水门」附近的一座中国庙宇。这所建筑是义和团之乱后中国人赠予美国政府的，待使馆街上新建的公使馆落成之前，美国公使便暂在此处办公。庙宇被改造为一处舒适的美国式居所——其中国原有的风神，只要与起居舒适不相抵触，都尽量保留了下来。那荫凉的天井里摆满了姣好的盆花（其中多是皇太后赠予康格夫人的），是一处极可爱的地方。起坐间的家具与那些讲求舒适的陈设固然是地道的美国风，其内部的构造与装修却是纯粹的中国式样，这就给这处宜人的美国式待客之所，添上了一抹东方的「地方色彩」（couleur locale），康格先生与夫人便是在此殷勤待客。

康格夫人凭着自家的热心主动，在中外女眷之间促成了颇多的友好往来。头一个想到在自己家中款待宫中公主与命妇的，便是她；在北京各公使馆里，率先邀请宫中女眷、设宴相待的，也是美国公使馆。率先款待其他中国夫人——官宦或缙绅之家女眷——的，也是它。其后也有几家公使馆设宴款待过宫中女眷，但不过是承袭康格夫人的先例罢了。在致力促成与中国人友好交际的同时，康格先生与夫人对待一切美国人——无论其地位尊卑、声名大小——一概以热诚相待。能再度置身于他们那可爱的家人之间，我满心欢喜。公使馆里那间属于我的屋子，处处带着几分温馨的家常气，正是我惬意的栖所。每一次到公使馆来，于我都恍如归家。

===Original===
The next morning at seven, a green official chair with its bearers came to take me to the Sea Palace. I was first carried to the Hsien-Liang-Hsu, the “Temple of the loyal and virtuous,” where Li-Hung-Chang formerly had his home in Peking, and a part of which the Yu-Kengs had arranged for their home after their return from their mission at Paris, their own semi-foreign house having been destroyed by the Boxers. At the Hsien-Liang-Hsu I was joined by the Ladies Yu-Keng, and we continued on to the Sea Palace. Our chairs, with their bearers, were preceded and followed by mounted attendants.

The Sea Palace is a comparatively new Palace, most of it having been built within the last fifty years. Our chairs were met at the northern entrance by the same eunuchs who had been set aside for our service at the Summer Palace. They led us to the boats in waiting to carry us across the lake, to the buildings occupied by Her Majesty and the Court. These boats were of the houseboat variety, with an enclosed cabin forming the center, and a platform running all around, on which the rowers walked up and down propelling it. The interior was carpeted, with a cushioned lounge, tea-tables, and chairs. The eunuchs and attendants sat outside on the prow. It takes twenty minutes to row across the lake in one of these boats, but the movement is delightful. When we reached the other side, we landed and went through several courts to that of one of Her Majesty’s private chapels. She, herself, had just been making an offering here, and was coming out, preceded by acolytes swinging incense-burners, the musicians playing the “Imperial Hymn.” When she saw us, Her Majesty called us to her side, asked if I had had a good trip into Peking, and how Mrs. Conger was. She then ordered the eunuchs to show us our apartments. We were led through corridors and courts to a charming pavilion which was to be our resting-place while at the Sea Palace. It had exquisitely and elaborately carved woodwork arches with heavy satin curtains, which divided it into five rooms. After we had rested a few moments here, we returned to the Throne-room. Her Majesty told me she had arranged for me to go out in one of the boats, and that I was to be shown all that I cared to see, or at least as much as I could see in that day. A eunuch standing near her held a number of strips of embroidery in his hand. They were embroidered headdresses, which are placed upon the heads of the Buddhas during the great ceremonies in the Palace temples. She explained their use to me and then dismissed us, and we went out to the landing-place on the lake.

===Chinese===
次日清晨七时，一顶绿轿连同轿夫前来接我到西苑。我先被抬到贤良寺（Hsien-Liang-Hsu）——所谓「忠良之祠」——李鸿章（Li-Hung-Chang）从前在北京的府邸便设于此，裕庚一家从巴黎公使任上归来之后，因自家那所半洋式的宅子已被义和团焚毁，便也将贤良寺的一部分布置为寓所。裕庚家的两位夫人在贤良寺与我会合，我们一同续程前往西苑。我们的轿子，连同轿夫，前后都有骑马的扈从开道殿后。

西苑是一座较为新近的宫殿，大半建于近五十年之内。我们的轿子刚到北门，便有原先在颐和园里派来伺候我们的那些太监迎上来。他们领我们上了泊在岸边的船，要渡湖送我们到皇太后与宫廷所驻的殿宇。这些船乃是游船式的，中央是一间封闭的船舱，四围一圈走道，船夫便在走道上前后走动着撑船。舱内铺着地毯，有一张带软垫的卧榻、几张茶桌和椅子。太监和随从们则坐在外头船头上。乘这样的船渡湖须二十分钟，行来却十分惬意。到了对岸，我们登岸，穿过几道庭院，来到皇太后一处私人佛堂的院中。她方才在此上过香，正待出来，前有执香炉的侍僧开道，乐工奏着「国歌」。皇太后一瞧见我们，便把我们叫到身边，问我进北京一路可好，又问康格夫人可安好。随后便吩咐太监领我们到下榻之处。我们被引着穿过一道道回廊与庭院，来到一座精致的阁子，这是我们在西苑这几日的歇息之所。阁内有雕工繁复精美的木拱，悬以厚重的缎帘，将它隔成五间屋子。我们在此歇了片刻，便回到宝座殿。皇太后告诉我，她已吩咐下一只船送我出游，凡是我想看的，都要领我去看——至少一日之内所能看的，都看得着。她身边侍立的一个太监，手里拿着数条绣花之物。那是几顶绣花的佛冠，宫中庙宇举行大典礼之时，便戴在诸佛头上。她将它们的用处说与我听，便吩咐我们退下，我们便出到湖边的登岸之处。

===Original===
A number of boats lay at the foot of the steps﻿—among them a charming open barge with blue silken awnings. As I had not been in a boat of this kind before, and as I was told to choose, I selected it for our row; and we started off, followed by several other boats carrying eunuchs and refreshments, with the necessary utensils for serving them. Our head eunuch, one of the six highest in the Palace, who had been appointed to look after me and the “sacred picture,” was very intelligent, an enlightened lover of Chinese art, and a great collector of old Chinese paintings and curios. He had been, in his youth, one of Her Majesty’s favorite players, was said to have great dramatic talent, and, when he was younger, had a fine voice for singing. Memory is among the most esteemed of the intellectual faculties by the Chinese and reaches a high state of cultivation with them. Many of the eunuchs can repeat whole pages from the classics, and some are accomplished literati. This eunuch had a good speaking voice, and recited poems and told stories in a charming way. As we were rowed along, he stood at the prow and recited verse after verse of classic lore and told stories of the heroic times. He intoned them like a recitative﻿—the rhythm so perfectly observed, the intoning so musical, it was a pleasure to listen to him, though I could not understand.

I lay back among the cushions, as we glided softly along, past beautiful pavilions, with splendid trees overhanging the lake and lovely flowers growing wherever there was a place to plant them. The tall figure of the splendidly attired eunuch, standing in the prow, repeating, with rhythmic cadence, poems and stories, gave one the illusion and charm of the *Arabian Nights*, which I had fed upon in my childhood, and which I seemed to be living through today.

===Chinese===
石阶脚下泊着好几只船——其中一只张着蓝色绸篷、玲珑可爱的敞口游舫，格外引人。我既未坐过这种船，又蒙允我自己挑，便选中了它代步。我们就此出发，后面跟着几只船，载着太监和点心，以及伺候茶点的诸般器具。我们的首领太监，乃是宫中品秩最高的六位太监之一，奉派照应我和那张「圣容」。他极为聪敏，是一位有见识的中国艺术爱好者，也是一位收藏中国古代书画古董的大藏家。他年轻时曾是皇太后最得宠的戏子，据说极有戏剧天分，年轻的时候还生得一副好嗓子。记忆力是中国人最看重的才能之一，造诣也极高。太监之中有许多人能将经史整页整页地背诵下来，还有些竟是有学问的文人。这位太监有一副好嗓子，诵诗说书，娓娓动听。船一路划去，他便立在船头，一阕一阕地吟诵古籍诗文，讲述前朝的英雄故事。他吟诵起来如同宣叙调一般——节奏把握得恰到好处，吟声又那般悦耳，虽我一句不懂，听着也是一桩乐事。

我倚在软垫之间，任由小船悄然滑行，掠过一座座精美的亭阁，古木参天，荫覆湖面，但凡有可植花之处，皆开着可爱的花朵。那位服饰华美的太监高大的身影，立在船头，以合于节奏的抑扬诵着诗、说着故事——这一番情景，正是我童年时所沉迷的那部《天方夜谭》（Arabian Nights）的意境与魅力，仿佛我今日竟亲身走入了书中。

===Original===
We soon came to a tiny islet in the lake, with a sort of open temple built over a black marble tablet which bore an incised inscription. I asked to land and examine it, and San-Gunia, the eldest of Lady Yu-Keng’s daughters, a remarkably clever girl and well posted in Chinese literature, translated the characters. The inscription was a poem, a tribute to the Great Father who had graciously placed there this island, which “by night was bathed in the glory of the Moon and Sun-kissed by day, while the crystal waters of the lake formed a brilliant necklace on its breast.”

Beyond the island I saw a temple. There was no landing-place, and the temple was under repair. The head eunuch, however, seeing how much I wished to go up, had the boat draw near and steps brought, up which we clambered, as best we could.

This was one of the temples so ruthlessly destroyed and unnecessarily desecrated by the Allies during their occupation of Peking. We passed through the vegetable garden of the monks﻿—all shorn of its glory, but where a few vegetables and flowers still grew﻿—and we went on through a beautiful grove of arborvitae, with centuries-old trees, planted in the form of a cross, and came into the court of the temple. Even in its dilapidated state, with the workmen still in it, it was beautiful, and before it was so injured it must have been a splendid example of Chinese temple architecture. The cells of the lama monks were now unoccupied, and there were no officiating priests. Workmen were repairing and regilding the Great Buddha, and most of the effigies of the saints and images of the personified attributes were standing in dejected rows in the corridors awaiting the completion of their niches and chapels. The interior, of splendid proportions, glowed in beautiful somber colors. The carved wood ceilings were in pendentive designs, recalling those I had seen in the Alhambra; but the painting, in primary colors, of this elaborately carved ceiling gave it a greater richness of coloring and lent to the interior a warmer, deeper harmony than the white Moorish designs. The chapels behind the high altar were separated from the main temple and from each other by beautifully carved wooden screens, with rich brocaded silk of brilliant green (the color of Buddha), stretched behind the openwork and showing through the interstices of the carving. These chapels are for the Sacred writings and for the vestments of the priests, and are also used for robing- and retiring-rooms for the officiants. They correspond to the sacristies of the Catholic Churches in Europe.

===Chinese===
不久我们便到了湖中一处小小的洲渚，上有一座敞口的小庙，覆于一方黑色大理石碑之上，碑上刻着铭文。我请人拢了岸，上前细看，裕庚夫人那位长女三姑娘（San-Gunia）——一个极聪慧、于中国典籍极有根柢的姑娘——将碑文译了出来。铭文是一首诗，颂扬那位将此岛赐于此地的「大行皇考」，说此岛「夜里沐浴着月华的清辉，白日承接着艳阳的亲吻，而湖中澄澈的水波，恰似它胸前一条璀璨的项铉」。

过了小岛，我望见一座庙。彼处无登岸之所，庙也在修缮之中。然首领太监见我如此心向往之，便叫船拢近，取来梯凳，我们便竭力攀了上去。

这是联军占据北京之时，被他们残忍毁坏、且无谓亵渎的庙宇之一。我们穿过僧人的一片菜园——如今已全无当年的气象，但还零零落落地长着几畦菜蔬、几丛花——又走进了一片极美的扁柏林，那些古树已有数百年之久，栽种成十字形，由此便进入了庙宇的院落。即便如今残破不堪、还有工匠在内，它依然极美，未受损毁之前，想必是中国庙宇建筑的一个绝佳典范。喇嘛僧人的禅房如今空无一人，也不见主祭的僧人。工匠们正在修整大佛、为之重贴金箔，大多数圣徒的造像与那拟人化的德性之像，都颓然地成排立在廊下，等候着各自的神龛和佛堂完工。殿内格局宏伟，色调解严深沉，自有一种光彩。木雕的天花板作穹顶式样，叫我想起我在阿尔罕布拉宫（Alhambra）所见的那一种；只是这雕工繁复的天花板以原色髹漆，色泽上更见丰艳，使殿内比那白色的摩尔式图样多了一层更为温暖、更为深沉的和谐。高坛后面的几间小堂，由雕工精美的木制隔扇与大殿、彼此之间分隔开来，隔扇的透雕之后绷着鲜艳的绿色（佛之色）锦缎，从雕花缝隙间隐约透出来。这些小堂是供奉经卷与收贮僧衣之所，也兼作主祭者更衣、退歇之用，正相当于欧洲天主教堂的圣器室。

===Original===
The space behind the altar was of apse-like form, and opened upon a semicircular marble terrace, thirty feet high, with a balustrade of the conventionalized lotus design so dear to the Chinese architects. From this terrace we had a beautiful view of the Coal Hill, surmounted by the curious Dagoba, so well known in all views of the Imperial City, as well as of the belvedere that marks the spot where the last Emperor of the Mings committed suicide when he was conquered. At the two extremities of the terrace were charming octagonal summerhouses, where the priests could go for rest and contemplation, and, while murmuring their prayers, could feast their eyes upon a charming view. After a few moments on the terrace, enjoying the beautiful view, we passed through the cells of the monks, which were large and comfortable, and, finally, out again into the sun-flecked shade of the marble-paved court, where we sat under low-hanging boughs of a splendid elm, and the eunuchs brought out tables and served us with tea and refreshments.

Then we took the boats and were rowed on further, till we came beneath a steep battlemented wall, surmounted by the rich green of arborvitae trees. I was surprised to learn that this was another temple, for it looked more like an old feudal castle than a peaceful temple to the mild Buddha. We landed at the foot of the beautiful white Marble Bridge that spans the narrow northern portion of the lake, just under the stone wall on which the temple was built. We were carried up the steep, winding incline in our chairs. It was a most picturesque approach, and when we reached the top, with the beautiful temple lying peacefully on these martial heights, we found it well worth the climb.

===Chinese===
祭坛之后的那一方空间呈半圆形龛室之状，开向一座高三十英尺的半圆形大理石露台，台上的栏板雕着程式化的莲花纹——中国营造家向来最喜此式。从这座露台望去，可以望见景山（Coal Hill），山顶上覆着那座奇特的覆钵塔（Dagoba），这是皇城全景中极为有名的一景；还可以望见一座望楼，正是明代末帝在国破之日自缢之处。露台两端各有亭亭一座玲珑的八角凉亭，僧人可入内歇息、静修，一边默诵经文，一边饱览悦目之景。在露台上盘桓片刻、赏过美景之后，我们穿过那些宽大舒适的僧房，终于又回到那铺着大理石的庭院里——日光透过树影，斑驳洒在荫凉之中——我们便在一株硕大榆树低垂的枝叶下坐定，太监们搬出桌子，奉上茶点。

随后我们复又登舟，继续前行，直至来到一堵陡峭的雉堞城墙之下，城墙上覆着扁柏那一片浓郁的翠色。我听说这也是一座庙宇，不禁称奇——因它看上去更像一座古老的封建城堡，而不像慈祥佛陀的一座清静庙宇。我们在那座美丽的汉白玉石桥脚下登了岸——这座桥横跨湖面北端狭窄之处，正在那座庙宇所依的石墙之下。我们坐在轿子里，被人沿着那陡峭蜿蜒的坡道抬了上去。这一路上山的路径画意十足，待到了顶上，只见那座秀美的庙宇安然卧在这几处居高临下的武备之地，我们顿觉不虚此攀。

===Original===
There was a grove of arborvitae trees leading to this temple. These trees seem to be sacred to the temples and burial-places in China, for all I ever visited in China were either built in a grove of arborvitae, or had some of these evergreens growing near. Did the Greeks get their idea and name of the ever-living tree from the Chinese, who regard the arborvitae as the tree of life and emblem of Immortality? This temple has a great Buddha of white jade, with jeweled stole and cuffs. Its expression of placid contemplation and kindly thought is typically Chinese. When Buddhism was first brought into China from India, the Buddhas had an Indian type; and not until the religion had taken firm hold of the people, was its divinity clothed in a Chinese personality, and a national individuality assumed. The day of our visit, the great jade Buddha was decked in a mantle of Imperial yellow satin, with a richly embroidered Manchu hood, such as I had seen that morning in Her Majesty’s Throne-room, on its head. Tall, lighted candles, fresh offerings of fruit and flowers, and the smoking censer standing on the altar, showed there had been services that morning, and added to the religious atmosphere of the interior. The service had been a continuation of the commemorative celebrations in honor of the Emperor’s Birthday and his sacrifice to his Ancestors.

The principal court of this temple is one of the most picturesque in the Sea Palace, shaded by magnificent cedars and stately elms. In the center, there was a magnificent cistern of verd-antique, splendidly carved in dragons. Over this cistern was a marble portico, its columns supporting a curious concave, copper roof. This roof had been a Palace cooking utensil, that had been used in former times to prepare food for the poor; hence its extraordinary size. When it was worn out in this capacity it was used as the interior of the dome over the temple well, where the poor and weary could come to rest under its shadow and drink of the water of the well it protected. There were cells and outhouses for the monks in this temple also. But as we sat in the shady court, looking across the sunlit lake, the sky became suddenly overcast, and we took our chairs and hurried down the steep, paved road that led from the temple to the lake. We did not take the boats again on reaching the lake, but were carried, in our chairs, across the beautiful Marble Bridge. Just beyond us, we saw the towers of the first Catholic Cathedral ever built in Peking. It was built on land given to the Catholics by the Emperor; but, when finished, its towers were found to overlook the Palace grounds; so the Cathedral was bought by the Emperor, and land was given the Mission further on, and another Cathedral was built. The first Cathedral is now within the Walls of the Sea Palace, and is visible from every part of the grounds of the two Peking Palaces. It seems a strange anomaly to see this Christian Church within the Precincts of the Palace of an Oriental potentate, who is one of the representatives on earth of the “Great Buddha.”

It began to rain, and the chair-bearers ran along to the Palace without stopping again, and we were soon called to dinner in the Throne-room, overlooking the small Theater, for there are two Theaters in the Sea Palace, one for winter use and one for summer. The latter is built on piles over the waters of a canal. Building the stage over water is supposed to give a peculiar musical resonance to the voices of the actors, softening the sounds and making them more pleasant to the ear.

===Chinese===
通往这座庙宇的路上，有一片扁柏林。在中国，这种树似乎与庙宇和茔地皆有渊源，我所到过的中国庙宇，无一不是建在扁柏林中，便是旁近也总有几株此树。希腊人那「常青之树」的意象与名称，莫非正是得自中国人？中国人视扁柏为生命之树、不朽的象征。这座庙里有一尊白玉大佛，披着缀满珠宝的袈裟与袖端。它那安宁沉思、慈悲含和的神情，是中国型的。佛教初从印度传入中国之时，诸佛仍是印度的样貌；直到这一教派在百姓中根基既固，那神祇才被赋予了中国的人格，也才有了本国的气象。我们游览之日，那尊大玉佛身披一袭明黄缎的法袍，头戴一顶绣工繁富的满洲风兜——正是我那日上午在皇太后宝座殿中所见的那种。坛上立着高高的、燃着的蜡烛，新鲜的果品与鲜花供在那里，香炉里香烟袅袅，可见那日清晨曾举行过法事，也给殿内增添了几分宗教的氛围。那场法事，乃是为皇上万寿以及他祭告列祖列宗所行诸般庆典的延续。

此庙正中的庭院，是西苑数处庭院中画意最足的一个，浓荫匝地，蔽以参天的雪松与挺拔的榆树。院正中有一只华丽的古绿石（verd-antique）大水缸，缸上雕龙，极为精美。水缸之上覆着一座大理石的小亭，亭柱撑着一顶奇特的凹形铜顶。这铜顶本是宫中一件炊具，昔日宫中施饭济贫之时所用，故而才有这般大得惊人的尺寸。等到它在济贫之用上磨耗殆尽，便被移作庙中那口井之上的井亭之顶，穷苦疲惫之人可来此在其荫蔽之下歇息，饮一口它所守护的那井中之水。这座庙里自然也有僧人禅房及偏屋。然正当我们坐在荫凉的庭院里，望着阳光照耀下的湖面，天色忽然阴了下来，我们便上了轿，急忙顺着那条从庙到湖边的陡峭石砌路赶下去。到了湖边我们未再登舟，却由轿子抬着，从那座美丽的汉白玉石桥上走过。只见离我们不远处，便是北京有史以来第一座天主教堂的塔楼。那是皇上赐地给天主教士所建；然建成之后，发觉塔楼竟可俯瞰宫禁之地；于是皇上将此教堂买下，另在更远处再赐一块地给教士，又另建了一座。第一座教堂如今在西苑城墙之内，从北京两宫苑囿的各处皆可望见。在这东方君主——「大佛」在人世的代表之一——的宫禁之内，竟得见一座基督教堂，实在是一种奇异的反差。

天开始落起雨来，轿夫们一路不停，径直抬着我们奔回宫去。不多时我们便被召到宝座殿进晚膳——此殿下临一座小戏台，因西苑共有两座戏台，一座冬日所用，一座夏日所用。后者建在运河水面之上，以木桩撑起。戏台建在水上，据说能给伶人的嗓音添一种特殊的乐感共鸣，柔化其声，使之入耳更为悦耳。

===Original===
After dinner in the beautiful summer Throne-room, with the rippling waters just beneath the windows, we made our adieus, first to Her Majesty and then to the young Empress and Ladies, and went out to be again rowed over the beautiful lake to the outside gates. The sun was setting! The arches of the Marble Bridge had become a beautiful, deep violet hue, and spanned the waters of the lake, now a gleaming mass of liquid gold. The sky beyond shone through the masses of foliage with a golden glow, and the towers of the old Cathedral were strongly silhouetted against this brilliant background. The scene was an ideal one. A beautiful silence pervaded everything, made the more rhythmic and intense by the regular movement of the oars in the water. When we reached the other side of the lake we were conducted to our green chairs, which were waiting without the gate, and were swiftly carried back to the Legation.

===Chinese===
在这座临水的华美夏日宝座殿中用罢晚膳，窗外水波轻漾，我们便向皇太后告辞，又向皇后与众位命妇一一作别，出到殿外，复又被人摇着渡过那美丽的湖面，送到外门。夕阳西下！汉白玉石桥的桥洞已染上一层美丽的深紫，横跨在湖水之上——此时湖面已是一汪熠熠的流金。树影之外的穹苍，透出金红的霞光，旧日教堂的塔楼在这辉煌的背景之上，投下浓重的剪影。此景真乃一幅理想的画卷。一种美丽的静穆笼罩着万物，又被船桨在水中那一下一下规律的划动，衬得愈发有韵律、愈发深沉。到了湖的彼岸，我们被引到门外等候的绿轿之前，便即飞快地被抬回公使馆去了。

## II Personal Appearance of Her Majesty—A Chinese Repast—Boating / 第二章 皇太后的容貌——中式盛宴——泛舟

===Original===
I was eager to be off the next morning, to have the promised long sitting from Her Majesty. The sitting of the day before had but whetted my desire for further work on the portrait. When we arrived within the Precincts we met the Empress Dowager and the Emperor coming out of the Great Audience Hall from their joint Audience. When Her Majesty saw us she stopped, as did the whole train of her attendant Ladies and eunuchs. She called me up to her side, took my hand, and asked me how I had rested and "whether I felt ready for work." This question showed her penetration, for she had seen the day before, from my eagerness and the breathless haste with which I used every moment, that my work was my first object, and she smiled when she put the query. I walked along by her side from the Audience Hall to the Throne-room where I had begun the portrait of the day before. When we reached the Throne-room she was divested of her official vestments, took a cup of tea, and called one of her tiring-women to bring her the dress and ornaments worn the day before, and she prepared to sit for me the second time.

===Chinese===
第二天早晨我急切地想动身，好赶上皇太后答应过的那场长时间坐班。前一天的坐班只是更勾起了我继续绘制那幅画像的渴望。我们进入禁苑时，正遇见皇太后和皇上从大觐见殿里出来，他们是同在那里行觐见礼的。皇太后看见我们，便停了下来，随侍的那一长列命妇和太监也跟着止步。她把我唤到身边，拉住我的手，问我昨夜睡得可好，又问「是否觉得精神饱满，可以开工了」。这一问题足见她的眼光犀利——前一天她已从我那股热切劲儿、从我抓紧每一刻时屏息疾行的模样里看出，作画于我才是头等要事；她发问时嘴角含笑。我从觐见殿随她一路走到正殿，也就是我前一天开始绘像的地方。到了正殿，她卸下朝服，饮了一盏茶，又唤一名贴身侍女取来前一日所穿的衣饰，准备为我第二次坐班。

===Original===
At this second sitting I looked at the Empress Dowager critically. I feared that the agreeable impression I had formed, the day before, of herself and her personal appearance had probably been too hasty, the result of the unusual glamour in which I had begun the portrait; I thought perhaps the Oriental environment had dazzled me and prevented my seeing the Empress Dowager as she really was, and I looked forward to a disillusion. As she sat there, upon the throne, before she was quite ready for me to begin, before she had transfixed me with her penetrating glance, before she knew I was looking at her, I scanned her person and face with all the penetration I could bring to bear, and this is what I saw:

===Chinese===
在这第二次坐班时，我细细地打量起皇太后来。我生怕前一日对她、对她容貌所生的好感过于草率，不过是这幅画像开笔时那层异样的光晕所致；我想，许是这东方的氛围令我目眩，使我没能看清皇太后的真面目，我便盼着这一回能清醒过来。当她坐在宝座上，尚未完全就绪、尚未以那锐利的一眼将我镇住、也尚未察觉我在端详她之前，我已用尽目力所能有的犀利去审视她的身形与面容，所见的便是这样：

===Original===
A perfectly proportioned figure, with head well set upon her shoulders and a fine presence; really beautiful hands, daintily small and high-bred in shape; a symmetrical, well-formed head, with a good development above the rather large ears; jet-black hair, smoothly parted over a fine, broad brow; delicate, well-arched eyebrows; brilliant, black eyes, set perfectly straight in the head; a high nose, of the type the Chinese call "noble," broad between the eyes and on a line with the forehead; an upper lip of great firmness, a rather large but beautiful mouth with mobile, red lips, which, when parted over her firm white teeth, gave her smile a rare charm; a strong chin, but not of exaggerated firmness and with no marks of obstinacy.1 Had I not known she was nearing her sixty-ninth year, I should have thought her a well-preserved woman of forty. Being a widow, she used no cosmetics. Her face had the natural glow of health, and one could see that exquisite care and attention were bestowed upon everything concerning her toilet. Personal neatness and an excellent taste in the choice of becoming colors and ornaments enhanced this wonderfully youthful appearance, and a look of keen interest in her surroundings and remarkable intelligence crowned all these physical qualities and made an unusually attractive personality.

===Chinese===
身量十分匀称，头端正地承于双肩之上，气度雍容；那双手实在美丽，纤巧小巧，形状高贵；头颅周正合度，在两只略大的耳朵上方发育饱满；乌黑的头发，在光洁宽阔的前额上齐整地分开；眉黛纤秀，弧度优美；一双明媚的黑眼，端端正正地生于头中；鼻梁高挺，正是中国人所谓「贵相」的那种——两眼之间宽阔，与额头齐平；上唇极见坚毅，嘴略大却很美，双唇红润而灵动，启露那一口结实洁白的牙齿时，笑容别有一种难得的风韵；下颏有力，却并不刚硬得过火，也全无固执之相。1 若不是我事先知晓她已将近六十九岁，我定会当她是个保养得宜的四十岁的妇人。她既居孀，便不施脂粉。她脸上自有那康健的天然红润，可见凡关乎梳妆打扮之事，无不精心呵护、细意照拂。她仪态整洁，于服色饰物的拣选又趣味高雅，使这出奇的年轻神采愈发鲜明；而那对周围事物兴致盎然的眼神与非凡的聪慧，更为这一切形貌之美加了冠冕，成就了一个异常动人的人物。

===Original===
When I was so far in my study of her appearance, the Empress Dowager had finished speaking to her attendants, had settled herself to her satisfaction on the throne, and she turned to me and asked "what part of the portrait I was to work on." I had been told she would be much pleased if I would paint in the face. Thinking it was important to please her at the outset, instead of perfecting and advancing the drawing of the whole figure, as I should have done, I began on the face; first correcting the drawing as far as possible and then putting in a thin wash of color. During the sitting the Ladies, attendants, and eunuchs were coming and going; she took tea and conversed, but she seemed to understand that she must keep her head in the same position, and she would look over apologetically at me when she moved it. I did not wish her to be stiff, and preferred her moving a little to sitting like a statue. Her Majesty, like all Oriental ladies, smokes, and during the sitting the eunuchs or some of the Princesses brought her either the graceful water-pipe, of which she would take a few whiffs, or she would indulge in European cigarettes. She never allowed the latter to touch her lips, but used a long cigarette-holder. She was extremely graceful in her use of both the cigarette and water-pipe.

===Chinese===
我端详她的容貌正到这地步，皇太后已与随侍们说完了话，照自己的心意在宝座上坐妥，便转向我，问「这幅像今日要画哪一处」。曾有人告诉我，倘若我肯画那面孔，她必十分欢喜。我想着起手之时讨她欢心要紧，便没有按本分去完善、推进那通身的轮廓，而是先画起面孔来：先把轮廓尽可能地校正一番，而后薄薄地罩上一层颜色。坐班之时，命妇、侍从与太监来来去去；她饮茶闲谈，却似乎明白自己须把头摆在一个方位不动，偶一动弹，便向我投来歉然的一瞥。我并不愿她僵着，宁可她略动一动，也不愿她如一尊雕像般坐着。皇太后与一切东方女子一样，是吸烟的；坐班之际，太监或某几位格格便为她捧来那姿态优雅的水烟袋，她吸上几口，又或者享用几支西洋纸烟。那纸烟她从不让它沾到嘴唇，专用一只长长的烟嘴。无论纸烟还是水烟，她用起来都极是娴雅。

===Original===
After little more than an hour's work Her Majesty decided that enough had been done for the morning and that we both needed rest! She came over to look at the face, and it was easy to see that she liked it much better now that the color was being put in. She stood behind me, discussing it for some time, and said she wished it were possible for someone else to pose for the face, so that she might sit and watch it grow. She thought it very wonderful that on the flat canvas the relief of the face could be represented. She then turned to me and said she knew I must be tired both mentally and bodily, as I stand to my work, advised me to go to my pavilion, have lunch, and rest, and added that she would try to give me another sitting in the afternoon before we went out for some sort of promenade.

===Chinese===
才画了一个多钟头，皇太后便以为今早画得已够，我们俩都该歇歇了！她走过来端详那面孔，不难看出，自打上了颜色，她比先前中意得多。她立在我身后，品评了好一会儿，说只盼能换个人来替她摆这面孔，好让她坐着看那画一笔一笔地长成。她又道，在平平的画布上竟能把面孔的凹凸神情都画出来，实在奇妙得很。随后她转向我说，她知道我既劳神又劳力——我是站着作画的——劝我回自己的阁子去用午饭、歇一歇，又说午后我们出去散一回步之前，她要设法再让我坐一次班。

===Original===
I returned to my pavilion with the Ladies Yu-Keng, whom Her Majesty had appointed to keep me company for the meals in my own quarters. There was a young Manchu girl at Court whose father had been an attaché at Berlin, who spoke German and English; she, also, had been ordered by Her Majesty to take her meals with us, so that I might have pleasant company and be able to converse in my own language and have proper relaxation during my meals. Besides, I did not know enough Chinese to direct the servants or make my wants known, and these Ladies were Her Majesty's interpreters.

===Chinese===
我同裕庚家的两位命妇一同回到我的阁子，皇太后已派她们在我的住处陪我用饭。宫中有一位年轻的满族姑娘，她父亲曾在柏林做过随员，能说德国话和英国话；皇太后也吩咐她与我们同食，好让我席间有悦意的伴，能以本国语言交谈，进餐时也好正经地松快松快。再者，我中国话说得不够，支使下人、达意陈情都办不来，这几位命妇便是皇太后给我派的通译。

===Original===
The meals at the Palace were all of the most lavish description, twenty or thirty dishes being placed upon the table at the beginning of the meal, while macaroni, rice, and a few other things were served from a side table. The Chinese are passed masters in the culinary art, and the delicacies seen at good Chinese tables are fit for a repast of Lucullus. Sharks' fins, deers' sinews, birds' tongues, rare fish, bird's-nest soups, fish brains, shrimps' eggs, and many other extraordinary dishes make up the everyday menu. No one can cook goose, duck, and in fact all fowls and game, to such perfection as the Chinese. Their soups are of a delicacy and flavor quite unequaled. Their breads and cakes seem to the foreigner, at first, the least delectable of their viands; their bread particularly, which is steamed instead of baked, is not tempting; but when you get over or rather through the raw-looking outside, with its five cochineal spots surmounting its pyramidal form, it is very sweet and wholesome. It is made of gray flour, as the Chinese do not believe in whitening the flour as we do. They make delicious creams, as to consistency; and these and their sweets generally are much esteemed by the foreigners.

===Chinese===
宫中的饭菜无一不是极尽丰盛那一类，开席之时，桌上便已摆好二三十样菜，面条、米饭及少数几样则从旁桌添来。中国人在烹饪一道上是炉火纯青的大师，像样的一席中国菜上所见的美馔，足堪比一场路库卢斯（Lucullus）的盛宴。鱼翅、鹿筋、雀舌、珍鱼、燕窝汤、鱼脑、虾子，还有许多别的不寻常的菜肴，便是日常的菜单。鹅、鸭，乃至一切家禽野味，谁也不能像中国人那样烹到这般完美的火候。他们做的汤，其鲜其味，实无匹敌。他们的面食糕点，在外人看来，起初是众馔中最不诱人的一类；尤其是他们的面包——是蒸出来的，不是烤出来的——并不招人；可一旦你克服了，或说是穿透了那看似生的一般的外皮，以及那金字塔模样顶上点缀着的五点胭脂红，便会发觉它又香又可口，且很养生。这是用灰面粉做的，因为中国人不像我们那样信奉把面粉漂白。他们做出的奶油（按其稠度而论）十分可口；这些以及他们各式甜食，都颇受外国人推重。

===Original===
At the Palace the food is served in tall dishes of painted Chinese porcelain, and everything is placed upon the table at once—soups, roast, sweets, all except the rice and macaroni. These latter dishes the Chinese eat boiling hot, and they are kept on chafing-dishes until served. Each person has a bowl, a small saucer, and a pair of chopsticks. A small square of very soft cloth is used as a napkin. There is never any salt upon the table. The small saucer at the side of each guest contains a very salty sauce; if extra salt is needed, this sauce is used. The Chinese consider powdered salt too coarse for seasoning food after it is cooked!

===Chinese===
宫中盛菜用的是高脚的彩绘中国瓷器，所有菜肴都一齐上桌——汤、烧烤、甜食，尽在其中，唯独米饭和面条不在其列。后两样中国人是要滚烫地吃的，未上桌前都搁在火锅上温着。每人一只碗、一只小碟、一双筷子。一方极软的小布巾充作餐巾。桌上从不见盐。每位客人的手边那只小碟里盛着一种很咸的酱汁；若要再加咸味，便用这酱汁。中国人以为，盐碾成粉之后再用来给做好的菜调味，未免太粗了！

===Original===
They rarely drink at meals, and when they do, only tiny cups, about the size of a liqueur-glass, of heated wine. This is poured out of silver teapots, and is kept hot by being placed in receptacles containing boiling water. Their wines are more like liqueurs than ours; they are generally distilled with flowers and herbs and have a delightful bouquet. Some of these wines have most poetic names, such as "Dew from the Early Morning Rose," and "Drops from the Hands of Buddha." The Chinese never drink cold water, nor do they take tea at meals. For me, being a foreigner, champagne was always provided, as well as claret or Burgundy. The Chinese do not drink coffee. After leaving the table, they take tea without milk or sugar.

===Chinese===
他们用饭时难得饮酒，即便饮，也只用那顶小顶小的杯子，约莫一杯烈性甜酒的大小，盛的是温热的酒。这酒是从银茶壶里斟出来的，搁在盛着滚水的水罐里保温。他们的酒与其像我们的酒，倒不如说更像甜酒；通常是和着花草蒸馏而成，香气宜人。其中有些酒名极是雅致诗意，如「清晨玫瑰之露」、「佛手之滴」。中国人从不饮冷水，也并不在用饭时喝茶。因我是外人，香槟常为我备着，红葡萄酒或勃艮第酒也有。中国人不饮咖啡。离席之后，才饮茶，不加奶，也不加糖。

===Original===
The middle of the day is set aside for the siesta, and during the heat of the summer, everyone goes to her apartments for two hours after luncheon. As I found the Chinese bed-cushions too hard to rest well upon, I took to my pavilion a foreign, eiderdown cushion, which I used for several days, until one day, on going to my room, I found two lovely new cushions with pale-blue silk, removable slips. On touching them, I found them to be soft and deliciously cool and fragrant as well. They were made of tea-leaves and had been sent as a present from the Empress Dowager. I found them a great improvement over eiderdown or feather cushions, especially for summer use. Though I did not care for this long midday rest, I was forced to go to my room and remain there, as there was nothing else to do.

===Chinese===
正午时分是留给午睡的；盛夏暑热之时，午饭后人人都要回自己房中歇上两个钟头。我嫌中国式的床枕太硬，睡不舒坦，便带回阁子一只洋式的鸭绒枕，用了好几天；直到有一天，我回到房里，看见两只崭新的漂亮枕头，外面套着淡蓝色绸子的活络枕套。一摸，又软又凉，沁人心脾，还带着香气。原来是茶叶做的，是皇太后差人送来的礼物。我觉着它比鸭绒枕、羽绒枕都好得多，夏天用着尤为相宜。我虽不喜欢这长长的午间歇息，却也只得回房待着，因为别无他事可做。

===Original===
When Her Majesty awakes, the news flashes like an electric spark through all the Precincts and over the whole enclosure, and everyone is on the qui vive in a moment. The young Empress and the Princesses go up to Her Majesty's Throne-room to be present at her *lever*. When her afternoon toilet is made, the Empress Dowager comes out of her private apartments into the Throne-room and generally partakes of some light refreshment, or drinks a cup of tea or some fruit juice.

===Chinese===
皇太后一醒，这消息便如电火一般掠过整座禁苑、遍传整个苑囿，顷刻之间人人屏息肃立、整装待命。皇后和格格们都上皇太后的正殿去，侍候她起身。2 待午后梳妆既毕，皇太后便从内殿出来，到正殿里，照例用些茶点，或饮一盏茶、一些果子汁。

===Original===
She gave me a short sitting after her nap this second day and then ordered the boats for a row on the lakes. Attended by the young Empress and Princesses, and with the usual train of attendants and eunuchs, we went out into the court of the Throne-room, passed through a small pavilion opening directly upon the beautiful white marble terrace, with its quaintly carved marble balustrade, which stretches all along the southern side of the lake. Her Majesty's own barge lay at the foot of the marble steps and numbers of other barges and boats lay around, forming quite a little fleet. She descended the steps and entered the barge. The young Empress, Princesses, and Ladies followed. Her Majesty sat in the yellow, throne-like chair in the middle of the raised platform of the barge. The young Empress, Princesses, and Ladies took their places as decreed by centuries-old tradition. They sat upon cushions placed upon the carpeted floor of the raised platform of the barge.

===Chinese===
这一日是她午后小憩之后，她又给我坐了一小会儿班，便传下旨意，备船游湖。由皇后和格格们随侍，照例又带着那一班随从与太监，我们出到正殿前的庭院，穿过一座小阁——那阁正开向一道美丽的汉白玉露台，带着雕琢古拙的汉白玉栏干，沿着湖的南岸一路逶迤而去。皇太后自己的御舟泊在汉白玉阶下，周围还停着好些只大船小船，俨然一支小船队。她拾级而下，登舟。皇后、格格与命妇们随后跟上。皇太后端坐在舟中高台正中那张黄色的、宝座似的椅子上。皇后、格格与命妇们各按数百年相传的规矩归了位。她们都坐在高台上铺着地毯的舟板上，垫着坐褥。

The Empress and Ladies of the Court in the Imperial Barge / 皇后与宫廷命妇在御舟之中

On the Lake of the Summer Palace / 于颐和园湖上

===Original===
When I stepped on, Her Majesty motioned me to come near her and sit at her right. The young Empress was on her left. Several of the high eunuchs stood at the back of the Empress Dowager's chair with her extra wraps, bonbons, cigarettes, water-pipes, etc. There were two rowers on the barge who stood with their long oars to guide it, for it was attached by great yellow ropes to two boats, manned by twenty-four rowers each, and was towed along by them. Only the eunuchs of the highest rank, Her Majesty's personal attendants, went on the barge with her, and the two boatmen simply guided it. All the Palace boatmen stand to their oars, for they cannot sit in the presence of Her Majesty, even though not upon the Imperial barge. And it is only on the barge that the Empress and Ladies sit in the presence of the Empress Dowager without being invited by her to do so.

===Chinese===
我踏上舟时，皇太后示意我到她跟前去，坐在她右手。皇后坐在她左手。几位品级最高的太监立在皇太后座椅之后，捧着她备用的外衣、糖果、纸烟、水烟袋等物。御舟上有两名舟子，手执长橹，站着掌舵；原来这舟用粗大的黄绳拴在两只船上，每只船各有二十四名舟子，由他们一路牵拽而行。能随皇太后上这御舟的，唯有那几位最高品级的太监——都是她贴身的侍从——舟上那两名舟子不过是掌舵而已。宫里所有的舟子都是站着摇橹的，因为他们在皇太后面前不得就座，纵使并不在御舟之上。也唯有在这御舟之上，皇后与众命妇才可不待皇太后赐坐，便在她面前坐下。

===Original===
A number of flat boats followed the Imperial barge with the army of eunuchs that go to make up the train of Their Majesties when they move about the Palace or grounds. One boat carried portable stoves and all the necessary arrangements for making tea, as this is taken so frequently by Her Majesty and the Ladies, it may be called for at any time.

===Chinese===
还有好些只平底的船跟在御舟之后，载着那一众太监——两宫在宫中或苑囿里行走时，这一大队太监便是随行的扈从。其中一只船上载着活动炉灶和煮茶的一切应用之物，因皇太后与众命妇时时饮茶，茶水随时都传得到。

===Original===
We were rowed across the lake to one of the islands; and when we looked back at the Palaces, the memorial arches, the temple-crowned hills, the curious camel-back bridges, and the beautiful white marble terraces jutting out into the lake with its islands, the scene was indeed fairy-like. We were then rowed into a field of beautiful lotus flowers, and Her Majesty ordered some pulled by the eunuchs to be given to the Ladies. She seemed delighted at my sincere admiration of this beautiful water-plant, so dear to the Chinese. After an hour on the lake, we were rowed back to our starting-point and disembarked. This time the Princesses and Ladies left the barge first and stood to receive the Empress Dowager when she landed. When she had dined she asked us to dine with the young Empress and Ladies at her table in her Throne-room, after which we made our adieus and returned to our own Palace, without the Precincts.

===Chinese===
我们被摆渡过湖，到其中一座岛上；待我们回首望去——那一片宫殿、牌楼、庙宇点缀其上的山峦、形制奇古的驼背桥，还有那伸入湖中、与湖中诸岛相接的美丽汉白玉露台——这景致真如仙境一般。随后我们又被摇入了一片盛开的荷花丛中，皇太后吩咐太监摘下几朵，分给众命妇。我由衷地赞美这中国人所深爱的美丽水生花卉，皇太后似乎颇为欢喜。在湖上行了一个钟头，我们又被摇回原处，弃舟登岸。这一回，格格与命妇们先下御舟，立在一旁，候皇太后登岸时接她。她进过晚膳之后，便请我们与她同席，在正殿里她的桌上与皇后、命妇们一同进膳；饭后我们辞出，回到苑外的自己的宫里去。

## XXVII Present-Giving in China / 二十七 中国的馈赠之风

===Original===
Present-giving is really carried to great excess all over China, and whatever obtains in China obtains at the Palace. The Palace is spoken of in Peking as the “Inside,” that is, the heart of the Empire. From this “Inside,” customs and habits flow and pulse over the rest of China, as the blood does from the heart, by a thousand arteries reaching to the very confines of the Empire, and it also receives the impress of what passes on outside among the people. Whether it be, in the instance of present-giving, that the custom has grown from the “Inside” to the “Outside” or vice versa, I know not, but it is universal in China. However, it probably reaches its greatest excess in the Palace.

===Chinese===
馈赠之风，在中国确是盛行到近乎泛滥的地步；凡是中国所通行的，宫中亦无不通行。在北京（Peking），人们把宫廷称为「里头」，意即帝国的核心。种种礼俗习尚，便由这「里头」流出，如血液之自心脏流出一般，沿着千百条脉络，一路流注、搏动，直达帝国的极边；而外间民间所行之事，亦同样在这「里头」留下印记。至于馈赠一节，究竟是此风由「里头」蔓延至「外头」，还是由「外头」传入「里头」，我不得而知；但在中国，它确是无处不在。不过，此风最盛之处，恐怕仍要数宫廷。

===Original===
Births, marriages, and deaths are all marked by presents, and there is a very riot of present-giving at the New Year! Everyone then exchanges them, from the lowest to the highest. Next comes the anniversary of the birth. This is celebrated with an unheard-of pomp in China. The more exalted the rank and the greater the age, the more splendid is the celebration and the more magnificent the presents. The Emperor’s Birthday was the first I saw celebrated, and I was astonished at the number and elegance of the presents that flowed into the Palace on this occasion. But at the Empress Dowager’s Birthday all this was far surpassed; and her presents exceeded in number and elegance His Majesty’s, for she was celebrating more years than the Emperor, and the number and value of birthday presents increase in proportion to the years. Their elegance and number are also regulated by the rank. The presents the Grand Secretary and the Prime Minister receive on such occasions would quite astonish a Westerner, and, of course, far surpass in number and magnificence what would be offered to the president of a department, as his would exceed, in elegance, those offered to secretaries of the Board.

===Chinese===
降生、婚嫁、丧葬，无不以礼物为记；而过年之时，馈赠之风更是闹腾得沸反盈天！届时人人相互馈赠，自最微贱者直至最尊贵者，无一例外。其次便是生辰之庆。在中国，生辰的庆贺排场之大，简直前所未闻。地位越高、年岁越长，庆典便越铺张，所受的礼物也越豪奢。皇上（the Emperor）的万寿节，是我头一回见到的庆典；那日宫廷中所收进的礼物，其数量之多、品物之雅，已令我惊叹不已。然而待到皇太后（Her Majesty the Empress Dowager）的万寿节，这一切又远过之；太后所受的礼物，无论数量还是雅致，都超过了皇上——一则太后所贺的年岁多于皇上，而寿礼之数与值，本就随年岁而增；再则礼物之精与多，亦因品级而有别。此等场合，大学士与首相所收的礼物，足以叫一个西洋人惊骇；自然，其数量与豪奢，又远非各部尚书所得者可比——正如尚书所得者，其精美亦胜于各部侍郎所得一般。

===Original===
Every festival, every ceremony, and all anniversaries are marked by presents, in the Palace. There is scarcely a day that presents are not sent into the Palace, that some are not sent out, and rarely a day when some presents are not exchanged by those “Inside.” The Empress Dowager and the Emperor receive the greatest number, and, of course, they give the greatest number. This seemed to me the greatest extravagance of the Empress Dowager. At every change of season, she presents the young Empress, the Princesses, and Ladies, without reference to her favorites, with silks, dresses, shoes, and ornaments appropriate to the season, and not only do the Ladies receive these articles of wearing apparel at the changes of the season: she gives them many presents at each festival. Besides this, she gives nearly all the expensive Court dresses that are worn at the Palace, which cost, with their embroideries, from three to six hundred dollars each. She presents the Ladies with coiffures and many jeweled ornaments as well. On the occasion of a wedding among the Manchu nobility, which must be announced to the Court before it takes place, Her Majesty presents the bride handsome rolls of silk, embroideries, and jewels. On the occasion of births among the courtiers, she sends handsome ornaments to be worn by the newborn child. Even on the death of certain people, she sends handsome presents to the family, or something to be worn by the departed, if it should be a widow, who had led a long and exemplary life of widowhood, and had devoted herself to charity and good works.

===Chinese===
宫廷之中，每逢节令、典礼、周年忌辰，无不以礼物为记。几乎日日都有礼物送进宫来，也日日有礼物从宫中送出，至于「里头」的人彼此之间交换礼物，更是极少有一日间断。皇太后与皇上所得最多，自然，所赠出的也最多。依我看来，这便是皇太后最为挥霍之处。每逢换季，她便按节令赏赐皇后（the Young Empress）、公主与女官们绸缎、衣裳、鞋履与首饰，并不独厚于自己所钟爱的人；而这些女眷于换季之时所得的，也远不止四季衣饰：每逢节令，她都另有厚赏。此外，宫廷里穿的那些华贵的礼服，也几乎全由她一手赏下——这些礼服，连同其上绣工，每件所值，自三百元至六百元不等。她也赏赐女官们首饰与种种珠宝头面。满族（Manchu）贵族嫁女，须于成婚前先报知宫廷，届时皇太后必赏新娘上好的整匹绸缎、绣品与珠宝。宫中人有得子之喜，她便遣人送去新生儿可佩戴的精美饰物。便是在某些人故世之时，她也要送一份厚礼给其家中——若是那亡者乃一位孀居多年、守节尽礼、一生行善积德的寡妇，她还要赏些可由逝者带去的饰物。

===Original===
When the ladies of the Legation were first received at the Palace, the Empress Dowager naturally followed the Chinese Imperial custom of giving each lady a present. This precedent having been established and seeming to have given pleasure, when the ladies were received the next time, which was after the Boxer rebellion, she gave them presents again. Unfortunately, this act was construed into a desire on her part to wheedle the foreigners, and curry favor, so that she might receive better treatment at the hands of the Powers. The truth is, she loves to play the Lady Bountiful, and she never mixes up the social with the political, and I am sure she had no arrière-pensée but was simply indulging her usual bent. After the first few Audiences (when the presents were really of value), Her Majesty gave small and unimportant presents at the garden parties, which were made the subject of ridicule. Her Majesty had heard that the ladies did not wish to receive such handsome presents as she had first given, and she hence gave inexpensive souvenirs. Finally, the Ministers asked the Chinese Foreign Office to request the Empress Dowager to give no more presents at the Audiences, and the custom was abolished; though Her Majesty continued to give presents in private, and she still sends, on the four great Chinese festivals, flowers, fruits, and confectionery to all the ladies of the Legation, as well as to every lady who has ever been received at the garden parties, and on the departure of any Minister from Peking, she sends his wife some parting presents.

===Chinese===
公使馆的夫人（ladies of the Legation）们初次蒙召入宫觐见（Audience）之时，皇太后自是依中国朝廷的规矩，赏每位夫人一份礼物。此例一开，似乎又颇令诸位夫人欢欣；待到她们第二次蒙召——那是在拳匪之乱以后——太后便又各赏了一份。不幸的是，此举竟被外界曲解为太后有意巴结洋人、讨好列强，以便在列国手中得到更好的对待。其实呢，太后素来爱做那慷慨施与的女主人（Lady Bountiful），从不把社交之事与政治相混；我敢断言，她绝无别样心思（arrière-pensée），不过是顺着自己一向的脾性罢了。头几次觐见（那时所赠的礼物确是贵重之物）之后，皇太后在园会上所赐的，便只是些微不足道的小物，因而惹来了讥笑。原来太后听说，诸位夫人并不愿收受她初次所赠那样贵重的礼物，故而改赠些不值几文的小纪念品。末了，各国公使竟请中国外务部（Wai-Wu-Pu）转请皇太后，勿在觐见时再行赏赐，此例遂废；然而太后私下照旧馈赠不绝，仍于中国四大节令之时，遣人把鲜花、果品、糖果送与公使馆全体夫人，以及每一位曾蒙召赴园会的女眷；每逢哪位公使离任出京，她也要送其夫人一份程仪。

===Original===
But though present-giving has been stopped at the Audiences of the foreign ladies, it goes on with the same excess in the Palace and among officials in China. At each of Their Majesties’ Birthdays, in spite of their protests and edicts to prevent it, presents pour into the Palace! Every official who has ever been presented in Audience, or who has the right, by his official position, to send anything, does so. Edicts from the Throne to prevent it will remain as ineffectual as those with reference to the binding of the feet of the Chinese women (which Her Majesty has for years been “recommending” in edicts to be abolished), for it has become so thoroughly a part of Chinese life as to be almost indispensable. Present-giving in China is one of those “unwritten laws” whose tyranny is hardest to break away from. Though the system of present-giving is a great tax on the officials, as well as their subordinates, in this instance the change must come from the people.

===Chinese===
然而，外国夫人们觐见时的赏赐虽已停止，宫廷之内、中国官场之中，馈赠之风却照旧泛滥如故。每逢二位陛下（Their Majesties）的万寿节，任凭太后皇上再三申诫、屡降谕旨禁止，礼物仍源源涌进宫来！但凡曾经觐见过、或按其官位有资格进献之物者，无不照进不误。朝廷所下的禁令谕旨，其无效亦正与禁缠足之谕旨无异——缠足一事，太后多年来一直在谕旨中「劝谕」废除，然而终归徒然——盖因此风已深入中国人生活之中，几乎到了不可或缺的地步。中国的馈赠之风，正属那种「不成文法」之一种，其专横最难摆脱。虽说馈赠之习于官员及其属下都是一大重负，但这一风气之改易，仍须出自民间。

===Original===
As I was an inmate of the Palace for so long, of course I came in for my share of presents from the Empress Dowager. At every festival I was remembered, as well as the Princesses and Ladies of the Court, and when presents were sent to the ladies of the Legation, she sent similar ones to me. Many of the presents she made me showed a real consideration for my comfort and displayed much forethought. When the weather became cool, and the Ladies of the Court put on wadded dresses, Her Majesty sent one of her maids to my apartments to get one of my tailor-made dresses. She had the Palace tailors copy this in wadded silk. It was wonderful how well they did it, too, for, as I knew nothing about it, I could give no advice. She ordered a few changes made in the severity of the tailor-costume, thinking it was too hard in its lines. She had a long, soft sash to tie at the side, which, she decided, made it look more graceful. When the Princesses put on furs, Her Majesty, herself, designed for me a long fur-lined garment which she thought would be comfortable to paint in. She had some trouble in arriving at a result which pleased her, which would be warm enough, and which, at the same time, would not interfere with the freedom of movement necessary for me to work with ease. At the time of the Chinese New Year, she sent me two curiously fashioned fur-lined dresses. She had the skirts copied from old pictures. They were not unlike our pleated skirts, with an embroidered panel down the center of the front. The jackets were a sort of compromise between European and Chinese, and the costumes were not only pretty but very comfortable.7

The Author in Chinese Costume

===Chinese===
我既在宫中长住，自然也分得一份皇太后所赐的礼物。每逢节令，我都不曾被忘却，与宫廷的公主、女官们一体受赏；逢到公使馆的夫人们得了赏赐，她也照样送我一份。太后赏我的许多物件，都可见出她对我起居冷暖的真切体贴，煞费了一番苦心。天时转凉、宫廷女官们都换上夹衣之时，皇太后便遣一名宫女到我房中，取去我一件量身定做的洋式衣裙。她命宫中裁缝照式样用絮绸仿制了一件。他们仿得竟十分妥帖，实在可惊——因为我对此道一窍不通，竟无从指授半句。太后以为那洋装线条过于硬朗，便吩咐在上面略作改动：添一条长长的软腰带，系于一侧，她以为如此方显得婀娜。公主们穿上皮衣之时，皇太后更亲自为我设计了一件长身的皮袄，以为穿着作画必甚相宜。她费了好一番心思，才得一个令她满意的结果：既须暖和，又不能掣肘，免得妨碍我作画时所需的挥洒自如。到了中国人过年之时，她又赏我两件式样奇特的皮袄。那裙子是照着旧画仿制的，与我们西洋的褶裙颇为相仿，正中一道绣花裙门。上衣则介于欧式与中式之间，可谓中西参半；这套衣裙不仅好看，而且穿着甚是舒适。7

着中装的作者

===Original===
For wearing with these she ordered a sable hat, for the Chinese ladies wear some sort of coiffure on the head, winter and summer. This had an embroidered crown of pale lavender satin, with long satin streamers embroidered in gold with good-luck emblems. The brim could be worn either turned off the face or pulled over the ears and tied under the chin with lavender strings. She said she had some trouble in finding a design which she thought would suit me. This hat she had also had copied from old prints. I learned later she had tried three sorts of sables before she got a color which she thought would be becoming to my unfortunate blonde hair! On the front of the brim she placed a Princess Button. This is worn only by Ladies of the Court, and represents the Flaming Pearl of the Dynasty. It was established by the founders of the Dynasty and is the distinguishing jewel of the members of the Imperial family. It consists of a large pearl, surrounded by three alternating rows of seed-pearls and corals, which are supposed to represent flames! This Flaming Pearl, symbol of the “Unattainable,” is the eternal quest of the double dragon!

===Chinese===
为配这身衣裳，她又命人做了一顶紫貂皮帽——盖因中国女子无论冬夏，头上总要戴些什么的。帽顶是淡紫色的缎子，上面绣着花；两条长长的缎带自帽后垂下，上面用金线绣着种种吉祥图样。帽檐可翻起露面，也可拉下来遮住双耳，用淡紫色的带子在颌下系住。太后说，为寻一个她以为合我面目的式样，倒费了她一些功夫。这帽子也是从旧画谱里仿制来的。后来我才听说，为着我那一头不幸的金发，太后前后试过三种貂皮，才觅得一种她以为衬我的颜色！她在帽檐正中安了一颗「公主钮」（Princess Button）。此物唯宫廷女官方得佩戴，所表示的，乃是大清国的「火珠」（Flaming Pearl）。这是开国之初所定的规矩，是皇族宗亲所独有的辨识之宝。其制：以一颗大珠居中，外环三圈，米珠与珊瑚相间排列，据说是象征火焰！这火珠，乃「不可得之物」的象征，是双龙永恒的追逐！

===Original===
Her Majesty also presented me with a number of other charming things that I shall always treasure as coming from her, and as evidence of her consideration for “the stranger within her gates,” or as spontaneous offerings from her naturally generous nature—ever desirous of giving pleasure. I wish I might have preserved the flowers and curious grasses which she, herself, gathered and gave me on our many promenades around the beautiful grounds of the Summer Palace, but which, alas! are withered and gone!

===Chinese===
此外，皇太后还赏了我许多别的可爱之物，我皆将永远珍存——一来因为这是太后所赐，二来也因这是她善待那「寄身门下的异乡客」的一份心意，又或是她那生性慷慨、唯愿与人同乐的性情，自然而然生出的馈赠。只可惜那些她在颐和园（Summer Palace）秀美的园苑之中、与我们一次次散步之时，亲手采来赠我的花草，我竟不能一并留存——哀哉！它们都已枯萎消逝了！

## XXIX Religious Rites in China / 二十九 中国的宗教祭祀

===Original===
There are three great religions in China﻿—Buddhism, Taoism, and the worship of Nature. The worship of Nature, in which is embodied their highest idea of an Invisible Deity, is the purest form of religion in China. Its Temples are situated in a magnificent Park in the Chinese City of Peking. The Temple of Heaven, the most imposing of the group, spherical and triple-domed, rears its proud height here and is visible from afar. Its triple dome tiled, without, in the sacred green of Nature and vaulted within in Heaven’s own blue, is surrounded by groves of century-old arborvitae. In other parts of the great Park are the scarcely less splendid Temples to the Earth, to the Sun and Moon and to Agriculture, and grandest, most unique of these Temples, is that to the Invisible Deity. Its foundations are the Earth, its walls are limitless Ether, its dome Heaven’s own vault! On its great open altar this Nature worship has its culmination and reaches its highest fulfilment. This altar is the Holy of Holies, the tabernacle of the group of Temples consecrated to the worship of Nature.

===Chinese===
中国有三大宗教——佛教、道教，以及自然崇拜。自然崇拜寄托着中国人对无形之皇天上帝（the Invisible Deity）的最高观念，是中国最纯粹的宗教形式。其坛庙坐落于北京（Peking）外城一片宏伟的园苑之中。其中最为巍峨的天坛（Temple of Heaven）呈球形，三重圆顶，于园中昂然矗立，远远便可望见。那三重圆顶，外面覆以神圣的自然之绿的琉璃瓦，穹顶之内则是一片上苍本身的青蓝，四周环绕着百年古柏的苍翠林海。在这片大园苑的其他各处，还有几座几乎同样华美的坛庙，分祭地、日、月与先农；而其中最宏大、最为独特的，乃是那祭祀无形之皇天上帝的坛庙。它的根基是大地，它的四壁是无垠的太空，它的圆顶便是苍穹本身！在它那宏大的露天祭坛之上，这自然崇拜臻于极至，达到其最高的圆满。这座祭坛便是至圣所，是这一组奉献于自然崇拜的坛庙中的圣幕。

===Original===
It is built in the center of a great marble-paved space with the secular arborvitae radiating therefrom in long concentric avenues. It is of pure white marble, round as is the Earth. The Trinity in Nature and its Infinity are symbolized in its three superposed circles. Each of the circular platforms is surrounded by an exquisitely carved balustrade and approached by flights of nine steps each, to the north, south, east, and west. The central point of the great upper circle thus represents the center of the Universe, accessible from every point of the compass.

===Chinese===
它坐落在一大片以大理石铺就的广场正中，岁月悠久的古柏自此向四面八方作同心圆式辐射，化作一道道长长的林荫甬道。它由纯白大理石砌成，圆如大地。自然界的三一与其无垠，正象征于这三层相叠的圆台之上。每一层圆台四周都环以精雕细刻的汉白玉栏杆，南北东西四面各设一段九级台阶，拾级而上。于是乎，最上层大圆的中心便代表了宇宙之中心，自罗盘上任何一方皆可通达。

===Original===
Here in this symbolic center of the world, in this great Temple, whose walls are Space, whose towers are Infinity, on this great triple altar, canopied with Heaven itself, the Emperor of China, “Son of Heaven,” glorifies the Invisible Deity and sacrifices for the prosperity of “the Great, Pure Kingdom” and his people. This worship of the Invisible Deity has no Priestly Hierarchy. The Emperor of China is its one High Priest. He alone is worthy, as the Son of Heaven, to perform its unique ceremonies, on its one great Altar, in its single great Temple of China.

===Chinese===
就在这象征性的世界中心，在这座以太空为四壁、以无垠为塔楼的大庙之中，在这座以苍穹本身为华盖的三层大坛之上，中国皇上——「天子」——赞颂那无形的皇天上帝，并为「大清国」与天下子民的繁荣而献祭。这种对无形之上帝的崇拜，并无神职体系。中国皇上便是其唯一的大祭司。唯独他，身为天子，方配在天下唯一的大坛之上、中国唯一的大庙之中，行其独异的祭典。

===Original===
The Emperor prepares himself for the great ceremony of the semiannual celebration on this altar by a rigorous fast of three days, spending the final night before the celebration in a vigil in the Great Park of the Nature Temples, where there is a Purifying Palace set aside for his use. This glorification of the Invisible Deity at the summer and winter solstices is the most solemn act performed by the Emperor in his quality of Son of Heaven.

===Chinese===
为这坛上岁中两次的大祭之礼，皇上（the Emperor）须先严格斋戒三日；及至祭前最后一夜，便在自然诸坛的大园苑中守夜不寐——园中特设有一座斋宫（Purifying Palace），供他斋宿之用。这于夏至、冬至二日对无形之上帝所行的赞礼，乃是皇上以天子之身所行的最为庄严肃穆的大典。

===Original===
Temple of Heaven﻿—Peking

===Chinese===
天坛——北京

===Original===
The Emperor is not only the one High Priest worthy to sacrifice on the great altars to the Invisible Deity, the Priestly Hierarchy of the whole of this Cult of Nature is vested in his Sacred Person. He alone offers sacrifices in the Temples of Heaven and the other Great Temples, at times set aside in the Book of Rites, and on special occasions.
