Poor lady! Private sorrows and sad memories were not all she had to grieve her now. I had noticed her growing anxiety for many days! She seemed to feel all the gravity of the political situation of China. As the rumors of war between Russia and Japan grew, her anxiety increased and she was looking sad and careworn. She seemed to be full of doubt and fear, and quite unlike her usual self. I fancy she thought of the unprepared state of her country and feared that it might be drawn into this struggle. She seemed to be in doubt as to the course that was best to be taken. Even should the Empire not be drawn into the conflict, two hostile nations were to meet within its borders. The struggle was to take place in Manchuria, the cradle of the Dynasty. That beautiful, smiling country would be ravished by war, and the awful possibility of the ancestral tombs being desecrated, loomed up before her. The desecration of the tombs of one’s ancestors in China is supposed to bring dire consequences upon the family, and a pious Chinaman would face any material loss rather than run the risk of these tombs being desecrated. She felt it all, and was sad indeed.

===Chinese===
可怜的太后！她此刻所忧所伤的，远不止一己之悲与旧日之痛。许多日来，我已留意到她那与日俱增的忧色！她似乎深切地感到中国时局之严峻。日俄之间将开战的传言愈甚，她便愈发忧心忡忡，形容愁苦。她似满怀犹疑与恐惧，全不似素日的那副模样。我揣想她心中所念，乃是中国那未及筹备的国力，所惧者，是怕中国亦将被卷入这一场争斗。她似乎举棋不定，不知何去何从才是上策。即令帝国不致被卷入战火，两个敌对的国度终要在中国的疆土之内交锋。战事将在满洲（Manchuria）展开——那可是本朝龙兴之地。那一片秀美丰饶、面目和悦的河山，将遭兵燹蹂躏；而列祖列山陵或有被发掘亵渎的可怖之虞，更沉沉地横亘在她眼前。在中国，祖宗山陵一旦被人惊扰，便要给这一家子招来凶险的报应；一个虔敬的中国人，宁可承当任何财帛上的折损，也不肯冒祖宗陵寝遭毁之险。这一切她皆感同身受，心中之痛，实非言语可状。

## XXXII The Chinese New Year—Official Audience / 三十二 中国年——百官觐见

===Original===
The Chinese New Year, the greatest of the popular festivals, is, of course, celebrated with much pomp and enthusiasm at the Palace. Splendid decorations, hundreds of beautiful horn lanterns, with their long, red silk tassels, the great red "Sho" emblazoned on their sides, made the courts and verandahs gay with color. Painted figures of red-clothed gods regarded one at every turn. Hideous monsters with vermilion faces, painted on the outside doors, brandished spears to frighten away the bad spirits. There were the usual gala representations at the Theater; and the Palace, as at all festivals, was filled with visitors.

The Chinese pay all their debts at the New Year. If they have not the ready money to do so, they will dispose of anything valuable they have, in order to begin the New Year free from debt. It is considered tempting Heaven to begin it otherwise. A great deal of silver imitation money is exchanged at this season. This is an old custom and supposed to bring abundance during the year. At the New Year, present-giving reaches its culminating point in China. Everyone, rich and poor, high and low, gives presents then.

Their Majesties not only gave to all the Ladies and Princesses, but to every inmate of the Palace, and even the beggar at the gate was not forgotten; but the presents exchanged at the New Year are never so handsome as those given for a birthday. The presents the Empress Dowager received on this occasion were principally flowers (her Throne-room was full of them, as well as her private apartments)—dwarf fruit trees twisted into fantastic shapes, laden with fragrant blossoms and splendid plants of peonies in full flower, and countless vases of the Chinese Lily, as they call the Narcissus in China. The Empress Dowager tried to be cheerful and not dampen the gaiety of the Festival by her alarm, but the long-looked-for and much-dreaded war between Russia and Japan had then actually begun, and she was mortally anxious! The Japanese were already in Manchuria, and no one knew how it might affect China!

===Chinese===
中国年乃民间最盛大的节日，宫中庆贺起来，自然极尽铺张、热闹非常。华美的装饰，数以百计的精致角灯，缀着长长的红绸流苏，灯壁上大大的红「寿」字分外醒目，将庭院与游廊装点得花团锦簇。彩绘的红衣神像处处含笑迎人。外门上画着朱红脸庞的狰狞鬼怪，手执长矛，用以驱赶邪祟。戏台上照例有应节的吉庆戏文；宫中一如每逢佳节那般，宾客盈门。

中国人过年都要清偿一切债务。若手头没有现钱，便变卖一切值钱之物，务求清清爽爽地过年。若非如此，便算是冒犯上天。此时节还要兑换大量的银色纸钱。这是一桩旧俗，据说能保一年丰足。在中国，过年时的馈赠之风臻于极盛。无论贫富贵贱，人人都在这时送礼。

两位陛下不仅赏赐众位宫眷和福晋格格，连宫中上上下下人等都有份，便是门前的乞丐也不曾被遗漏；只是过年相赠的礼物，总不及贺寿时所送的那般贵重。皇太后此番收到的贺礼以花为多（她的宝座间里摆满了花，私宅之中亦然）——虬枝盘曲、形态古怪的矮种果树，缀满幽香的花朵，还有开得正盛的雍容牡丹，以及无数瓶中国水仙——他们在中国管水仙（Narcissus）就叫「水仙花」。皇太后强打精神，不愿以自己的忧惧扫了节日的兴致，然而俄日之间那场早有预料、令人忧惧的战争，其时竟当真打了起来，太后真是忧心如焚！日本人已开进满洲，谁也说不准这件事会怎样牵连中国！

### Slave Girls / 丫头

===Original===
Though I did not work on the portrait during the New Year's festivities, it was now really advancing. When Her Majesty saw how the hands looked when they were drawn in, with the palms of the hands hidden by the long fur undersleeves, in the position I had dared to find fault with at the first sitting, she at once suggested having the fur undersleeves taken off, but she still said nothing about changing the position of the hands, though I saw she had her doubts about them, and I felt confident her good taste would finally prevail and she would want them changed. I painted them in with a thin wash of color, knowing they would be changed later. A few days after this, she remarked that my "idea about the position of the hands was not bad," and suggested that the left hand "would look well on a cushion." I made this change in the small study, much to her satisfaction, and then did the hands likewise in the large portrait.

The New Year festivities were hardly over before the Empress Dowager decided to move the Court to the Sea Palace. This Palace, though not so much a favorite with her as the Summer Palace, she liked better than the Winter Palace; the latter's small, shut-in courts, walled-in walks, and rigid traditions seemed to depress her. At the Sea Palace she had gardens for her promenades and there was a lake. It was not so beautiful as the Summer Palace, but was an improvement over the Winter Palace.

===Chinese===
过年热闹的那几日我虽未动笔作画，画像此时倒真真有了起色。当初第一次坐班时，我曾斗胆挑剔过那个手势，如今手已画出，手掌藏在长长的毛皮内袖之下，仍是那个姿势，皇太后瞧见，当下便提议把毛皮内袖褪去；然而关于改一改手势的话，她仍只字未提——虽说我也看得出她心里存着疑惑，我却很有把握，以她那份好品味，到底会占上风，终归还是要改的。我便用极淡的颜色将手薄薄罩上一层，心知日后总要重画。果不其然，过了几日她便说「我那关于手势的主意倒也不坏」，又建议说左手「搁在一只垫子上倒好瞧」。我在那幅小稿上照此一改，太后甚是满意，我便在大画像上也将手照样改了。

新年的热闹方才散去，皇太后便决意迁銮西苑。这西苑虽不似颐和园那般得她欢心，她却较紫禁城更爱此处的；紫禁城里那些逼仄幽闭的庭院、夹在高墙之间的甬道，以及那些森严的旧规，似乎都令她闷闷不乐。在西苑，她有花园可以闲步，也有一片湖。景致虽不及颐和园，却比紫禁城要胜过一筹。

===Original===
This move to the Sea Palace necessitated another change of studio for me, just as I was comfortably installed in my quarters in the Winter Palace, and had begun to progress with my work. I knew I should be obliged to have the new place arranged with upper glass windows and that I would again lose time, and the date of the opening of the St. Louis Exposition was approaching! But there was no help for it; I must go with the Court to the Sea Palace. I was told that there I was to have a magnificent pavilion on the lake, with a perfect light for painting. As to the pavilion's being magnificent, I had no doubt, but I did doubt, from past experiences, whether the light would be all that could be desired.

One morning our chairs carried us to the Sea Palace instead of to the Winter Palace. All my painting things, materials, canvases, as well as Her Majesty's Throne, on which she was seated for the portrait, had been moved. Not the smallest piece of paper, nor even a bit of charcoal was missing. I had painted until the last moment at the Winter Palace, the day before; and early the next morning my things were in perfect order—the portrait on the easel, and the Throne in the proper position in my quarters at the Sea Palace. It was an "Aladdin's-Lamp" move.

The group of buildings that had been set aside for my painting fronted on the lake, and were really charming, but the overhanging verandahs to each pavilion forced me again to have the upper windows put in. After this was accomplished, it was the best working-room I had ever had at any of the Palaces. The days were getting longer and the light better, and I hoped now to soon finish the portrait.

===Chinese===
迁往西苑一事，害得我又得另换一处画室——其时我在紫禁城的寓所正住得安逸，画作也眼看着有了进展。我深知到了新地方，势必又要装上半截玻璃窗，这又要耽搁时日，而圣路易斯博览会的开幕之期已迫在眉睫！然而事已至此，别无他法；我只得随銮迁往西苑。听说我在那边可以分到湖上一座精美的亭阁，光线极佳，正宜作画。至于亭阁之精美，我倒不疑；只是依着从前的经验，那光线是否能如人所愿，我却十分存疑。

一日清晨，我们的轿子没往紫禁城去，却抬到了西苑。我所有的画具、颜料、画布，连同皇太后画像时所坐的那张宝座，都已搬运一空。便是最小的一张纸片，甚至一块炭条，也未曾失落。前一日我在紫禁城一直画到最后一刻；次日清早，我的什物已安置得妥妥帖帖——画架支着画像，宝座也安在西苑我那寓所里恰当的位置。这一番搬移，真如「阿拉丁神灯」一般。

拨给我作画的那一片殿宇，面湖而立，着实可人，只是每座亭阁上都有外伸的游廊，逼得我又不得不在上首装上玻璃窗。此事一毕，这里便成了我在三处宫禁中所曾有过的最好的画室。白日渐长，光线愈佳，我满心盼望这画像不久便可告竣。

===Original===
A few days after the Court moved to the Sea Palace, the members of the Corps Diplomatique were received in Audience to present their congratulations to the Emperor and Empress Dowager on the occasion of the Chinese New Year. They were received in the Great Audience Hall; but the ladies of the Legation, whose reception took place the following day, were received in Her Majesty's Throne-room opening on the Court of the large Theater at the Sea Palace. As it was cold, the Theater and its court were entirely enclosed and roofed over in glass, in panes of about a foot and a half square. On each pane was painted, in red, the ever-present character "Sho" (longevity), surrounded by five bats. The marble pavement of the court and the steps leading up to the Throne-room were carpeted in red; and when the great doors were thrown wide, there was a good effect of size given, although this Throne-room was one of the smallest in the Sea Palace.

As this was to be a formal reception, several members of the Wai-Wu-Pu were present as interpreters. The ladies of the Legation were presented by the Baron Czikan, the Austrian Minister, Doyen of the Corps. He made a graceful address in French, wishing Their Majesties a Happy New Year, and China much prosperity. This was translated into Chinese by one of the Secretaries of the Wai-Wu-Pu. The Empress Dowager replied for herself and the Emperor, in Chinese. Her Majesty's words were interpreted by His Excellency Liang Fang, a good French scholar. Then the Doyen presented the ladies individually, and the usual order of ceremonies followed. When the presentations were over, the Doyen, foreign attachés and interpreters, with the Chinese officials, repaired to the hall which had been set aside for their luncheon, while the ladies, accompanied by the Princesses, went to their repast in another part of the Palace!

===Chinese===
朝廷迁至西苑数日后，外交使团蒙皇上、皇太后召见觐见，恭贺中国新年。觐见在正大殿举行；而各国公使夫人则在次日接见，地点是皇太后的宝座间——那间屋子正通着西苑大戏台的庭院。其时天寒，戏台与其庭院一并用玻璃封顶围合，所用的玻璃约一英尺半见方。每一块玻璃上，都用红色描着那无处不在的「寿」字，四周环以五只蝙蝠。庭院的大理石地坪以及通向宝座间的台阶，都铺上了红毯；待到两扇大门洞开，倒也显出几分阔大的气象，虽说这间宝座间原是西苑最小的一间。

因这是正式的礼节性接见，外务部派了数位官员在场充当通译。各国公使夫人由使团团长、奥地利公使齐坎男爵（Baron Czikan）引领进见。他用流畅的法文致祝辞，祝两位陛下新年安康，亦祝中国国运昌盛。这祝辞由外务部的一位秘书译成中文。皇太后以中文作答，所言系为己也代皇上而发。太后之言又由梁诚（Liang Fang）大人转译——梁大人是一位通晓法文的饱学之士。随后团长便将各位夫人一一引见，接着便照例行的礼节行事。引见既毕，团长与各国参赞、通译，连同中国官员们，一起到专为他们设午宴的厅中入席，夫人们则在福晋格格的陪同下，到宫中另一处赴宴去了！

===Original===
Only a few days after this, came the lantern festival; but this was not an interruption to my work, for I painted all day, and only went to the Theater for the final piece and the spectacular tableau. We dined in the Imperial loge, and after dinner there were beautiful lantern and torchlight processions. In the court opposite the Throne-room where we dined, there was a beautiful pai-lou of transparent gauze, painted in charming designs, illuminated from within, and hung with luminous flowers and quaint lanterns. Tall eunuchs, in gala red, stood around the courts, holding great lanterns aloft, like huge caryatides with luminous burdens. Others with fanciful vermilion lanterns wound in and out through corridors and courts. When they reached the court of the softly glowing pai-lou, they maneuvered and made intricate designs and luminous tableaux, holding aloft their red-globed lanterns to form characters and phrases of "felicitous omen." These huge, luminous characters were wonderfully accurate.

After the torch- and lantern-lit processions, and the glowing tableaux, a pair of illuminated dragons writhed into the court and struggled for the "flaming pearl," which flitted around with elusive fantastic movements, ever beyond their grasp. I was not able to find out the origin of the Imperial legend of the Double Dragon and the Flaming Pearl, representations of which appear everywhere at the Palace on whatever is meant for Imperial use, or for any official function over which the Emperor is supposed to preside. It is on all the Thrones of the Dynasty; it adorns the Imperial pennant; it is cut into stone, carved into wood, and painted in pictures. It decorates the gowns of the higher officials, and is embroidered upon the Court dresses of the Ladies of the Palace. At the Birthdays of the Emperor and Empress, and at all Dynastic celebrations there are realistic representations of the immortal struggle where the Double Dragon strives to absorb the "flaming pearl." The significance of the legend seems to be: The Double Dragon represents the Powers of Earth or Evil which try ever to absorb the Flaming Pearl, Emblem of the Dynasty, symbol of Heaven or Perfection. The Flaming Pearl, the Unattainable, keeps ever beyond and above their grasp, seeming to serve always as an incentive for further effort.

===Chinese===
此后不过几日，便是灯节；然而这一回倒不致耽误我的活计，我白天整日作画，只到戏台去看末一出和那排场宏大的灯彩戏。我们在御用看阁里用了膳，饭罢便有那华美的灯笼火把游行。与我们用膳的宝座间相对的那个庭院里，立着一座精美的牌楼，系用透明纱罗扎成，上绘悦目的花样，内里点灯，又悬着明光烁烁的花朵和古趣盎然的彩灯。高高的太监们身穿节庆吉服的红袍，立在四周的庭院里，将大灯笼高高擎起，宛如一根根顶着光亮重担的巨大人像柱。另一些太监手执式样精巧的朱红灯笼，在游廊与庭院间穿进穿出。待他们行到那座柔光熠熠的牌楼所在的庭院，便操演起来，排出种种繁复的阵势和光灿灿的画面，他们将红球灯笼高高擎起，拼成种种「吉祥话」的字句。这些巨大的灯字，竟精确得出奇。

火把与灯笼游行既罢，光彩熠熠的灯彩戏散过，一对灯扎的龙蜿蜒游入庭院，争夺那颗「火珠」——火珠在四下里忽隐忽现，行动诡谲莫测，总在他们掌握之外。皇室「二龙戏珠」的传说，其源流如何，我未能考究清楚；然而宫中但凡御用之物，或皇上理应主持的任何典礼，处处都见得到这个图样的踪迹。本朝历代宝座之上，皆有此纹；御用旗帜上也以此作饰；或刻于石，或雕于木，或绘于画。高级官员的袍褂以此作饰，宫眷们的朝服上也以此绣为纹。每逢皇上、皇后的万寿，以及一切本朝庆典，都要把二龙争夺「火珠」的那场千古之争如实地演上一回。这一传说的寓意，似乎是这样的：二龙代表尘世之力，亦即邪恶之力，时时想要吞下那颗火珠——火珠乃是本朝的徽识，是上天或曰圆满的象征。然而火珠终归是求之不得之物，永远在他们伸手莫及之处高悬，仿佛始终是一种鞭策，催人不断精进。

===Original===
For a fortnight after the lantern festival, there were fireworks every night on the banks of the lake. We would dine in the Throne-room, and then Her Majesty and the Emperor, accompanied by the Ladies, and attended by the usual number of eunuchs (each bearing transparent horn lanterns), would go through the courts and paths of the garden to the lake, on the banks of which the fireworks were sent up. Here, in full view of the set pieces, stood four large, roomy sleds. When the lake was frozen, these sleds were used to push Their Majesties and the ladies over its glassy surface. They had not been used as sleds this winter, for the ice had not been sufficiently firm, the winter having been comparatively mild. But when the lake was well frozen, as is usual at this season in Peking, Their Majesties viewed the fireworks from these sleds as they skimmed along over its smooth surface. There was a sled for each of them—one for the Empress and second wife, and one for the Princesses. They were cloth-covered, lined with fur, and had great fur rugs. There were seats around the three sides; the wadded curtain, with its large square of plate-glass that hung down over the front, was taken off for the fireworks. Their Majesties occupied each of theirs alone, but the Empress had several of the Ladies in hers.

The fireworks were superb. There were beautiful set pieces, pagodas, with ladies on balconies, pavilions with grapevines, wistaria arbors, and beds of flowers so lifelike they seemed to grow at the side of the luminous cascades, and many other effects I had never seen before in fireworks. One day, during the time of the lantern festival, we had fireworks in the brilliant sunshine. When these day rockets exploded, all sorts of curious paper devices fell to the ground—fish dragons and animals, as well as flags and baskets. When anything interesting was revealed, Her Majesty would send the eunuchs to pick it up as it fell and bring it to her that she might examine it. Many fell outside the Palace walls, and she said these would give pleasure to the "poor people outside."

Formerly, at these fireworks in the Palace to celebrate the lantern festival, the public was admitted into the Enclosure, but this practice stopped when the two Empresses were Co-Regents for the first boy emperor, Tung-Chih. As this was coincident with the establishment of the first Foreign Legations in Peking, the latter fact may have had some influence in changing the custom. The Chinese people were shut out because it was feared that the foreigners might also come into the Precincts. These beautiful fireworks I could enjoy without any qualms of conscience, for I could not paint at night, and they were consequently no interruption to my work.

===Chinese===
灯节之后整整半个月，夜夜都在湖畔放烟火。我们往往在宝座间里用罢晚膳，皇太后与皇上便由宫眷们随侍，照例由一众太监（每人各执一盏透明角灯）簇拥着，穿过一道道庭院、一条条花园小径，来到湖畔——烟火便在湖岸上施放。这里正对着那些排设的烟火盒子，摆着四架宽大的雪橇。待到湖面封冻之时，这雪橇便用来推着两位陛下与众位宫眷在光洁如镜的冰面上滑行。这一年冬天，雪橇倒不曾当作雪橇来用，因冰冻得不够结实——这一个冬天算来尚算和暖。然而待到湖面冻得结结实实——在北京，这个时节本是如此——两位陛下便在这雪橇上，一面在光洁的冰面上轻快地滑行，一面观看烟火。两位陛下各乘一架——皇后与二夫人合一架，福晋格格们合一架。雪橇外蒙布面，内衬毛皮，里头还铺着厚厚的毛皮毯。三面都设有座位；那面挂着的大方平板玻璃的棉门帘，为看烟火便被卸下。两位陛下各自独坐一橇，皇后那橇里却带了好几位宫眷。

烟火实在精彩。有种种华美的烟火盒子，有宝塔、带阳台上的仕女，有葡萄藤缠绕的亭阁、紫藤花架，还有那一片片花圃，活灵活现，竟像是从那光灿灿的瀑布边生出来的一般，又有许多我在烟火里从未见过的花样。灯节期间有一天，我们在耀眼的日光底下放了一回烟火。这些白昼火箭炸开时，各式各样的纸制玩意儿纷纷坠地——鱼、龙、走兽，也有旗子和花篮。若有什么有趣的物事落下，皇太后便差太监去捡拾起来呈上，好让她细细赏鉴。有许多落到了宫墙之外，她说这些正可给「外头的穷苦人」增添几分乐趣。

从前宫中为灯节施放烟火时，本来还容百姓进入禁苑观看，但到了两宫皇太后为同治——头一位小皇帝——垂帘听政之时，此例便废止了。此事与各国在北京初次设立公使馆大约同时，后一事或者也与此番改例不无关系。中国百姓之所以被挡在门外，是怕外国人也跟着混进禁苑来。这好看的烟火，我大可安心赏玩，全无半点愧疚——我原不能在夜里作画，故而这烟火也并不耽误我的活计。

## IX The Emperor's Birthday / 第九章 皇上的万寿节

===Original===
We went to the Palace early the day of His Majesty's Birthday, and were in the Empress Dowager's Throne-room at six o'clock in the morning; but long before that time, the outer court was filled with the red and yellow chairs and carts of the visiting members of the Imperial Family, who had come in from Peking and from the neighboring Palaces for the day. The high eunuchs were in gala costume, wearing silken gowns of great beauty, embroidered in the Double Dragon. The eunuchs of lower rank were more simply gowned, as the representation of the Double Dragon on the Court gown is only allowed to those of a certain rank. Our chair-bearers were clad in the festive red, with brocaded figures, representing the characters for Longevity.

===Chinese===
皇上万寿节（Emperor's Birthday）这一日，我们一早就进了宫，早晨六点钟便已到了皇太后的宝座殿里；然而远在此时辰之前，外朝诸庭院已挤满了红黄两色的轿子和车驾——都是皇室宗亲的，他们或自北京（Peking）城中，或自附近各处宫殿赶来，专赴这一日的盛典。那品级高的太监（eunuch）们皆着礼服，身穿绣工绝美的绸袍，袍上绣的是双龙（Double Dragon）。品级低的太监所穿之袍较为素简，因朝袍之上能否绣双龙，是有品级限制的：唯具有一定品级者方准如此。我们这一抬轿的轿夫，皆穿一身喜庆的红，衣上是织锦的图案，作「寿」字花样。

===Original===
We passed through the beautifully decorated courts, past the gaily decked Palaces to the Throne-room of Her Majesty, where the Emperor had come to receive the private congratulations of the Princesses of the blood and the Ladies of the Court. It would have been against the laws of Chinese etiquette for these Ladies to go into the Emperor's Palace to congratulate him, even on such an occasion as his Birthday. When we entered the Throne-room, the Emperor was seated, or rather, reclining upon a lounge in the most informal manner. He was not averse, as was Her Majesty, to the reclining position when on the Throne. His greater Orientalism was evidenced here, for the Oriental proverb says, "'Tis better to be sitting than standing, to be lying than sitting," etc. He sat up a little straighter on our entrance, and the Ladies made the formal Chinese bow, which he returned by a friendly nod and kindly smile. I made the European reverence as usual.

===Chinese===
我们穿过张灯结彩的庭院，经过装饰得花团锦簇的宫殿，来到皇太后的宝座殿。皇上已先到此，接受宗室福晋格格与宫中命妇们的私下行礼祝贺。按中国的礼法规矩，纵是万寿节这等大日子，这些夫人也断不能径到皇上的寝宫去向他贺寿——那是有违礼数的。我们进宝座殿时，只见皇上坐着——或者不如说是斜倚在卧榻之上，神态最为随便。在宝座之上，皇太后素不取这半倚半卧的姿势，皇上却不然。他这股东方气派，于此可见一斑——东方有句谚语道：「坐胜于立，卧胜于坐」，云云。我们进来时，他略坐直了些；诸位命妇行了正式的中式之礼，皇上则点头答礼，含笑甚是和悦。我则照例行西式礼。

===Original===
His Majesty was dressed a little more elaborately than usual, in a yellow gown, tightly belted in around his slender waist with a handsome belt buckle of jade. At this morning salutation by the Ladies of his family, his hat lay beside him on the couch, which showed it was unceremonious, for ceremonies are carried on by the Emperor and all Chinese with their hats on. The great Imperial Pearl, one of the most precious of the Imperial jewels, formed the button of his hat on his Birthday. The seven official ranks of Mandarins are shown by the different colors of the buttons worn on their hats. The color of these buttons denotes the rank acquired by their wearers, those of the Manchu Princes, alone, being hereditary. The buttons of these latter are generally of jewels or semiprecious stones. The Emperor, the most simply dressed man I saw in China, wears, as a rule, a plain red silk button, but the Pearl, which can only be worn by a reigning Emperor, is used on state occasions.

===Chinese===
皇上这一日装束较平常稍为讲究，身着一袭黄袍，纤腰之间束着一条华美的玉带钩，束得紧紧的。今晨自家女眷行礼之时，他的帽子便放在身边的榻上——这便是非正式场合的明证，因凡有典礼，皇上乃至全体中国人，都是戴帽而行的。皇上万寿节的这顶帽子，帽顶所嵌的纽扣，便是那颗硕大的御用珍珠——御用珍宝中数一数二的宝物。朝廷命官分七品，品级之分，便在帽顶所嵌之「顶子」颜色上见出。这顶子的颜色，是戴者所获品级的标识；唯有满族（Manchu）王公的顶子乃是世袭。王公的顶子，多系宝石或半宝石制成。皇上是在中国我所见到的衣着最朴素的人，平日所戴不过一颗素红绸的顶子；但那颗御珠——唯在位之皇上方得佩戴——则用于大典礼之时。

===Original===
After we had greeted His Majesty, we moved further into the Throne-room to await the *lever* of the Empress Dowager. When she came out of her sleeping apartments, the Ladies fell upon their knees and simultaneously repeated the words of greeting used every morning to Her Majesty, "*Lao-Tzu-Tzung Chee-Siang*" (Great Ancestress, be happy). After acknowledging their salutations, she advanced and held out her hand to me, and I took it and, as was now my custom, raised the tips of her fingers to my lips. I, of course, never made any but a European salutation to either Her Majesty or the Emperor. She was very gracious and said I would be the first foreigner who had ever seen the birthday celebration of any of the Sons of Heaven, and she hoped I would enjoy it! She then commented on my dress and ornaments, examining the few jewels I wore. After this she turned to the Ladies and, with a quick glance, took in all the details of their Court costumes, calling their attention to the way their official beads hung and signaling any little deviation from traditional forms that she noticed in their attire. She was extremely rigid as to all the details of Court dress.

===Chinese===
我们向皇上行礼既毕，便往宝座殿更深处去，等候皇太后（Her Majesty the Empress Dowager）起身。太后从寝宫里出来时，诸位命妇一齐跪下，齐声念诵每日早晨对太后所用的那一套请安之语：「老祖宗吉祥」（*Lao-Tzu-Tzung Chee-Siang*，意即「老祖宗，请多珍重」）。太后受了她们的礼，便走上前来向我伸出手，我接过她的手，按我如今已成习惯的礼数，将她的指尖轻轻举至唇边。当然，我无论对皇上（the Emperor）或皇太后，从不曾行过别的礼，只行西式之礼。太后和颜悦色，说我是头一个得见任何一位天子（Sons of Heaven）万寿庆典的外国人，盼我看得开心！随后她又品评我的衣裙与首饰，端详了我所戴的几件珠宝。这之后，她转向诸位命妇，目光一扫，便将她们朝服上的一切细节尽收眼底：见她们的朝珠挂得有何不妥，便指点出来；衣饰上有何细处不合传统规矩，也一一指出。她对朝服的一切细节，规矩都极其森严。

===Original===
The Court costume of the Ladies is magnificent. That worn at the Emperor's Birthday (the summer costume) was of the stiff transparent silk I have described in the gown worn by Her Majesty for the portrait. The Court costume of the married ladies is of dark red, embroidered in golden dragons. The widows wear blue; the unmarried girls, bright red—all with the Double Dragon embroidered thereon. The married ladies and widows, when in Court attire, wear a magnificent court headdress with jeweled crown. The young girls, even in Court dress, wear the ordinary Manchu coiffure, with the long red silk tassels falling to their shoulders. The young Empress was charming on the Birthday. Her headdress was of golden filigree, thickly set with jewels. Across the front, nine beautifully chased golden phoenix, with jeweled tails outspread, held in their bills strings of pearls that fell to her shoulders and veiled her forehead. Square, conventionalized bunches of flowers projected from either side of this curiously and elaborately wrought headdress. Her gown was of the Imperial yellow, embroidered with the golden Double Dragon. She had, around her neck, a solid piece of chased gold, like a huge open ring, with balls at the ends; and she wore the official beads that are always worn in Court dress by Princes and Officials and their wives. The Emperor and Empress Dowager are the only members of the Court who wear, neither the Double Dragon on their Court dress, nor the official beads. Suspended from the Empress's neck was a magnificently embroidered stole, about four inches wide, which reached to the hem of her gown. This stole is only worn by the wives of Emperors, during their husband's lifetime. The young Empress seemed unusually happy today, and this was the first time I had ever seen her and the Emperor in conversation. Next to the young Empress came the only secondary wife of the Emperor. She was dressed exactly as the young Empress was; the same gown, the same headdress, the same embroidered stole, only her jewels were not so handsome, and her dress, instead of being of the Imperial yellow, was of orange. Yellow can only be worn by the first wife of an Emperor!

===Chinese===
命妇们的朝服甚是华美。万寿节所着的（夏朝服）正是那种硬挺的薄如蝉翼的丝绸——我前文形容过皇太后画像时所穿的那一件便是。已嫁妇人的朝服用暗红色，上绣金线龙。寡居的夫人穿蓝色；未出阁的姑娘则穿鲜红色——袍上无不绣有双龙。已嫁夫人和寡居之人在朝服之上，皆戴一顶华美的朝冠，冠上饰满珠宝。年轻姑娘即便着朝服，也只梳寻常的满族发髻，长长的红丝流苏垂至肩头。万寿节这一日，皇后（the Young Empress）风姿动人。她的朝冠为金丝累丝所制，密密嵌着珠宝。冠前横列九只雕工绝美的金凤，凤尾作扇形展开，尾上镶珠宝；金凤口衔珠串，垂至她的肩头，覆在她的额前。这顶工艺奇巧繁复的朝冠两侧，各伸出一方块扇形的花簇。她的袍子是御用明黄色，绣金线双龙。她颈间戴着一件雕花纯金的饰物，形如一只硕大的开口指环，两端各有一颗圆球；她身上还挂着朝珠——王公大臣及其命妇着朝服时无不挂此。皇上与皇太后是宫中唯独二位朝服之上既不绣双龙、也不挂朝珠的人。皇后颈间还悬着一条绣工绝美的披肩，约四英寸宽，一直垂到袍子的下摆。这一条披肩，唯有皇上之妻于夫君在世之时方得披用。皇后今日似格外欢悦，这也是我头一次见她与皇上说话。皇后之下，便是皇上唯一的那位侧室（secondary wife）。她的衣饰与皇后分毫不差：同一式袍子，同一式朝冠，同一条绣花披肩；只是珠宝稍逊一筹；衣裳的颜色不是御用明黄，而是橘黄。原来明黄色，唯皇上正妻方得穿！

===Original===
After the salutations to the Emperor and Empress Dowager in Her Majesty's private Throne-room, Her Majesty went out into the court and took her place in her yellow chair of State, the Emperor following, on foot, as was his custom. The cymbals clashed. The flutes sounded and all the instruments of the Imperial Band played the curious minor air, with its tragic undertone of sound, its rhythm like a Gregorian chant, which is only played at the passing of Their Majesties for some great ceremony or official function, and which I soon called the "Imperial Hymn." This is the only approach to a National air that I ever heard in China.

===Chinese===
在皇太后私用的宝座殿里向皇上、皇太后行礼既毕，太后便出到院中，登上她那乘明黄色的仪轿；皇上照例步行随后。铜钹铿锵。长笛悠扬，宫廷乐队的全部乐器齐奏那一支奇特的小调曲子——曲调里有一种悲怆的低音，节奏如格雷果圣咏（Gregorian chant）一般。此乐唯在二位陛下为某大典或官式仪仗出行之时方才奏起，不久我便称它作「国歌」（Imperial Hymn）。这是我在中国所听到的、唯一近似国歌的曲子。

===Original===
Their Majesties went in ceremonious procession to the Great Audience Hall, where the Princes, Nobles, and high Officials privileged to enter Precincts, were to present their homage and congratulations to the Son of Heaven on the happy occasion of his Birthday. Besides these privileged visitors, there were a number of officials whose rank was not high enough to allow them to enter the Great Hall of Ceremonies. These kneel and make the prostrations in the outer courts.

===Chinese===
二位陛下排齐全副仪仗，前往大朝殿（Great Audience Hall）。此殿之中，王公贵胄与够品级入苑的高官，要在皇上这一吉庆的万寿之日，向这位天子（Son of Heaven）恭行朝贺之礼。除了这些有幸入苑的宾客之外，还有许多品级不够、不准入这大朝殿行礼的官员。这些人只在外朝诸庭院里跪下行叩拜礼。

===Original===
The young Empress and Ladies of the Court did not follow Their Majesties to the Great Hall, but stopped at the Palace of the young Empress, to await there their turn for the official congratulations, which were not to be made until after those of the Princes and Nobles. The young Empress is a charming hostess, and her eunuchs and women handed us tea and cigarettes while we were waiting. She also had her dogs brought in for me to see. Her apartments opened on a sunny court, full of flowering shrubs and fruit trees. Around the other three sides of the court were built the pavilions for the use of her attendants and ladies. We spent half an hour in her pavilion, waiting for the congratulations of the Princes and Nobles to be finished.

===Chinese===
皇后与宫中命妇们并未随二位陛下前往大朝殿，而在皇后的宫里停下，候在那里轮到自己行正式的贺礼——她们的贺礼须排在诸王公贵胄之后。皇后是一位可亲的女主人，我们等候之时，她的太监和女侍为我们奉上茶和香烟。她又命人把她的狗抱来给我看。她的寝宫开向一处向阳的庭院，院里满栽着花木果树。庭院其余三面，建着她随侍女官女侍们所用的阁子。我们在她的阁子里坐了半个钟头，等候王公贵胄们的贺礼行完。

===Original===
The Emperor, for these official congratulations, was seated upon the Dynastic Throne, erect and stiff as an archaic figure; no longer the shy boy, but the Monarch clothed in all his power, and, for today, alone upon his great ancestral Throne. He was attended by his Master of Ceremonies, gorgeously attired, who stood in the rigid attitude prescribed for this ceremony.

===Chinese===
皇上受此正式朝贺之时，端坐于本朝历代相传的御座之上，腰背挺直，凝然不动，宛若一尊古旧的造像。他不再是那个羞怯的少年，而是手握全权的君主，唯此一日，独自端坐于他那伟大的祖传宝座之上。在他身侧侍立的，是他的鸿胪官（Master of Ceremonies），装束极尽华美，按此礼所定的姿势，僵直而立。

===Original===
Each splendidly garbed Prince and Noble knelt and made the prostration prescribed by the Book of Rites, and each presented His Majesty with a jade emblem, called by the Chinese *ruyie*,3 erroneously supposed to be a scepter by most foreigners; but the *ruyie* is simply an emblem of Good Luck, and may be presented on festive occasions to anyone whom the givers wish to honor, and is not an emblem of Imperial authority. The Emperor held each of these *ruyie* in his hands for a few seconds after their presentation, bowed profoundly to the kneeling Prince, and then handed the emblem to an attendant eunuch, who placed it on a Dragon table at the left of the Emperor. When the Princes and Nobles had congratulated His Majesty and left the Throne-room, the young Empress and secondary wife, followed by the Princesses and Ladies, went in to make their official congratulations. The greeting in Her Majesty's Throne-room in the morning had been but a friendly salutation, without any official signification. The young Empress knelt and made her bow first and presented—as did each of the Ladies—a *ruyie*. She made the same official salutation as did the others, but her *ruyie* was of a much richer style than those presented by the other Ladies.

===Chinese===
每一位衣饰华美的王公贵胄，依次跪下，行《礼记》（Book of Rites）所规定的叩拜之礼，并各向皇上呈献一件玉质的吉祥之物，中国称之为「如意」（*ruyie*）——多数外国人误以为这是一种权杖；其实如意不过是一件吉祥之物，凡喜庆之日，献者欲向何人致敬，便可献与何人，并非皇权的象征。皇上接过每一柄如意，于手中把玩数秒，向那位跪着的王公深深一揖，再将此物交与身侧侍立的太监，太监便将它放在皇上左侧的龙几上。诸王公贵胄既向皇上贺毕，退出了宝座殿，皇后与那位侧室，随后由诸王妃格格与命妇们簇拥着，进去行正式的朝贺之礼。清晨在皇太后宝座殿里那一番行礼，不过是私下致意，并无官式礼数的意义。皇后先跪下行礼，并献上一柄如意——一如诸位命妇所献。她所行的官礼与众人相同，只是她的如意，较之其他命妇所献的，更为华贵。

===Original===
After the ceremony of formal congratulations was over, Her Majesty, the Emperor, and Empress, followed by the Ladies and attendants, went in state to the Theater, with the same ceremonial and pomp with which they had gone into the Hall of Ceremonies. The Empress Dowager, who was always the most gorgeously attired person at Court, was, on His Majesty's Birthday, dressed with an extreme simplicity that amounted almost to plainness, and she wore no jewels. This plainness of attire was not an accident, but had been arranged with her usual forethought. She wished the Emperor and Empress to be the central figures of this day's festivities, and did not wish to vie with the Empress even in her attire.

===Chinese===
正式朝贺之礼既毕，皇太后、皇上、皇后，由命妇和随从们簇拥着，排齐全副仪仗，前往戏楼——所行仪注排场，与方才进大朝殿之时一般无二。皇太后素来是宫中衣饰最华丽的人，然皇上万寿节这一日，她却穿得极其素淡，几近于简朴，身上也不戴一件珠宝。这素朴的装束并非出于偶然，而是她照例先期安排好的。她要叫皇上和皇后做这一日庆贺的中心，连衣着上也不愿与皇后争辉。

===Original===
The Princes and Nobles, who had come to the Palace for the official congratulations, were invited to the theatrical performance. They occupied the boxes that ran at right angles to the Imperial loge, which I have already described as forming the other two sides of the court of the Theater. A huge screen of painted silk, twelve feet high, was stretched from the last of the boxes occupied by the Princes to the stage—allowing the latter to be perfectly seen by the occupants of the boxes, but cutting off their view of the Imperial loge, whence Their Majesties, the Empress, and Ladies viewed the play. These invited guests are thus neither seen by the Imperial party, nor can they see the latter.

===Chinese===
进宫行正式朝贺的王公贵胄们，也蒙恩被请来看戏。他们坐在与御用看阁成直角的那几间隔厢里——前文我已说过，这些厢座构成戏楼庭院其余的两面。从王公们所坐的最后一间隔厢起，直至戏台，张起了一道十二英尺高的彩绘大绢屏——这屏风使隔厢里的人看戏台一览无遗，却将他们望向御用看阁的视线尽数遮断。御用的看阁，正是二位陛下、皇后与命妇们观戏之处。如此，这些被邀的客人既不能为御前的人所见，也看不见御前的人。

===Original===
When Their Majesties and the Empress were seated in their loge, the principal actors came to the front of the stage, knelt, and kowtowed to the Imperial box. Then the play began. There was first a noisy burst of weird music, then the chief actor recited a laudatory, congratulatory poem in honor of the Birthday of the Emperor, wishing His Majesty "ten thousand years" of happiness and all the blessings possible. The poem was intoned like a chant by the actor, dressed in the gorgeous historic costume of an Imperial Herald of the time of Kublai Khan. This poem was most impressive. One of the verses ran thus:

> "The vast merits of His Imperial Majesty's August Ancestors have been handed down to Him from generation to generation.

"To the wisdom of His whole Dynasty we owe it, that we have lived in happiness,

"Ever ready to comply with the lofty teaching of our Rulers, leading us unto Good.… "

The poem went on to recite His Majesty's merits as a son, his respect for his August Mother, his filial piety, and ended with a wish that Great China might flourish and prosper—grow strong outwardly and inwardly, through the blessings of his reign and his desire for Progress.

===Chinese===
二位陛下与皇后在看阁里坐定，主要伶人们便走到台前跪下，向御座叩头。然后戏便开场。先是一阵喧天古怪的乐声，紧接着那正生朗诵一首颂扬皇上万寿的贺诗，祝皇上享「万岁」之福，凡百吉祥，无所不备。这诗由那伶人如吟如诵地唱出，他身着忽必烈汗（Kublai Khan）时代御前宣旨官那般华美的古装。这一首诗气派甚是庄严。其中一节道：

> 「皇上巍巍先祖之大德，世世代代，传之于陛下。

> 「吾等得以安乐而居，皆赖本朝列圣之明，

> 「吾等恭循吾主之崇高教化，引吾侪以归于善……」

诗中又诵念皇上为人之子的美德、他对圣母的敬重、他的孝道，末了祝愿大清国兴隆昌盛——内修外攘，日益强盛，皆赖皇上当朝之福与其求新之志。

===Original===
After this poem had been intoned by the chief actor, with the whole company of players grouped around on the lower, as well as on the two superposed stages, all in splendid historic costumes, there was another noisy clash of weird music and the play itself began. The Chinese theater, which goes on from morning to night with a series of plays, generally begins with a short one, a curtain-raiser of a quarter to half an hour's length. Today it began at once, after the poem was intoned, with a great historic drama. The exploits and high deeds of former Emperors were shown, and the actors were magnificently costumed in superb historic gowns which had been handed down from antiquity and were absolutely authentic.

===Chinese===
这首诗由正生朗诵既毕，全体伶人围着站立——下层台以及上下两层叠台之上，皆站满了人，个个身着华美绝伦的古装——又是一阵喧天的古怪乐声，正戏便开场了。中国戏楼自晨至夜接连演一整串戏文，通常头一出是短的，不过一刻到半点钟的「开锣戏」。今日却在诵诗之后，径直便开演一出盛大的历史大戏。所演乃是前朝帝王的丰功伟业，伶人所穿的，皆是历代相传的旧戏衣，华美绝伦，确为古制。

===Original===
At half-past eleven, with the Theater still in full swing, the eunuchs brought out tables of sweetmeats on the verandah of the Imperial loge, and set them before the young Empress and the Princesses and Ladies, and we were served to refreshments. Sweets and fruits in China are served between the regular meals. The sweetmeats today were "birthday food," and were all inscribed with some character meaning "Longevity," "Good Luck," "Happiness," "Peace," etc. There were pyramids of the delicious crystallized fruits which the Chinese excel in making; macédoines of queer fruits, nut pastes, almond creams, and all the fresh fruits in season. With this preliminary repast were served, also, some delicious Chinese wines.

===Chinese===
十一点半，戏台上正演得热闹，太监们在御用看阁的廊上摆出几桌点心，放在皇后、王妃格格与命妇们面前，我们便受此款待。在中国，糖果鲜果是放在两顿正餐之间吃的。今日的点心是「寿筵之物」，上头都用糖霜或模具印着「寿」「福」「喜」「平安」之类的字样。有塔似的一盘盘蜜饯果脯——此种手艺中国人最是擅长；有种种奇果杂拌、果仁膏、杏仁酪，以及当令的各种鲜果。这一道席前的点心，还配有几样美味的中国酒。

===Original===
Soon after the repast of sweetmeats was finished, we were served in the court of the Theater this time to the regular meal. It was an immense table to which we sat down on the Emperor's Birthday. There were so many Princesses, Duchesses, and Ladies of high degree from a distance, that our usual number was more than quadrupled. The repast was a joyous one. The Chinese are very witty and gay, and though I could not understand all the scintillations of wit, their gaiety was contagious! Each gave me special delicacies that she liked, to try, and each seemed to vie with the other in endeavoring to make the "stranger" feel at ease. Some of the Ladies drank champagne in my honor, and held up their glasses toward me as they had seen the foreigners do. When the elders had finished eating, the young people sat down. These were the children of the Princesses and Nobles who had been invited to join their parents for these festivities at the Palace. No girl or boy under sixteen is allowed to sit down with their elders to a ceremonious dinner at the Palace.

===Chinese===
点心既罢不久，这一次是在戏楼庭院里开正餐。皇上万寿节这一日，我们所坐的是极大的一桌。从远道而来的王妃福晋与高品命妇极多，我们寻常的席面比之此刻，四倍也不止。这一席吃得其乐融融。中国人本就善谐谑、生性旷达，虽她们那些妙语机锋我不能尽解，她们那份快活却是叫人感染的！每个人都把她自己所喜的珍馐夹给我尝，似乎彼此争着要叫这位「外客」自在适意。几位夫人还为了我饮香槟，举杯向我致意——这是她们见洋人这么做过，便学了样。长辈用毕，小辈们才入座。这些小辈，乃是被邀随父母入宫同庆的王公贵胄家的儿女。盖宫中盛筵，十六岁以下的男孩女孩，是不准与长辈同席而坐的。

===Original===
Soon after we had finished our gay luncheon in the court of the Theater the Ladies retired within their loge, next to that of Their Majesties, and the screen which hid the visiting Princes and Nobles from the Imperial party was removed by the attendant eunuchs. When it was taken away, there sat, Turkish fashion, the great Princes and high Nobles in their splendid Court dress. Those of the highest ranks occupied the boxes nearest the Imperial party. The Princesses pointed out to me, from their box, their brothers and kinsmen and others whom they recognized; but we saw without being seen, and were only looking from behind the scenes.

===Chinese===
我们在戏楼庭院里那顿欢宴既毕，命妇们便退入紧邻御用看阁的自家看阁之中；方才隔在来贺王公贵胄与御前之间的那道屏风，便由当值的太监撤去。屏风一撤，只见那些冠冕堂皇、朝服齐整的大王公贵胄们，正盘腿坐在那里。品级最高的几位，坐在离御前最近的隔厢里。王妃格格们从自家厢座里，将她们的兄弟亲族、以及她们认得的旁人一一指给我看；只是我们看得见人，人却看不见我们——我们不过是从幕后窥看罢了。

===Original===
The eunuchs then handed around refreshments to the Princes and gentlemen, sweetmeats and fruits, such as we had partaken of before our luncheon. Then there were some huge steaming silver cauldrons brought into the court, and from these cauldrons the eunuchs ladled into bowls some sort of white drink. As we had had nothing of this kind at our repast, I was curious to know what it might be. I knew it could not be wine, for that is served only in tiny cups, and this was served in the ordinary-sized eating-bowls. I was much surprised to learn that this drink was simply hot milk, flavored with almonds, and slightly sweetened, a drink of which the Manchus are very fond, and which is a special mark of Imperial favor, given only on great occasions. The gentlemen raised their bowls to their lips with both hands and drank it off with great ceremony, as if it were a sacred beverage, and seemed, in drinking it thus, to pledge the Emperor's Health and Happiness.

===Chinese===
随后太监们便向王公大人们奉上点心，糖果鲜果之类，与我们席前所用的相同。接着，几只冒着热气的大银锅被抬进庭院，太监们从这些锅里舀出一种白色的饮品，盛在碗里。我们方才席上并无此物，我不免好奇它究竟是什么。我断定那不会是酒——因酒只用极小的杯盏盛，此刻所盛的却是寻常吃饭用的碗。后来我大为惊奇地得知，此饮品不过是杏仁味、略加甜味的热牛奶——满族人极爱此味，乃是御赐特恩的标志，唯有大节方才赐饮。诸位大人双手捧碗齐唇，煞有介事地将它饮尽，仿佛这是何等神圣的饮品，看那神情，竟似在为皇上的福寿安康而举杯。

===Original===
After the Princes had partaken of these refreshments, and while some eunuchs were removing the cauldrons and dishes, another army of eunuchs came in, in pairs, each pair carrying between them trays of Imperial yellow, decorated with the red characters for Longevity. These trays contained presents from the Emperor to each of the invited guests, for His Majesty gives as well as receives presents on his Birthday! There was no difference made in the presents given, each tray being the exact counterpart of every other. Each contained a pair of porcelain vases from the Imperial Potteries, a bronze Incense-burner, a scroll with a quotation from the classics or an aphorism of Confucius written thereon. The scrolls were enclosed in silken covers, tied with the Imperial colors. There was also a jade *ruyie* in each tray, such as had been handed the Emperor at the morning ceremony, and an Archer's ring. After the contents of the trays had been delivered to each gentleman present, and the empty trays borne away by the Palace eunuchs, the dividing screen was again placed between the visiting Princes and Their Majesties, and the young Empress and Ladies went out of their loge to the verandah once more, and the theatrical performance again went on. In fact, it had been going on throughout our luncheon and the subsequent entertainment of the Princes, but we had paid no attention to it.

===Chinese===
王公们用罢这些点心，正当几名太监在撤锅撤盘之际，又有一大群太监成双成对地进来，每两人抬一只御用明黄色的托盘，盘上饰以红色的「寿」字。这些托盘里盛的，是皇上赐予每一位来宾的礼物——盖皇上万寿节，既受贺亦赐物！所赐各人皆同，每只托盘中所盛之物，与他盘分毫不差。每盘中有一对御窑所出的瓷瓶，一只铜香炉，一轴卷子，卷上所书或为经典之句，或为孔子（Confucius）的格言。卷子皆盛于锦套之中，系以御色丝绦。盘中还有一柄玉如意——如清晨典礼上献给皇上的那一种——以及一枚扳指（Archer's ring）。诸盘所盛之物既已分赐在座诸公，空盘由宫中太监们撤下，隔在来贺王公与二位陛下之间的那道屏风又重新张起；皇后与命妇们从自家看阁里再回到廊上，戏台上又演了下去。其实，方才我们午宴之时、与随后款待王公们之时，戏一直未停，只是我们无暇顾及罢了。

===Original===
At four o'clock there was the grand finale. The three superposed stages were occupied by all the gorgeously attired actors, and another Hymn of praise to the Emperor was intoned. He was extolled as the Son of Heaven and representative on earth of Buddha, and other extravagant wishes for "ten thousand years" of happiness were made. When this Hymn was finished, the floats, which we had seen the day before behind the scenes, came out in procession. These floats represented mythical animals, Buddhas, fairies, and personifications of the higher attributes. There were gigantic fruits which opened, disclosing figures representing eternal beauty, perfect happiness, and serene old age. Prominent among the gigantic fruits was the peach, the emblem of Longevity. Last of all, in this curious procession, came the Imperial Dragon, of huge proportions. Its contortions, as it struggled for the Flaming Pearl, emblematic of the unattainable, were most curious. All these figures made their obeisances to Their Majesties and the Empress. They were accompanied by splendidly clothed warriors, heralds, princes, and many gorgeously attired attendants, bearing banners and escutcheons. After the procession had made the tour several times, the dragon stopped with his huge head in the middle of the stage, made an obeisance to His Majesty, then raised it with a mighty roar and spouted forth—a copious shower of fresh spring water, which sprinkled the whole flower-filled court! The Empress and Princesses were all in the secret and knew what was coming, but they kept it from me, and much enjoyed my start of surprise as some of the spray fell upon me, as I had advanced to the very edge of the verandah in order to miss nothing.

===Chinese===
四点钟，便是压轴的大结局。三层叠台之上站满了华服盛装的全班伶人，又朗诵了一首颂扬皇上的颂歌。歌中称颂皇上为天子、为佛陀在世间的代表，并作种种夸张的祝愿，祝皇上享「万岁」之福。这一颂歌既毕，前一日我们在后台所见的那些「抬阁」，便排队而出。这些抬阁所扮的，是种种神兽、诸佛、仙子，以及种种高尚德性的化身。有巨大的仙果，果壳张开，从中现出象征永恒之美、圆满之乐、安享遐龄的人物。巨果之中最显眼的，是桃——长寿之征。这一支奇特的游行队伍压轴出场的，是一条硕大无朋的御龙。它为争夺那颗「火珠」（Flaming Pearl）——「不可得之物」的象征——而翻腾挣扎，种种扭动之态，最为奇观。这一切人物，都向二位陛下与皇后行礼。簇拥着它们的，是装束华美的武士、宣旨官、王孙公子，以及一大群服饰鲜明、执旗擎盾的扈从。游行队伍绕场数匝之后，那龙停了下来，硕大的龙头正落在台子正中，先向皇上行了一礼，随即昂首大吼一声，喷出一股——新鲜泉水如大雨倾盆，洒了满满一庭院繁花！皇后与王妃格格们皆在秘中，知道接下来要有什么好戏，却故意瞒着我，见我被那水雾溅了一身、吓了一跳，个个乐不可支——原来我为不放过半点热闹，已挤到廊前的最边沿去了。

===Original===
When all was finished, the screen was again removed and the great glass doors of the Imperial loge were thrown open, so that Her Majesty and the Emperor could be seen. The visiting Princes and Nobles came forward from their places and knelt in a body, though still observing the laws of precedence as to their ranks. They knelt three times, and bowed their heads to the ground nine times to thank Their Majesties for the entertainment they had received. To receive these prostrations from the Princes, the Emperor and Empress Dowager assumed their Buddha-like poses and acknowledged the genuflections by a formal inclination of their heads. When the Princes had retired, the actors, clothed in their usual garments, came to the front of the stage and knelt and kowtowed, but Their Majesties did not return this salutation.

===Chinese===
待一切既毕，那屏风又被撤去，御用看阁的几扇大玻璃门一齐打开，皇太后与皇上便现身其中。来贺的王公贵胄们从各自的座处上前来，齐齐跪下——虽跪，仍按品级森严地排着次第。他们三跪九叩，谢二位陛下的款待。皇上与皇太后受了诸王公的跪拜，便端坐如佛像一般，以正式的颔首受了他们的叩首之礼。王公们既退，伶人们换上寻常衣裳，走到台前跪下叩头，但二位陛下并未还礼。

===Original===
When the Princes and players had left and the Imperial party was alone, cushions were brought into the middle of the court, the Emperor and Empress and secondary wife knelt thereon, while their "Great Ancestress," the Empress Dowager, preceded by acolytes, swinging golden incense-burners which gave forth azure clouds of perfumed smoke, came down the steps to the weird accompaniment of the flutes and cymbals playing the "Imperial Hymn." The Emperor and Empress knelt to do Her Majesty homage, as the greatest living member of their Ancestors. When she reached them, they arose and followed her, and the three moved along in stately procession to the slow beating of the cymbals, followed by the Princesses and Ladies and all the attendant eunuchs. The subtle perfume of the incense, the stately rhythm, the splendid costumes, the flashing jewels and brilliant colors, made a magnificent picture never to be forgotten. The Imperial procession moved through several sunlit courts until it finally came to the entrance of the Sacred Hall, containing the Ancestral tablets; here the Empress Dowager stopped at the threshold until His Majesty and the young Empress had passed within, to complete the ceremonies of the day by worshiping and kneeling together before the tablets of their Ancestors. The music ceased. The ceremony was finished. His Majesty the Emperor Kwang-Hsu had accomplished another year.

===Chinese===
王公与伶人们既退，御前仅余自家人时，便有人将坐垫铺在庭院正中，皇上、皇后与那位侧室跪在垫上；而他们那位「大长辈」皇太后，前有侍僧手执金香炉开道，炉中升起缕缕碧青的香烟，在长笛铜钹所奏「国歌」那古怪的乐声里，缓缓下了台阶。皇上与皇后跪下，向皇太后行礼——她是他们祖先中辈分最高、尚在人间的一位。太后到了他们跟前，他们便起身随后；三人在徐徐的钹声之中，缓缓前行，排成庄严的仪仗；其后随行的，是诸位王妃格格、命妇以及一应随侍的太监。那若有若无的幽香、庄严的节奏、华美的衣冠、璀璨的珠宝与绚烂的色彩，汇成一幅永难忘怀的壮丽画面。这一支御用的仪仗穿过几重阳光照耀的庭院，终于来到奉先殿（Sacred Hall）的门口——殿中供奉着祖先的神主牌位；皇太后在门槛外停下，直待皇上与皇后入内，于祖先牌位之前一同跪拜行礼，以完毕这一日的典礼。乐声止歇。典礼既毕。光绪皇帝陛下（His Majesty the Emperor Kwang-Hsu）又过了一年。

## XXXI The Empress Dowager’s Charities, Sense of Justice, Extravagance, and Personal Characteristics / 三十一 皇太后的慈善、正义感、奢靡与个人性情

===Original===
The Empress Dowager’s charities are extensive; she feeds the poor and succors the unfortunate. When her sympathies are aroused, she gives freely and generously. Her edicts are constantly ordering sacks of rice and food to be distributed among the poor and sent to districts where famine reigns. There is a great refuge in Peking, which she supports, where ten thousand poor are succored and fed during the year. During the winter, edicts are constantly appearing similar to this—commanding “The distribution from the Imperial granaries of fifteen hundred piculs (133⅓ pounds to the picul) of rice for refuges and gruel stations for the poor in North Tung Chow.” Edict of November 6, 1904.
She also sympathizes with misfortune, tries to right the wrongs she knows of, and correct the abuses that come to her ears. From the *North China Herald* of November 19, 1904, I copy the following, and this paper cannot be accused of viewing any of Her Majesty’s acts with a partial spirit (much to the contrary):

> “During the Boxer troubles a bad character, by the name of Wang, owed money to a certain Chinese Mohammedan. Wang had been frequently dunned, and was finally condemned by the courts to pay the debt; he was, besides, ordered to be beaten, as he had been insolent to the Mandarin trying the case. This incensed Wang, and he swore vengeance. When the Boxer troubles were in full swing in Peking, he became the leader of a band of insurgents and led his band to the house of the Mohammedan whom he had been forced by the courts to pay. Wang and his band massacred not only his old enemy, but eleven members of his family; leaving only a young daughter-in-law who had hidden in a loft and saw the whole tragedy; she also saw them march off, carrying, on spears, the heads of the old man and four of his sons.
> “The poor daughter-in-law escaped from Peking soon after, and was not able to return there until a few months since, in 1904. She discovered the dwelling of the murderer of her husband’s family, and had a petition drawn up on the subject.
> “One day when Her Majesty was proceeding from one Palace to another, the young widow threw herself before the Empress Dowager’s cortège. Her Majesty saw the prostrate girl (only nineteen years of age), and commanded her guards to ask what she wanted. The girl, dressed in deep mourning, held above her head her petition, calling for justice against her husband’s murderers. Her Majesty read the petition, and her brow became black as night. She called to a eunuch in her train and commanded him to take the young petitioner and her petition to the ‘Board of Punishments,’ and deliver the Imperial Commands that no time be lost in arresting the murderers; that they should be tried, and the result reported to Her Majesty. This was done, and on the first of November, 1904, the chief murderer Wang, his two sons and a nephew were decapitated to expiate their cruel crimes.”

===Chinese===
皇太后乐善好施，所及甚广；她周济贫苦，抚恤不幸。一旦动了恻隐之心，便慷慨施与，毫不吝惜。她的谕旨频频下令，将一袋袋米粮食物分赐贫民，发往饥荒之地。北京城内有一处她出资维持的大收容所，一年之中所收容赈济的贫民不下万人。入冬之后，谕旨接连不断，与此相类——下令“由官仓拨米一千五百石（每石合一百三十三又三分之一磅），发放北通州（North Tung Chow）各处贫民收容所与粥厂”。此为 1904 年 11 月 6 日所颁。
她亦怜恤不幸，凡有所闻的冤屈必力求昭雪，凡有所知的弊政必力求纠正。下面一段我自 1904 年 11 月 19 日的《北华捷报》（*North China Herald*）转录而来——而这家报纸断不能被指为偏袒皇太后的任何举措（恰恰相反）：

> “拳乱之时，有个姓王的歹人欠了一名中国回回的银钱。王某屡被催讨，终为官府判令偿债；又因在审案的官员面前无礼，被判杖责。王某怀恨在心，誓图报复。及至北京拳乱正炽之际，他便作了为首之乱民，领着一伙人径奔那名他被迫偿债的回回家中。王某一伙不仅杀死了旧仇本人，更屠戮其家眷十一口；只留下一个年轻的儿媳妇藏身阁楼之上，目睹了整场惨剧；她亲眼看着他们挑在枪矛上，将那老者与其四个儿子的头颅扬长而去。
> “那可怜的媳妇事后不久便逃出北京，直到 1904 年前数月方得重回故地。她访得杀害她夫家满门的凶手住处，便据此写就了一纸呈词。
> “一日，皇太后正自一宫移驾另一宫，那年轻的寡妇忽地扑到皇太后仪仗之前。皇太后看见了那俯伏在地的女子（年方十九岁），便命侍卫问其何求。那女子一身重孝，将呈词高举过头，请求为她丈夫的凶手伸冤。皇太后览毕呈词，面色霎时阴沉如夜。她唤过随行的一名太监，命他将这名年轻的呈控人与她的呈词一并送往“刑部”，并传下懿旨：立刻缉拿凶手，不得延误；审明之后，将结果回奏皇太后。此事一一照办，至 1904 年 11 月 1 日，首犯王某及其二子一侄皆已伏法斩首，以偿其残暴之罪。”

===Original===
The Empress Dowager is said to be recklessly extravagant in her own habits as well as in the management of Palace affairs. As for extravagance in the Palace, bad management doubtless exists, and extravagance does prevail. Abuses always creep in where the management of great establishments is entrusted to money-seeking officials, and to eunuchs, as is the case in the Palace at Peking.

Extravagance in the Palace has been the theme of Chinese economists for many generations, for hundreds of years before the Manchu Dynasty came to the Throne. Several of the Emperors have themselves attempted to stem this extravagance by personal efforts and private economy, but to no avail. It is related of one Emperor that the sleeve of his State robe being a little worn, he called up his Master of the Household to ask what a new robe would cost. He found that it would cost three thousand taels, and as only the right sleeve of this gown was worn (as he used his arm a great deal in writing), he decided, in order that he might himself show a good example, and inaugurate economy, to have a new sleeve made, instead of ordering an entire gown. He gave his commands, in consequence, and the gown was taken out of the Palace and remained several months. When it was returned, what was His Majesty’s astonishment and chagrin to find that the cost of the new sleeve had exceeded that of a new gown!

In his walks outside of the Palace, another Emperor bought an article of food for a few pence. The next time he had it in the Palace he asked what the dish cost and was told it was “four taels.” When he remonstrated, saying what he had paid for it outside of the Palace, his Master of the Household told him it was impossible to have it “inside” the Palace or on His Majesty’s table at any less than the sum of four taels. If His Majesty wished it for a few pence, His Majesty might buy it outside the Palace and bring it in himself for that sum, but no one else could bring it inside for the price it could be bought outside, as it had to go through so many official hands before it reached His Majesty’s table, that it actually cost the sum of four taels.

===Chinese===
世人皆说皇太后行事奢靡无度，于宫中诸务的打理亦然。至于宫中的奢靡，管理不善固然难免，浮华铺张也确实存在。凡是把大处产业的经管之权交给那些逐利之官与太监——北京宫中正是如此——弊端总会悄然滋生。

宫禁奢靡一事，早已为中国理财之士议论了数代，远在满族入主大位之前数百年，便已是陈言。历代皇上之中，也曾有几位欲以躬行节俭、私室省用来力挽此风，却终归徒劳。相传有一位皇上，因朝服袖口略有磨损，便唤内务府大臣来问新制一袭须费几何。及至知悉须银三千两，又念及此袍唯右袖磨损（因其挥腕秉笔之时多用右臂），便欲以身作则、倡导节俭，决定只另制一新袖，而不另办整袭朝服。他遂降旨照办，那件袍子被送出宫外，一去数月。及至送回，皇上何其惊诧懊恼——那一只新袖的工料银，竟超过了一袭新袍！

又有一位皇上，偶在宫外闲步时，以几个铜板买得一食物。下次在宫中进此物时，他便问这道菜所费几何，答曰“四两银子”。皇上不以为然，说自己在外头买时不过几文钱，内务府大臣却回奏：此物进了宫、上了皇上的御案，少于四两银子断断不能。皇上若要花几文钱来吃，自可出宫去买、自己带进来；可别人若要从外头带进来，断不能按外头的价钱——因为此物要送到皇上的御案，须经许多道衙门手，层层过手之后，工料自达四两之多。

===Original===
After several efforts of this kind at reducing the Palace expenses, even these wise and economical Emperors were obliged to give it up. If these Emperors of ancient times, when the Palace was conducted on more simple lines than it is today, were powerless to check extravagance in the Imperial household, how much more difficult must it be to do so now that the system has become petrified with age—especially for the Empress Dowager, who can never go outside and see things for herself! It is said that each egg at Their Majesties’ table costs three taels, but Palace reform, necessary as it is, must come from without, from the officials, and no private effort of Their Majesties can change things.

As for the Empress Dowager’s personal extravagance, aside from present-giving, I saw no evidence of it. Her wardrobe, in point of actual cost, aside from her jewels, would not be superior in price to that of the wives of some of our American millionaires; for the styles do not change in China, and furs and embroideries are handed down from generation to generation. Her jewels, even, are not more gorgeous or more numerous, though they are more unique, than those of any of the European sovereigns. She has an immense number of pearls—for the pearl is her favorite precious stone, besides being the jewel of the Dynasty—but she has no diamonds, no emeralds, and very few European precious stones. She has a quantity of fine jade jewels, but these, as well as pearls, are cheaper in China than elsewhere.

===Chinese===
几番这般裁减宫中开支的尝试之后，即便是这些圣明节俭的皇上，也只得作罢。古时的皇上，宫中规制比今日远为简朴，尚且无力遏止皇家奢靡之风；时至今日，积弊已历时久远、僵固如石，要加以整饬谈何容易——何况皇太后又永远不能走出宫门亲眼看一看外头的情状！据说二位陛下御案上的一枚鸡蛋便须三两银子；然宫禁的整顿纵然势所必行，也必须自外而入，须由朝臣来推动，二位陛下凭一己之力，断难有所更张。

至于皇太后本人的奢靡，除却赏赐馈赠之外，我并未见其端倪。她的衣饰，论其实值，除珠宝不计外，未必贵过我们美国若干富商之妻的衣饰；因为中国衣制不变，皮货与绣品皆可代代相传。她的珠宝，纵然较任何一位欧洲君主的更为独特，却未必更为华美或更多。她有无数的珍珠——因珍珠是她最钟爱的宝石，又是本朝的御用之宝——但她没有钻石，没有祖母绿，欧洲的宝石也极少。她有相当多的上等玉饰，而这些玉饰以及珍珠，在中国的价钱都比别处便宜。

===Original===
I saw several incidents which seemed to point rather to personal economy on Her Majesty’s part than to extravagance. While I was painting one of the portraits, she decided that the trimming on the gown must be changed. She had bolts of different kinds of ribbon brought in to select from and finally decided upon a certain piece. She called a maid to sew some around the neck. When I wanted to have this piece cut off, so that some might be sewed around the hem where it was also visible, she said the ribbon had better not be cut, for it was a “handsome piece,” and, if cut, it might spoil it for use in “trimming another gown.” These pieces of ribbon and embroidery come in lengths for one dress only. One day when she was drinking some fruit juice, her hand slipped on the polished jade bowl and some of it fell upon the front of her jacket. She was most annoyed, and after several ineffectual attempts of her own and the attendants to remove the spot, she said she had heard that the foreigners had some wonderful processes of cleaning and she must have them investigated, for it was too bad to have a thing spoiled by an accident of that kind when a good garment was rendered useless for anyone!

She had the good of China at heart and was really a patriot; in fact, I observed more patriotism, more National pride among the people I saw at Court, than I ever noticed elsewhere in China. I feel convinced the Empress Dowager has strong National feeling and really loves her country, and is as patriotic a Chinese as there is in China. When there were internal troubles, or exterior complications, she seemed to be really worried and to grieve, as if it were a personal thing. She made mistakes, of course, and grave ones, but when it is remembered that her knowledge of what takes place “outside,” comes entirely from the reports made to her, that she has no opportunity of seeing things for herself, it seems wonderful she does not make more.

===Chinese===
我倒见过几桩小事，与其说是显示皇太后奢靡，不如说是显示她躬行节俭。我绘其中一幅画像时，她忽然决定袍上的镶边要换。她命人将一匹匹各色缎带取来挑选，终于选定一段。她唤一名侍女来，叫她在领口处缝上一圈。我正想请人把这段缎带剪下，好也在袍下摆可见之处缝上一圈，她却说这缎带还是不剪为妙——这是一段“好料子”，若一剪，便怕毁了它，不能再用来“镶另一件袍子”。这一类缎带和绣品，每段的长短原只够一件衣裳之用。一日她饮果子露时，手在大玉碗光润的碗沿上一滑，露汁溅了一些在她的上衣前襟。她颇为懊恼，自己与侍女几番设法揩拭终归无效，她便说她曾听闻洋人有几样奇妙的洗涤之法，务必要叫人查究一番——因为区区一场意外竟毁了一件好衣裳，使它再不能为人所用，未免太可惜！

她心系中国之福，确是一位爱国之人；事实上，我在宫中所见之人，其爱国之心、民族之骄，远胜我在中国别处所见。我深信皇太后具有强烈的国族情怀，是真的热爱她的国家，其爱国之忱不逊于中国任何人。每逢内忧外患，她似乎都忧形于色，悲痛于心，仿佛是切身之事。她自然也有过失，且不乏大过；但若念及她对外头“外面”所发生之事的所知，全凭奏报而来，并无机会亲眼目击，则她能不犯更多错，已可谓难得。

===Original===
Last winter a new scheme of taxation, by which the revenue would be largely increased and which taxation would be scarcely felt by the people, was presented to Their Majesties for consideration. Her Majesty soon grasped the entire scope of the scheme and thought it good and feasible; but though the payment of the foreign indemnity made it imperative to increase the revenue by every means possible, she hesitated over the inauguration of this new scheme, fearing it might give the officials a new opportunity to oppress the common people, for it is not the laws that oppress the people in China. This is done by the officials who enforce them. She evidently realized this power that the officials have of squeezing the people, and she wished to be assured of the manner in which this taxation would be enforced before she gave her consent to the scheme. At the first presentation to her of this plan of taxation, she repeated several times, “I fear it may harass the people; we cannot harass the people; they have enough burdens to bear.” She was not so particular about not harassing the Officials, for they were called upon all over China to make great contributions to the Imperial Treasury for the purpose of assisting in paying the foreign indemnity.

===Chinese===
去冬，一项新的税制方案呈交二位陛下裁度——此案可令国库岁入大增，而百姓所负担的税额几乎可忽略不计。皇太后很快便领会了全案大意，认为此举既好且可行；然而，虽偿付外债赔款使得必须千方百计增加岁入，她对此项新制之施行却犹豫不决，唯恐由此给朝臣一项新的鱼肉小民之机——因为在中国，压迫百姓的并非律令本身，律令之所以成为压迫，乃在于执行律令之官。她显然洞晓官吏这等盘剥百姓的权柄，故而未肯遽然允准，必欲先确知此税将如何征收，方才首肯。此税案初次呈进之时，她反复言道：“我怕此举会扰民；我们不可扰民；百姓所负已多矣。”她对于不扰官员则并不那么在意，因为为助偿对外赔款，全国上下官员皆奉命向内帑捐输巨款。

===Original===
Notwithstanding her penetration of character, her naturally good judgment, she made mistakes in her appreciation of those who surrounded her; but this was not strange, for she had almost no opportunity of seeing them in their true light. She was a good physiognomist, but one cannot always trust to physiognomy. She was in the habit of giving all who surrounded her a certain amount of latitude, until they came to rely on her favor and revealed themselves in their true light to her. Then she would quickly suppress them or cast them aside. This often seemed cruel and heartless. She sometimes would take another’s estimate of a character which she had favorably judged, for, of course, there is a great deal of jealousy and intrigue among her entourage, and she was influenced by reports that she heard; for she was obliged, in order to form an opinion, to listen to the gossip of the Palace. Her own penetration, however, would generally come to her aid and, in the end, her judgment would right itself.

She had strong prejudices, and often allowed herself to be deceived by the favorites to whom she had given her confidence. After several preliminary trials of their character, and when she thought she had arrived at a proper estimate of it, she was an easy victim. These favorites could then act with impunity, and she was sometimes made the dupe of their schemes. Thus Ministers, courtiers, friends, and attendants, who had once thoroughly established their positions with her, could often get the advantage of her and impose upon her natural acumen.

===Chinese===
尽管她识人极明，天性又善于判断，但对于身边之人，她的评价也偶有失准；但这并不足为怪，因为她几乎全无机会得见这些人的真面目。她善于相人，然相术之道，未可全恃。她惯于给身边之人留出一定的余地，直待他们恃宠而骄，将真面目显露于她眼前，她便立予裁抑，或弃之如敝屣。此举每每显得无情寡恩。她有时也会采纳旁人对她素所看好之人的评价——因为她的近侍之间，原也多有嫉妒倾轧——她难免为流言所动；因为要形成见地，她不得不倾听宫中的闲言碎语。然而她的天生的明察每每终能助她一臂，到头来她的判断终会归于平正。

她有强烈的偏私之好，常为自己所宠信之人所欺。她几番试过这些人的性情，自以为已得其真，便易为人所乘。这等宠臣遂得以肆意妄为，她有时竟成了他们奸计的傀儡。于是，那些一旦在她面前站稳脚跟的朝臣、近侍、故交与随从，每每能占到她的上风，蒙蔽她那与生俱来的明察。

===Original===
She could be most sarcastic, sometimes cruelly so, but I generally found there was some reason for her sarcasm. She was very impulsive and had her share of temper, but there was never any unladylike display of it. When she was angry her voice was never raised; it simply lost its silvery sweetness and took the quality of some ordinary metal, and she was always quiet and well-bred.

From what I saw of the Empress Dowager, it seemed to me she would not brook interference in the accomplishment of a design she had set her heart upon—that she would not hesitate even at crushing an individual who stood in the way of the realization of some plan she had fixed upon. But her judgment was so good, she did not decide upon a thing unless she felt it was absolutely imperative to carry it out.

===Chinese===
她言辞可以极尽锋利，有时甚至近乎刻薄，但我每每发觉她的讥诮必有所因。她性急，也自有其脾气，但从未有过失了贵妇身份的发作。她恼怒之时从不提高嗓门，只不过声音失却了那银铃似的甜美，化作一种寻常金属般的质地——她始终是沉静的、有教养的。

就我所见，皇太后既已立意成一事，便断不容人横加干涉——凡阻挠其成事者，她即便加以摧折也在所不惜。然她的判断力极高，凡未觉其势在必行，便不下决断。

===Original===
As for tact and social savoir, she is remarkable. I never knew anyone to possess these qualities to a greater degree. At her first Audience to foreigners, Sir Claude MacDonald, in reporting it, spoke of the Empress Dowager as “a kind and courteous hostess, who displayed both the tact and softness of a womanly disposition.” Lady Susan Townley says of her: “Where has she learned the ease and dignity with which she receives her European guests?” These opinions of her social tact, so far as I could learn, are held by all the members of the Foreign Legations in Peking.

When the young Prince Adalbert of Prussia was received in special Audience by Their Majesties, on his visit to Peking, he was accompanied not only by the German Minister and his staff, but by a number of officers as his personal escort. This made an unusually large number of presentations necessary. I have been told that at the Audiences of the Diplomatic Corps, where only gentlemen were present, the Empress Dowager had a sort of shyness and did not show the same ease of manner as when she received the ladies. But at this Audience of the young Prince she became interested in talking with him, and I heard one of the gentlemen who was present say it was the first time he had seen Her Majesty thoroughly at ease at one of the Audiences to the Diplomatic Corps, and that on that day she was perfectly charming, seeming to take the liveliest interest in questioning the young Prince and conversing with him in a motherly way, and that he then realized to its full extent her wonderful charm and her great social instinct.

===Chinese===
至于其机敏与交际之风度，她可谓卓绝。我所识之人中，未有一人能逾其上。她初次接见洋人时，麦克唐纳爵士（Sir Claude MacDonald）在奏报中称皇太后为“一位和蔼有礼的女主人，既有机敏，又有女性气质的温柔”。汤利夫人（Lady Susan Townley）论及她时说道：“她款待欧洲宾客时那份从容端严，究竟从何习得？”据我所知，北京各国公使馆中人，对她这份交际机敏的评价，皆出一辙。

普鲁士年轻的阿达尔贝特亲王（Prince Adalbert）访京时，二位陛下特予觐见。陪同他的不仅有德国公使及其随员，还有若干武官作为他的随扈。这使得引见的数目之多异乎寻常。我听说，在各国公使团觐见之时——因到者皆为男宾——皇太后略显羞怯，举止不如接见女宾时那般自如。但在接见这位年轻亲王的觐见上，她与他谈得颇有兴致；我听当日一位在场的男宾说，这是他头一回在公使团觐见中见皇太后如此泰然自若，那一日她魅力横溢，仿佛兴致勃勃地一一诘问年轻亲王，以慈母之态与他交谈；他这才彻底领略了她那奇异的魅力与非凡的社交天性。

===Original===
I have heard it said that the Empress Dowager puts all this charm on for these occasions; that she is a consummate actress, but during the whole time I was in the Palace I never saw her other than the charming hostess, considerate of the comfort of those who surround her and readily sympathizing with sorrow, and I have seen her under all circumstances, at Audiences and in private, in anxiety and sorrow and in joy. She was too great a lover of Nature in all its phases to be cruel and heartless, and I am convinced she is really genuinely kind. She apparently greatly admired intelligence, and goodness always seemed to appeal to her. She was ever a fascinating study, and her magnetic personality full of charm. I found her thoroughly human and perfectly womanly.

===Chinese===
我曾听人说，皇太后这一切魅力都是为此等场合装出来的，说她是个绝顶的戏子。但我在宫中的整个时日里，从未见她不是那位动人的女主人——体恤身边之人的安适，对悲伤者易于生出同情。我在种种场合见过她：觐见时与私下里，忧愁时与喜乐时。她如此深地热爱自然万象，断不会残忍无情；我深信她实是发自真心地仁厚。她似乎甚赏聪慧，善良每每令她动容。她永远是个令人着迷的研究对象，她那富于魅力的个性引人入胜。我觉得她彻头彻尾地富有人情味，且十足地具有女性之柔情。

## XVIII The Festival of the Harvest Moon—Work on the Portrait / 第十八章 中秋节——画像的工作

===Original===
We think the Chinese so unemotional, so little given to pleasure or amusement; but there are more popular festivals in China, indulged in by all classes of people, than in any country in the world, except perhaps Japan. The people, from the highest to the lowest, enter into these celebrations with whole-souled earnestness and real enjoyment, and all the popular festivals, as well as the religious ceremonies, are celebrated in the Palace with apparently the same zest as among the people.

===Chinese===
我们总以为中国人感情内敛，鲜于寻欢作乐；然而中国民间流行的节庆，举国上下各阶层皆沉醉其中，其数目之多，恐为世界之冠，或许唯独日本可与之一较。上自最高贵者，下至最卑贱者，人人都怀着一片赤诚与真切的欢愉投入这些庆典；而一切民间的节日以及宗教的仪典，在宫廷之中也都举行得热闹非凡，其兴致之浓，看上去与民间并无二致。

===Original===
The Mid-Autumn Festival, popularly known as the Festival of the Harvest Moon, which is at its full at the time of the celebration, was, of course, observed with due ceremony at the Palace. For these festivals there are always representations at the Palace Theater, and one of the plays on such days is the dramatization of the Legend of the Festival. The legend of the Harvest Moon is this: One day an Emperor received the visit of a fairy. When she left she gave the Emperor an herb, saying, should he eat it, he would be endowed with Immortality. The Emperor was called out, soon after the fairy's visit, and forgot the gift for a time, and the herb lay upon his table. During his absence from the Throne-room, a young handmaiden entered and, seeing the root on the table, with childish curiosity, tasted it, and, finding it good, ate the whole of it. When the Emperor again thought of his precious gift from the fairy, he hastened back to the Throne-room, to remove it from the table where he had left it. What was his horror to find it gone! Learning that the little handmaid was the only person who had been in the Throne-room, he called her up to find out what she had done with it. When he found she had eaten it, he ordered her killed, that he might thus again obtain the herb. Before the eunuchs could accomplish their task, the charm began to work, and she felt the wings of Immortality; and borne up by them, she flew to the skies and took refuge in the Moon, where she still lives with the pet white rabbit she had in her arms at the time she flew away from the earth. She is now an Immortal, and in the Moon she compounds the Elixir of Immortality. The rabbit, also, shares her immortality, and ever watches at the lunar threshold.

===Chinese===
中秋节，民间俗称「中秋节」，届时正值明月圆满，宫中自然也依礼隆重地加以庆贺。每逢这些节庆，宫中的戏台上总有演出，而这一日的戏目之一，便是将本节的传说编成戏文搬演。中秋的传说原是这样的：一日，有位皇上得了一位仙女的造访。仙女临去之时，赐给皇上一株仙草，说道：他若食之，便可得不死之身。仙女去后不久，皇上因事被请出，一时竟将这份赏赐忘在脑后，那株仙草便搁在案上。趁他离了宝座殿，一名年幼的宫女走了进来，看见案上那株草根，出于孩童的好奇，便尝了一尝，觉得滋味甚佳，索性把它整个儿吃了下去。待皇上忽然又想起仙女所赐的这件珍宝，急忙赶回宝座殿，欲将它从搁置之处取回。谁知一瞧——仙草竟已不翼而飞！他不禁大惊失色。得知那小宫女是唯一进过宝座殿的人，便将她唤来，追问她把仙草弄到何处去了。及至查知她竟已将仙草吞下，便下令将她处死，好借此再把仙草取回。太监们尚未来得及动手，那灵药已开始发作，她已觉出长生之翼的轻拂；被那双翅膀托举着，她翩然飞升，逃到月亮之上，至今仍住在那里，怀中还抱着她离开尘世时所携的那只白兔宠物。她如今已是仙人，在月宫之中调配长生不老的金丹。那只兔子也分享了她的长生，永远在月宫门前守望。

===Original===
The drama, with this little maiden as heroine, was played by Her Majesty's actors on the day of the Moon Festival, and the finale of the plays that day was one of the most beautiful spectacular tableaux I have ever seen. The Chinese obtain most artistic effects in their illuminations, and by the most simple means. The stage represented a lake covered with luminous lotus, with the full moon floating above. Throned on a gigantic lotus flower in the center of the lake sat an immense, golden Buddha, impassible and serene, ingeniously illuminated lotus flowers and luminous birds, emblems of Immortality, hovered over the lake, and the whole tableau was supposed to represent Nirvana, when the soul is absorbed into Nature and forms a part of it. It was really fairy-like.

===Chinese===
中秋这日，皇太后的伶人们便把以这位小少女为主角的戏文搬上了台。当日诸戏的收场，是我平生所见最为华美的盛景之一。中国人凭着最简便的法子，便能在灯火之术中取得最具艺术意味的效果。台上所布置的，乃是一片缀满明灯的荷花池，一轮满月悬浮其上。湖心一朵硕大的莲花之上，端坐着一尊巨大的金佛，神色恬淡、泰然不动；巧夺天工的灯荷与发光的飞鸟——长生不老的象征——盘旋于湖面之上，整幅画面意在描绘涅槃之境：灵魂消融于自然之中，与之合而为一。那景象当真是如入仙境。

===Original===
The Ladies dined in Her Majesty's loge, and this beautiful, illuminated tableau was scarcely finished before we were obliged to hurry away to join Their Majesties, who had already started for the gardens where the ceremony was to take place. The procession, with the Emperor and Empress Dowager and Ladies in full dress, as usual for a ceremony, was accompanied by hundreds of lantern-bearing eunuchs. It wound, in and out, through the verandahed corridors and the paths of the garden like some great glowworm, until it came to the marble terrace beneath the Temple of the Ten Thousand Buddhas, on the great terrace over the lake.

===Chinese===
命妇们在皇太后的包厢里用了膳。这一幅灯火辉煌的美丽画面方才落幕，我们便不得不匆匆赶去与皇上、皇太后会合——他们一行已然动身，往举行典礼的花园去了。这一支队伍，皇上、皇太后与命妇们都如典礼之时一律身着盛装，由数百名执灯的太监簇随。队伍在游廊与园中小径之间逶迤穿行，宛若一只硕大的萤火虫，最终来到万佛寺之下、俯瞰昆明湖的大理石台榭之上。

===Original===
Here, in an open space bathed in the rays of the softly glowing moon, with the glory of the setting sun still in the west, in front of the great Stone Pai-lou stood a beautifully illuminated floral *pai-lou* and an altar decorated with the usual pyramids of fruits, floral offerings, and flagons of wine. The *pai-lou* to the Moon was entirely of chrysanthemums, with an inscription "To the Glory of the Chaste and Pure Celestial Orb" in white blooms, like gleaming stars, across the top.

===Chinese===
就在这一片被柔和的月光沐浴着的空地上，西方天际仍留着落日的余辉。那座巍峨的石牌楼之前，立着一座灯火装饰得极美的鲜花牌楼，并有一张供案，上面照例堆叠着塔状的果品、鲜花供奉以及一壶壶的美酒。这座祭月的牌楼全以菊花扎成，上端以白色花朵缀成「致敬于皎洁纯净的天球」的字样，宛如闪烁的星辰。

===Original===
Their Majesties first made the bows and prostrations to the Moon, and placed floral offerings on the altar. Then the young Empress and Ladies did likewise, while the eunuchs recited a poem in melodious and rhythmic cadence. The Chinese "recitative" is very musical, much more so, to the foreign ear, than their music. This poem to the Moon was recited by two voices in alternating rhythm with wonderful effect. When the recitation was finished, an "auto da fe" was made of the offerings, to which were added sticks of sweet incense and paper cut in curious designs. Over all was poured some of the inflammable wine from the flagons on the altar, and the flames leaped high above the huge incense-burner that stood on a great bronze tripod in the center of the moonlit terrace. It was a wonderfully picturesque sight—the brilliant circle of splendidly gowned Ladies, with the Emperor and Empress Dowager in their midst, around the flaming censer, whose leaping flames glinted and glowed upon the jewels and gold embroidery of their costumes. The lantern-bearing eunuchs formed a faintly glowing circle around this shining center; and over the whole fantastic picture the brilliant Harvest Moon shone with unwonted splendor, as if to show itself worthy of the obeisances it had just received from this brilliant group.

===Chinese===
皇上与皇太后先向月亮行礼叩拜，将鲜花供奉在供案之上。随后皇后与命妇们也照样行礼，太监们则以悠扬而富有节奏的声调吟诵了一首诗。中国人的「吟诵」颇有乐感，在外国人的耳中听来，远比他们的乐曲还要动听。这首祭月之诗由两个嗓音以交替的节奏吟唱而出，效果绝佳。吟诵既毕，便将供品付诸一炬——「火刑」似地焚烧起来，又添上几支馨香的细烛与剪成种种奇异花样的纸片。坛上酒壶中的烈酒被浇淋其上，火苗便从那只巨大的香炉里腾空而起——那香炉立于月光皎洁的台榭正中一座大铜鼎之上。那真是一幅如画般的美景：盛装华服的命妇们围成一道明艳的圆圈，皇上与皇太后便在其间，围绕着那熊熊燃烧的香炉，跳动的火光映照在她们衣饰的珠宝与金绣之上，熠熠生辉。执灯的太监们围在外面，形成一个隐隐发光的光环，拥着这璀璨的中心；而在这整幅瑰丽奇幻的画面之上，那一轮明月以从未有过的辉煌照耀着，仿佛是在显示自己当之无愧，配得上这一群华彩照人之人刚刚向它行的礼。

===Original===
When the flames no longer leaped from the censer, when only the white smoke of the incense curled through the interstices of its cover, Their Majesties turned away, and the lantern-lit procession followed them to the banks of the lake, where the whole Palace fleet, brilliantly illuminated, lay moored beneath the marble terrace. The eunuchs, holding aloft their gleaming lanterns, stood along the terrace and knelt on the steps leading into the water, while Their Majesties descended them. On two of the boats, at either side of the Imperial barge, the eunuchs held their lanterns to form the characters "Peace" and "Prosperity." The waters of the lake were now glowing with the reflections of the myriad lanterns and dancing under their many-colored lights. A faint glow still illumined the western sky, while the reflection of the resplendent Moon gleamed like liquid diamonds across the lake! When we reached the Imperial landing-place, its great arc-lights on the two tall, painted poles sent their reflections shimmering, in long, wavy lines, far out into the lake, and almost rivaled in their splendor that of the celestial orb itself.

===Chinese===
待香炉中再无火苗腾起，只剩袅袅的白烟从炉盖的缝隙间缭绕而出时，皇上与皇太后便转身离去，灯火通明的队伍随他们来到湖畔。宫中的船队都已张灯结彩，停泊在大理石台榭之下。太监们高举着熠熠生辉的灯笼，沿岸而立，又跪在通往水中的石阶两侧，皇上与皇太后便从这石阶上走下去。御舟两侧各有两只船上，太监们以手中的灯笼排出「太平」与「丰收」的字样。此时湖面被万千灯火的倒影映得波光粼粼，在五彩的灯光之下荡漾跳荡。西方天际仍有淡淡的一抹余晖，而那一轮皓月的倒影，则如流动的钻石般在湖面闪烁！我们抵达御码头之时，两根高高的彩绘杆上巨大的弧光灯将它们的倒影化作长长的、波动的光带，远远地延伸到湖心，其辉煌壮丽，几乎可与那天上的明月一较高下。

===Original===
Although I took part in all these Palace festivals, my work on the portrait was advancing, but I longed for more opportunity to quietly study it and for a little more freedom in working. I felt I needed more time also for my painting. I ardently desired to be able to work some when Her Majesty did not pose, and I finally decided to ask her to allow me to remain at my painting when she and the Ladies went for their morning walks after a short sitting. It was a deprivation for me to give up even one of these delightful walks, when I saw such a charming side of the Empress Dowager's character, but I felt it must be done. She reluctantly consented to excuse me on a few occasions, but she seemed to feel it was not hospitable on her part to leave me alone; and when she did so she would remain out a shorter time than usual. She seemed so concerned at my working while the others enjoyed themselves, that I soon ceased to ask to be left at work; I could only try to make the best of the time I had at my disposal.

===Chinese===
尽管我参与了宫中这所有的节庆，画像的工作仍在推进，但我渴望有更多的机会静心研习它，也渴望在作画时能多一分自由。我觉得自己的画也需要更多的时间。我热切地盼望能在皇太后不坐班的时候也做些工作，终于决定求她恩准：在她与命妇们短暂坐班之后晨出散步时，允许我留在画前。要放弃哪怕一次这样怡人的散步，于我而言是一种损失——因为在那些散步中，我得以见到皇太后性情中如此迷人的一面；然而我感到这事非做不可。她勉强应允了几次破例准我不去，可她似乎觉得把我一个人撇下未免有失地主之谊；每逢这种时候，她在外头逗留的时间总比往常短些。她对我趁别人游乐之时独自工作这件事，似是十分挂怀，我便很快不再请求留下作画了；我只能尽量善用手头所有的时间。

===Original===
My desire to have more time for my painting and more opportunity for studying the work was not the only cloud in the heaven of these delightful days. As the portrait progressed I found myself constantly running up against Chinese conventionalities as to the way it was done. They wished so much detail and no shadow. Had Her Majesty been alone to be considered, she was artistic and progressive enough to have, in the end, allowed me more liberty; but she, also, was obliged to conform to tradition, and no fantasy could be indulged in painting the portrait of a Celestial Majesty. It was necessary to conform to rigid conventions.

===Chinese===
我之渴求有更多的时间作画、更多的机会研习这幅作品，并非这些愉快时日的晴空之中仅有的一片阴翳。随着画像的推进，我发觉自己在作法上不断地与中国人的成规发生抵触。他们要的是详尽的细部，却不要一丝阴影。若只需顾及皇太后一人，她既有艺术的眼光、又足够开通，终究是会容我更多自由的；然而她也不得不遵从传统，而给天朝皇上画像，是容不得半点奇思异想的。必须恪守那些森严的成规。

===Original===
I had such a fine opportunity to do something really picturesque in painting this great Empress and most interesting woman, and I found I was to be bound down by the iron fetters of Chinese tradition! I could neither choose an accessory, nor even arrange a fold according to the lines of the composition. I was obliged to follow, in every detail, centuries-old conventions. There could be no shadows and very little perspective, and everything must be painted in such full light as to lose all relief and picturesque effect. When I saw I must represent Her Majesty in such a conventional way as to make her unusually attractive personality banal, I was no longer filled with the ardent enthusiasm for my work with which I had begun it, and I had many a heartache and much inward rebellion before I settled down to the inevitable.

===Chinese===
为这位了不起的皇后、这位极其有趣的女人画像，原是做一番真正富于画意之作的绝好机会，而我却发现自己竟要被中国传统的铁镣所束缚！我既不能挑选一件饰物，甚至也不能依照构图的需要去安排一道衣褶。每一处细节，我都不得不遵从那数百年来的成规。画中不能有阴影，透视也几近于无，一切都必须在那种铺天盖地的强光之下画出，以至于失去了一切凹凸之感与画意之趣。当我意识到自己必须以这样刻板的方式去描摹皇太后，把她那异常动人的人格画得平庸乏味，我再也找不回动笔之初那股对工作炽热的激情了；在安于这无可奈何的命运之前，我不知暗自伤过多少次心，又起过多少回抵触之念。

===Original===
The Empress Dowager, however, knew nothing of my discouragement, and seemed perfectly contented with the progress of the portrait then on hand—so pleased, in fact, she asked me if I would not like Mrs. Conger to come and see it. I, of course, replied that I would, and an invitation was accordingly sent, through the Foreign Office, inviting Mrs. Conger to come to see the portrait.

===Chinese===
然而皇太后对我这份灰心丧气一无所知，对当时手头这幅画像的进展似乎十分满意——她竟满心欢喜，问我可愿请康格夫人来看看。我自然答说愿意，于是便经由外务部发出了一份邀请，请康格夫人前来看画。

===Original===
As Her Majesty was to receive her in the Throne-room where I painted, it was decided I could not work on that day. I fully expected the portrait would be exhibited in the Throne-room, the only place where it had a proper light; but, to my disappointment, Mrs. Conger was asked to look at it in the small room where it was kept when I was not working on it. When we went in, the Chief Eunuch ceremoniously removed the yellow covering over the "Sacred Picture," which hung flat against the wall in a very bad light, with annoying reflections. The small room was also uncomfortably crowded with Her Majesty and suite, so that it was impossible to see the whole canvas at once. Mrs. Conger was, however, so pleased with the likeness and lifelike expression in the eyes, the upper part of the picture being in a fairly good light, that the comment stopped here.

===Chinese===
因皇太后要在她接见康格夫人的那间宝座殿——也就是我作画之处——见她，这一日我自然不能动笔了。我原以为画像会陈设在宝座殿中供人观赏，唯有那里才有适宜的光线；然而令我失望的是，康格夫人却被请到我不作画时存放画像的那间小屋里去观看。我们走进去时，总管太监郑重其事地揭去了「圣容」上的黄色帷盖——那画像平平地贴墙挂着，光线甚暗，又有恼人的反光。小屋里还挤着皇太后和随从，十分逼仄，根本无法一眼看全整幅画布。幸而康格夫人对画像的形神毕肖、尤其是双目中栩栩如生的神采十分赞赏——画的上半部光线尚可——她的评赞也就到此为止。

===Original===
This first portrait represented the Empress Dowager sitting on one of her favorite Cantonese carved Thrones. The figure was life-size. In one hand she held a flower, and the other lay over a yellow cushion. The tip of one small embroidered shoe, with its jeweled, white kid sole resting on a dragon footstool, showed under the hem of her gown. The head was a three-quarters view, with the eyes looking at the observer. A jardinière, with her favorite orchid, stood behind the Throne at the right. It was painted in full light. The canvas was four by six feet in size; and there was thus no place for any of the emblems or insignia of Her Majesty's rank, save that she was clothed in her official costume of Imperial yellow.

===Chinese===
这第一幅画像，画的是皇太后端坐于她心爱的一张粤绣雕花御座之上。人像与真人等大。她一手拈花，另一手搭在一方黄色引枕之上。绣花小鞋的鞋尖——那白色小羊皮的鞋底缀着珠宝，搁在一只龙纹脚踏上——自衣裾之下微微露出。头部取四分之三侧面，双目直视观者。御座之后偏右处，立着一只花盆，栽着她所钟爱的兰花。整幅画都在强光之下绘成。画布为四英尺乘六英尺；因此，除了她那一身明黄色的礼袍之外，画中竟无一处可容皇太后品级的任何象征或徽记。

===Original===
This was the conventional reality, and I had dreamed of painting Her Majesty in one of her Buddha-like poses, sitting erect upon an antique Throne of the Dynasty, with one beautifully rounded arm and exquisitely shaped hand resting on its high side, contrasting in their grace with its severe lines. I should have exaggerated her small stature by placing her upon the largest of these Dynastic Thrones. Her wonderfully magnetic personality alone should have dominated. At the left of the Throne, I should have placed one of those huge Palace braziers, its blue flames leaping into the air, their glow glinting here and there upon her jewels and the rich folds of her drapery; the whole enveloped in the soft azure smoke of incense, rising from splendid antique bronze censers. Across the base of the picture, under her feet, should have writhed and sprawled the rampant double dragon.
