The Eternal Feminine, with its eternal enigma shining from her inscrutable eyes, should have pierced, with almost cruel penetration, the mystery of her surroundings. Her face should have shone out of this dim interior, as her personality does above her real environment. I should have tried to show all the force and strength of her nature in that characteristic face, exaggerating every feature of it, rather than toning down one line.

===Chinese===
这便是那刻板的事实，而我却曾梦想着把皇太后画成她那一种宛若佛陀的姿态——笔直地端坐于一张古朝御座之上，一只丰润圆润的手臂、一只精巧成形的手，搭在那高高的靠背之上，以其婀娜与御座那凝重的线条恰成对照。我本该把她安置在最大的一张御朝宝座之上，以反衬出她身形的纤小。唯有她那不可思议、令人倾倒的人格，应当独占画面。御座之左，我本该安置一只宫中的大火盆，蓝色的火焰腾空而起，火光不时地映在她的珠翠与衣褶丰厚的缎面之上；整幅画笼罩在柔和的青蓝色香烟之中，那烟从几只华美的古铜香炉里袅袅升起。画幅的下端，在她足下，本该有腾跃的蟠龙蜿蜒张牙。永恒的女性，连同她那双莫测的眼中闪射出的永恒之谜，本该以近乎冷酷的洞察之力，刺穿她周围一切的神祕。她的容颜本该从这幽暗的内室中熠熠生辉，一如她的人格在其真实的处境之上焕发光彩。我本该竭力在那张富有特征的面容上，展现出她天性中全部的力量与刚毅，把它每一处五官都加以夸张，而不是削弱一笔一画。

===Original===
With all these possibilities that the Empress Dowager's person and surroundings would suggest to the most unimaginative of artists, and with the conventional traditions, which I was obliged to follow, no wonder I became discouraged. But I had always the solace of her personality—the fascinating study of herself to delight and console me. New phases of her character and personality were constantly opening out before me. She dominates everything and everybody in the Palace, and is far and away, the most interesting personality there, not because she is the first figure at the Court, but because she is really the most interesting one, and she would be that in any position. No wonder that when she smiles the Court is gay—her smile is so entrancing. No wonder that when she frowns the Court trembles, for she excites sympathy in all her moods.

===Chinese===
皇太后其人其境所能给予最缺乏想象力的画家的这一切可能性，加上我不得不遵从的那些成规旧法，难怪我会灰心丧气了。然而我始终有她的人格作为慰藉——研究她本人这件引人入胜的事，既令我喜悦，又给我以慰藉。她的性情与人格，不断地在我眼前展现出新的面目。她主宰着宫中的一切事物和一切人，远远地胜过他人，是宫中最有趣味的人物——这并非因为她是朝中位居首席之人，而是因为她实在是其中最有趣味的一个，无论置于何种地位，她都会是如此。难怪她一笑，宫中便一片欢悦——她的笑容是如此迷人。难怪她一蹙眉，宫中便为之战栗，因为她在每一种心境之下都能牵动人心。

## XVI The Great Audience Hall—Some Official Customs / 十六 大朝殿——若干官场礼制

===Original===
When His Majesty the Emperor reigned alone, he was in the habit of holding his Audiences as early as three o'clock a.m. It is said, however, that this custom was owing as much to his personal shyness as to his love of early rising, for at these Audiences he would allow but two candles on the Throne-table in front of him, and the Great Hall was lighted elsewhere only by the beautiful Chinese lanterns, which shine with but a dim brilliancy and are not very effective as lights. Thus his face could not be seen if an official should so forget the Proprieties as to raise his eyes to the Imperial Person.

===Chinese===
皇上独自秉政之时，惯于清晨三点钟便开觐见。然而据说这一惯例，既因其喜早起，也因其天性腼腆——盖每逢此类觐见，御案上他面前只许燃两支蜡烛，大殿其余各处则只悬那些华美的中国宫灯照明；宫灯虽美，光辉却甚幽暗，作为照明实在无甚效用。如此一来，若有官员竟忘了礼数，抬眼偷觑天颜，也看不清圣容。

===Original===
Their Majesties' Audiences are held in the Great Audience Hall, a detached building apart and quite distinct from all the other buildings of the Palace enclosure. The inscription over its great doors points out that it is the "Hall where Industry is to be applied to State Affairs." In all the Palaces the Audience Hall is nearest the outside walls and entrances, so that the officials who are privileged to have Audiences must only pass through the outer courts to reach the hall—Their Majesties' Palaces with their private apartments being at some distance beyond. At the Winter Palace, where there are so many walls within walls, each of Their Majesties' Palaces is surrounded with walls, and the Audience Hall is also in a walled-in enclosure near one of the Great Gates, but at the Summer Palace there are no walls except the exterior ones!

===Chinese===
二位陛下的觐见，在大朝殿中举行。这是一座独立的殿宇，与宫苑之内其余所有殿宇截然分开，判然有别。其大门之上的匾额，点明此乃「勤政亲贤之殿」——即以勤勉之心料理国政的殿宇。在所有宫苑里，觐见殿都最靠近外墙和宫门，使有资格觐见的官员只须穿过外朝的几重院落便可到达——二位陛下的寝宫及内苑则在更深处，相隔尚有一段距离。紫禁城里墙内有墙，重重叠叠，二位陛下的寝宫各为一圈围墙所绕，而大朝殿亦在一处围合的院落之中，靠近一座大门；唯独颐和园，除了外墙之外，再无别的围墙！

===Original===
The interior of the Audience Hall, at the Summer Palace, is not by any means bare or austere. It is furnished in the same style as the Throne-rooms, with splendid ornaments, curios, tea-tables and chairs, and, curious anachronism, there are here three pianos! The walls are hung with ornamental scrolls, as well as with those bearing some gigantic character traced by an Emperor's hand or some condensed bit of philosophy of the Sages. One of these scrolls has an admonition to the Emperor to remember that "he is responsible to Heaven for the happiness and prosperity of his people."

===Chinese===
颐和园里这座觐见殿的内里陈设，绝非素朴简陋。它与宝座殿的陈设同一气派，满是华美的摆设、古玩、茶几和坐椅——还有一处古怪的错位：殿中竟摆着三架钢琴！四壁悬着各色挂轴，既有供赏玩的画轴，也有写着一个巨大字样的御笔之书，或是圣贤精炼的格言。其中一帧便是告诫皇上，须切记「他须为万民的安乐与福祉，向皇天负责」。

===Original===
There is a great dais in the center of the hall, on which stands the Throne, with its table, behind which is the three-, five-, or seven-leaved screen. The ancient dais was lower than those now used, and the antique Throne, with its capacious size and cushions, was more like a lounge than the modern Throne. This seems to indicate that the administration of justice by the Emperor was in ancient times less formal and more patriarchal than today. In former times the Emperor could lounge upon his Throne at his ease when seeing his Ministers, and they could approach nearer the Sacred Person, as the dais was not so large nor so high as that in use today.

===Chinese===
大殿正中设一座高大的宝座台基，台上便是宝座，前面摆着御案，后面立着一架三扇、五扇或七扇的屏风。古时的台基比今日所用的为低，古时的宝座，以其宽大的形制和厚厚的软垫，更近乎一张卧榻，而不像今日的宝座。这似乎表明：古时天子临朝听政，礼数不似今日这般繁文缛节，而更近于家长之风。从前皇上接见大臣时，可自在地斜倚在宝座之上；而大臣们也可更接近圣驾，因为那时的台基并不如今日这般高大。

===Original===
Heads of departments and Princes with honorary official positions have Audiences on certain days of the month, to report upon affairs of their Boards or to pay their respects to His Majesty. Every day Their Majesties hold Audience and see the Prime Minister and Grand Secretary, and there are frequent meetings of the Grand Council. The Prime Minister, Prince Ching, has the last Audience of the day, and business reported on during the other Audiences is then discussed.

===Chinese===
各部堂官及有荣誉官职的王公，于每月指定的日子觐见，奏报本部事务，或向皇上请安。二位陛下每日皆御觐见，接见首相与大学士，军机处亦频繁开会。庆亲王（Prince Ching）身任首相，每日最后一次觐见便是他的，其余各次觐见所奏之事，皆在此次一并面议。

===Original===
All telegrams and despatches go to their respective Boards, and are, except in cases of extreme gravity, only reported to Their Majesties at the Audiences. After eleven all State business is supposed to be finished by Their Majesties. They are then free from State worries and cares until the following day. During the times of the rebellion in the Province of Kwang-Si, when the Russian evacuation of Manchuria was expected, and at the time of outbreak of hostilities in Manchuria (the three grave events occurring during my stay in the Palace), telegrams and despatches were constantly being sent to Her Majesty out of Audience hours. They were brought to her Throne-room, and sometimes even during her walks in the gardens they would be handed her. These despatches were sent over to the Palace from the Wai-Wu-Pu on their arrival. Of course, it was by Her Majesty's express command that her privacy was thus infringed upon. No official would otherwise have dared transgress the prescribed rules. The despatches were received at the entrance of the Palace by the eunuch whose province it was. He placed them in the yellow-covered, silken-lined box, in which they were presented to Her Majesty on bended knees.

===Chinese===
一切电报公文，皆送往各部衙门；除非事关极其重大，否则只在觐见之时奏闻二位陛下。过了十一点钟，二位陛下的国事便算全数办毕。此后直至次日，方才再理国政之忧。然而广西叛乱之际、俄国将从满洲撤兵之说纷传之时，以及满洲战事爆发之时（这三桩大事皆发生在我留居宫中的那段时间里），电报与公文常常在觐见时辰之外，接连送呈皇太后。它们被送到她的宝座殿，有时甚至在她园中散步时，也直接呈到她手中。这些公文一到，便由外务部（Wai-Wu-Pu）送进宫来。自然，皇太后的私憩如此被扰，全出于她本人的特旨。若非如此，无一名官员敢于逾越既定的规矩。这些公文由专司此事的太监在宫门接收，放进一只黄绫裹面、丝缎为里的匣子里，再以这匣子盛着，跪呈皇太后。

===Original===
In front of the Throne dais, during the hours of Audience, there are five cushions placed on the floor for the members of the Grand Council to kneel upon when they are memorializing Their Majesties. The Prime Minister's cushion is nearest the Throne. A cushion to kneel upon is a privilege only granted members of the Grand Council. Any other official, when making communications to Their Majesties, must kneel upon the bare marble floor, and must kneel beyond the space occupied by these five cushions. He is thus placed at a disadvantage. The distance at which he is from Their Majesties may prevent his hearing some of their words, especially the Emperor's, whose voice is very low and without any carrying quality. The official may overcome this difficulty and shorten the distance by paying the eunuch who conducts him to the Audience Hall, to remove some of the cushions, so that he may kneel nearer the dais. The Prime Minister's and Grand Secretary's cushions may on no condition be removed, but the other three are subject to the will of the introducing eunuch. If this latter be sufficiently paid, and there is a fixed price for each cushion, he will remove the three of the lower members of the Cabinet.

===Chinese===
觐见之时，宝座台基前的地上铺着五只拜垫，是供军机大臣们向二位陛下面奏时跪用的。首相的拜垫离宝座最近。跪用拜垫，是唯有军机大臣方得享的特权。其余任何官员，向二位陛下奏事时，都必须跪在光秃秃的大理石地面上，且必须跪在这五只拜垫所占的位置之外。如此一来，他便处于劣势。他与二位陛下之间的距离，可能使他听不清某些话，尤其是皇上的话——皇上声音极低，毫无传声之力。官员可以设法克服这一不便、缩短这段距离：给那引他入觐见殿的太监一笔贿赂，请他撤去几只拜垫，他便可跪得更近台基。首相与大学士的拜垫无论如何不可撤去，其余三只则由那引见的太监做主。只要给的银钱够数——每撤一只拜垫都有定价——他便肯把那三位较低军机大臣的拜垫撤掉。

===Original===
When the official who has been granted an Audience is conducted to the Audience Hall by the eunuch appointed for the purpose, the latter throws open the great doors, falls upon his knees at the threshold, and announces the name and position of the official, gives the hour and minute of his arrival at the Palace, and, before he rises, he has deftly removed the cushions for which he has received the required sum. After his name has been announced, the official enters and kneels as near the dais as is consistent with his rank and the sum paid the eunuch. When the eunuch has introduced the official, he turns from the door and must run away as fast as he can. Officials and eunuchs stationed at some distance watch his departure. Should he linger or transgress this law, capital punishment is the result. This is to avoid eavesdropping and the possible transmission of State secrets.

===Chinese===
蒙恩觐见的官员，由专职太监引至觐见殿时，那太监便推开大门，在门槛处跪下，唱出该官员的姓名和官职，并报出他到宫中的时辰与分秒；而未及起身之前，他已灵巧地撤去了那几只收过钱的拜垫。名姓唱过之后，官员便入内，按其品级和所付太监的银数，跪到尽可能接近台基之处。太监把官员引见既毕，便须转身，并以最快的速度奔离。远处驻守的官员和太监们都注视着他离去。倘若他踟蹰逗留、违了这条规矩，便要处以极刑。此举乃为防偷听，亦防泄露国家机密。

===Original===
When the official granted an Audience hears the last echo of the steps of the departing eunuch, he falls upon his knees and begins the relation of his business. Their Majesties question him, if necessary, to elicit further explanations. When the Audience is finished, the official rises and walks out. The Chinese never back out of the Presence, and it is not considered a breach of etiquette to turn their backs upon Royalty!

===Chinese===
蒙恩觐见的官员，听见那离去太监脚步声的最后一缕余响，便跪下，开始奏陈己事。若有必要，二位陛下便向他问话，以引出进一步的说明。觐见既毕，官员起身，步行退出。中国人从不行倒退之礼退出御前；在君前转身以背相向，并不算失仪！

===Original===
The officials who are obliged to go often to Audiences resort to an amusing subterfuge to protect their knees from the marble floor. They strap heavily wadded cushions around their knees before they go in, and they can thus kneel in comfort. The long Chinese gown worn by the men, of course, hides these knee cushions.

===Chinese===
那些不得不常常入觐的官员，为护住自己的膝盖免受大理石地面之苦，想出了一个颇有趣味的权宜之计：入觐之前，他们在膝上缠缚厚厚的棉垫，如此跪着便不觉难受。男子所穿的长袍，自然把这些护膝遮得严严实实。

## The Official Audience of Their Majesties / 二位陛下的正式觐见

===Original===
His Majesty assumed the cares of State at an early age, when he was still filled with boyish spirit. Many of the heads of departments are old men, and some of them doubtless most tiresome in reiterating facts and dwelling upon details. When the young Emperor first took over the direction of affairs and held his Audiences alone, he would get very impatient at hearing several of these old men go over tiresome details. As it is not "according to the laws of propriety" for the official to raise his eyes to the Sacred Person, while the old man rambled on, with prosy detail, the young Emperor would slip off the Throne and quietly descend from the dais, and when the poor official raised his eyes to make his obeisance to the Emperor, he would see only the vacant Throne! His Majesty had been in the rear of the hall behind the screen for perhaps five minutes smoking a cigarette or otherwise diverting himself!

===Chinese===
皇上年纪尚轻便已肩负起国政，那时他还满怀少年心气。各部堂官多有年迈之人，其中几位想必翻来覆去地陈述、絮絮叨叨地讲求细节，实在叫人厌烦。当初年轻的皇上初秉国政、独自御觐见之时，听见这几位老臣一味地讲那些乏味的细枝末节，心中便十分不耐。因官员「按礼」不得抬眼仰视圣驾，那老臣还在絮絮叨叨、噜苏不休之际，年轻的皇上便从宝座上溜下来，悄然下了台基；待那可怜的官员抬起眼来，要向皇上行礼时，只见一座空空的宝座！皇上却已在大殿后方的屏风背后，也许已待了五分钟之久，正在那里吸一支纸烟，或以别的法子自娱！

===Original===
I noticed a curious fact as to the quality of the sacredness of the persons of Their Celestial Majesties. This sacredness seems to belong to them as rulers and not as individuals. In the Audience Hall when administering justice, they are not approached nor addressed, except upon bended knee. In the Palace, in their own privacy, when they give an order or any command touching upon official affairs, this order is received by the attendant, be he courtier, high official or great prince, on his knees. When any official communication is made to Their Majesties, in private or elsewhere, it is made kneeling, but when Their Majesties are in their private capacity and spoken to on ordinary affairs, they are addressed almost familiarly, and the courtier or simple attendant stands while speaking to them. If, however, in the midst of a familiar conversation an order is given, the attendant immediately drops upon his knees to receive it.

===Chinese===
关于二位陛下圣体的那种神圣不可侵犯之气，我注意到了一桩奇特之事：这种神圣，似乎是属于他们作为君主的一面，而非属于他们作为个人的一面。在大朝殿临朝决事之时，除非跪着，无人可近其前、无人敢与之语。在宫中，当他们身处私室，一旦下一道涉及公务的谕旨或命令，奉命之人——不论是近臣、高官还是显贵的王公——都须跪着接旨。凡向二位陛下奏报公事，无论在私室还是他处，皆须跪奏；然而二位陛下若以私人身份而谈寻常琐事，旁人则几乎可与之不拘礼数地交谈，近臣或寻常侍者与他们说话时，是站着的。不过，倘若在这种不拘礼数的闲谈之中，忽然降下一道旨意，那侍者便须立即跪下接旨。

===Original===
The kowtow (pronounced ker-toe and meaning literally to bow the head) is used as a form of thanks, and is not a manner of greeting. The actors kowtow to Their Majesties at the beginning and end of each performance at the Theater, first to thank for the honor they are to receive in being allowed to act before them, and at the end to thank for the privilege granted. The officials "bow the head" to thank for an Audience or any favor or gift they have received or are to receive from Their Majesties. The kowtow is not only made by people at the Palace and at Imperial Audiences: it is sometimes used by equals to each other as a proper manner of thanking for some great favor. To make the kowtow, the person kneels three times and each time bows his head three times, touching the ground with it. The kowtow could not be made by a foreigner without looking most awkward and appearing most servile, but the Chinese do it with dignity, and it is neither ungraceful nor degrading-looking. It is a time-honored manner of giving thanks, a Chinese tradition surviving from a time when the courtiers were perhaps like slaves, but at present it does not imply any slave-like inferiority on the part of him who performs it.

===Chinese===
「叩首」（kowtow，读音近「克-托」，字面义即「以首叩地」）乃是一种致谢之礼，并非问候之礼。戏园里伶人每演一戏，开场与收场都要向二位陛下叩首：开场时，是谢恩准他们在御前献技的荣宠；收场时，是谢所赐的这一番恩典。官员们「叩首」，是为一次觐见、或为已受或将要受自二位陛下的任何恩宠与赏赐而致谢。叩首之礼不仅行于宫中及觐见御前：地位相等之人之间，有时为某桩大恩而郑重致谢，亦用此礼。行叩首之礼时，其人跪三次，每跪一次便以首叩地三次。外国人行此礼，难免显得笨拙、一副奴态；然中国人行之，则自有其庄重，既不难看，亦无卑微之态。这是一种由来已久的致谢之仪，是中国的一项古传之礼，可溯至近臣尚如奴仆的时代；然在今日，行此礼之人并不因此含带半分奴性的卑下。

## XV The Literary Tastes and Accomplishments of the Empress Dowager / 第十五章 皇太后的文学品味与才情

===Original===
When Her Majesty the Empress Dowager was Empress of the Western Palace, Co-Regent with the Empress of the Eastern Palace, who died in 1881, the Empress of the Eastern Palace was known as the "Literary Empress." All State affairs were left to the stronger executive ability of the Empress of the Western Palace; while she of the Eastern Palace gave herself up to literary pursuits and led the life of a student. She was a woman of such fine literary ability that she, herself, sometimes examined the essays of the aspirants for the highest literary honors in the University of Peking. She was also a writer of distinction.

===Chinese===
当今皇太后昔年为西宫皇后、与东宫皇后同朝并理朝政之时——东宫皇后崩于 1881 年——那东宫皇后素有「文后」之名。凡国家大事，一概交由西宫皇后那更为刚强的执政才干去裁处；东宫皇后则一心潜心于文事，过着书生一般的日子。她文才极佳，有时竟亲去阅看北京太学中求取最高科名之士的文章。她也是一位颇有声名的著作大家。

===Original===
During the long Co-Regency of these two remarkable women, widows of the Emperor Hsien-Feng, one led the life of a student; the other, the active, militant life of the ruler. For the present Empress Dowager has been the real ruler of the great Chinese Empire for the last forty-five years. Had the Empress of the Eastern Palace not been such an exceptional light as a literary woman and had not Her Majesty, Tze-Hsi, possessed so many other and more remarkable qualities, the latter's name might also go down to history as a "literary Empress," for the Empress Dowager has literary qualities of no mean kind. She writes a graceful poem, is able to express herself in elegant Chinese, as well as in the ruder, more forcible Manchu language. She can write in literary style, fine idiomatic Chinese, and this is a rare accomplishment for a woman. The written Chinese language is quite different from that spoken by even the most cultivated. Imagery and figure abound to such a degree, literary form is so important, that many fine scholars are unable to write the language acceptably, except for practical purposes. Aside from Her Majesty's literary acquirements, she has an enlightened taste, is a great reader of the classics, and a fine critic. She also loves poems of heroic adventure. One of her favorite historical characters is the Chinese Jeanne d'Arc, the warlike Maiden, Whar-Mou-Lahn, who went forth to battle in masculine guise, had many heroic adventures in her twelve years' service, and, through them all, remained a virgin pure.

===Chinese===
这两位奇女子——咸丰帝（Hsien-Feng）的遗孀——长年同朝听政之时，一位过着书生的日子，另一位则过着君主那种活跃而争斗不息的日子。因为当今皇太后，这四十五年来才是这大清帝国的真正主宰。倘若东宫皇后不是作为才女如此卓绝出众，倘若皇太后慈禧不曾具备那许多别样且更为显赫的资质，那么她的名号或许也会作为一位「文后」而流传青史——因为皇太后的文学造诣，实非泛泛。她能写一手清丽的诗，既能以雅正的汉文抒写胸臆，也能以较为质朴、却更为刚劲的满语表情达意。她能以文言撰写典丽的、地道的中文，而这对一个女子而言，是极难得的本领。书面的中文，与哪怕最有学问之人嘴里所说的话，都截然不同。意象与辞藻之繁、文体之讲究，到了这般地步，以致许多饱学之士除为实用起见外，都难以把这种文字写得像样。皇太后除却这等文学上的造诣，更有着开明的品味，博览经史，又是一位极好的鉴赏家。她也爱读那些建功立业的英雄诗篇。她最喜爱的历史人物之一，便是中国的圣女贞德——那位戎装的女杰花木兰，她女扮男装奔赴沙场，在十二年的征战中历尽英雄壮举，而历经这一切，始终守身如玉。

===Original===
The Empress Dowager has a wonderful verbal memory. Memory, so highly esteemed by the Chinese, is most carefully cultivated, and is generally better developed with them than with us. Her Majesty's memory is, however, considered exceptional, even among the Chinese. She can repeat pages, not only of the classics, but of her favorite authors. One of the widows of her son (the Emperor Tung-Chih), who came regularly every week to pay her respects to Her Majesty, is a very clever woman and a great favorite of her august mother-in-law. This lady also possesses a remarkable memory. On her visits to the Palace I used to hear Her Majesty and this Empress quoting from some of their favorite classics or poems. The quotations would pass from one to another, sometimes for a half-hour without stopping, and, at times, they would repeat in concert some favorite phrase. I will never forget how they looked: Her Majesty sitting at her Throne-table with her flowers or some light occupation, her daughter-in-law standing beside her, each of their faces lighted up with pleasure as they repeated line after line.

===Chinese===
皇太后有着惊人的记忆力。中国人极其看重记诵之力，下工夫悉心栽培，一般而言，他们这方面的能力比我们更胜一筹。然而皇太后的记忆力，即便在中国人中间，也被视为超群绝伦。她能背诵不止一页——不独经典，连她所钟爱的诸家之作也能成页成页地背出。她儿子（同治帝）的一位遗孀，每周按例入宫向她请安，是个极聪慧的女子，也极得这位威严婆母的欢心。这位夫人同样有着非凡的记忆力。她入宫请安之时，我常听见皇太后与这位皇后互相引诵各自钟爱的经书或诗篇。那些引文从一人传到另一人，有时持续半个钟头也停不下来，间或两人又会齐声同诵某一句心爱之语。她们那时的模样，我永世难忘：皇太后坐在她的御案前，赏弄着花卉，或做些轻省活计，儿媳侍立在侧，两人背下一句又一句，脸上都因喜悦而焕发着光彩。

===Original===
When the Empress Dowager went to her own apartments for her siesta, her reader would come bringing volumes of her favorite authors. Some days I could hear his voice rising and falling in regular cadence during the whole time she was resting in her apartments. When she was particularly interested in what had been read to her she would have the book taken out when she went for her daily promenade and would sit and read as she was carried along in her open chair, or was rowed along on the barge. This did not often happen, however, for she took such keen delight in all its manifestations, she preferred to read in Nature's book when out-of-doors.

===Chinese===
皇太后回到自己寝宫午歇之时，她的侍读太监便携了她所钟爱的诸家卷帙前来。有些日子，她在寝宫歇息的整段时辰里，我都能听见他吟诵的嗓音抑扬顿挫、起落有致。倘若她对所读的内容格外有兴致，便命人把书带出去，待每日出游之时，坐在敞轿里被抬着、或在游船上被荡着，便坐下来展读。只是这般情形并不多见，因为她在天地万物的种种显现中都能体味到那样浓烈的兴味，身在户外之时，她倒更爱去读大自然那部大书。

===Original===
She is a great lover of the theater and prefers the classic, the old plays, to the modern Chinese drama. She had one new play staged, while I was in the Palace, with which she seemed to be much pleased. She studied the play for several days before it was given for the first time, and, at the first representation, she followed every line with intense interest. She sent her eunuchs several times to the stage to suggest changes in the rendering of certain parts and in the interpretation of certain lines. The Theater generally begins with a short play, which is often a light farce. She seemed sometimes to enjoy these very much and would laugh heartily at the good hits, which were often original additions by the actors, allusions to some passing event. Contrary to my preconceived idea as to the Chinese, they are witty and appreciate humor in others. The Empress Dowager has a fine sense of humor. She not only sees the point of a joke, but she can turn one very cleverly herself.

===Chinese===
她极爱看戏，比起近代的中国戏文，她更偏爱那些古典的、旧日的传奇。我在宫中之时，她命人新排了一出戏，似乎颇为中意。这出戏头一回上演之前，她研读了数日；及至头一场开演，她每一句唱念都紧跟不舍，兴致极浓。她几番差遣太监到戏台上去，吩咐将某些段子的唱念、某些句子的诠释加以改动。开戏之前，照例先演一出短戏，往往是轻松的笑剧。她有时似乎也极赏鉴这些，遇有巧妙之处，便朗声大笑——那些巧妙之处往往是伶人自己的穿插，暗指着眼前刚刚发生的事。与我对中国人先入为主的看法恰恰相反，他们其实机智得很，也懂得欣赏他人言语中的风趣。皇太后颇有幽默之感。她不仅能领悟笑话的妙处，自己也能极其巧妙地回敬一句。

===Original===
She is very particular about the way Chinese is spoken, a great stickler for purity of expression and elegance of style. There are as many dialects in China as there are Provinces in the Great Empire; and although the literati and gentry speak, what is called Mandarin Chinese, some of the most highly educated of the literati from the Provinces speak it with an accent. Her Majesty, who has a musical ear and great discernment as to sounds, gets very impatient when listening to Chinese spoken with an accent. It is said, other things being not quite equal, she will give the preference, in an appointment, to an official who speaks perfect Chinese and who has a good voice, especially if his office brings him often into the Presence. However, particular as she is, bad Chinese in a man of merit is not a bar to advancement, for Li-Hung-Chang, whom she appreciated so highly, and to whom she gave such preferment, is said to have spoken very indifferent Chinese.

===Chinese===
她对人说中文的法子极为讲究，极力主张用语之纯正与风度之雅驯。中国这偌大帝国有多少省份，便有多少种方言；虽说读书人和缙绅之流所说的，是那被称为「官话」的言语，然而外省一些极有学问的士子，说起官话来仍带着乡音。皇太后天生一副懂音律的耳朵，辨音极精，听见有人带着乡音说中文，便十分不耐。据说，在其余条件相去不远的情形下，她任官之时，宁愿拔擢一位中文说得纯正、嗓音又好的人，倘若这官职还须常常到御前行走，则尤其如此。然而她再怎么讲究，有才干之人纵然中文不好，也不至于因此受阻而不能升迁——李鸿章便是一例，她那样器重他、给他那样优厚的提拔，据说他说的却是一口颇为平庸的中文。

===Original===
Whether it be, that Her Majesty's musical and exquisitely modulated voice, so fresh and silvery, so youthful, adds to the charm of her Chinese, when she speaks it, it sounds like beautiful rhythmic poetry. She speaks it so graphically, with such expression and graceful gestures, that it charms one even who does not understand the language.

===Chinese===
不知是否因为皇太后那一副乐感十足、抑扬有致的嗓音——那般清新、那般清亮、那般年轻——更为她所说的中文平添了几分风韵，总之她一开口说起中文，听去便如优美而富韵律的诗篇。她说得那样绘声绘色，神情那样丰富，手势那样优雅，纵使不懂中国话的人，听了也会为之倾倒。

===Original===
One day when she was out for a walk, one of the directors of the gardeners was brought up to explain something to her, some change in the laying out of new flower beds. She listened a few moments, but I saw her frown and begin to look impatient. After a few more words from the poor man, who was evidently overcome by timidity and probably speaking worse Chinese than usual, Her Majesty turned to the Chief Eunuch and said, "Let him tell you and you can translate to me; I can't stand any more of that language," and she walked away, still frowning.

===Chinese===
有一日她出外散步，园丁的一名管事被带到跟前，向她回禀一桩事——新花圃的布置有所改动。她听了片刻，我便见她皱起眉头，面露不耐之色。那可怜的人显然被怯意所慑，说的中文多半比平日更糟，又嗫嚅了几句之后，皇太后便转向总管太监说道：「让他说给你听，你再译给我听吧；那种话我可再也受不了啦。」说着便走开了，眉头依旧紧锁。

===Original===
Another day, I heard the Empress Dowager tell one of the Ladies at Court (her daughter-in-law), who was also a great purist in the matter of language, about her own Chinese having been misunderstood by one of the eunuchs. There are many Chinese words almost exactly alike in sound, which are only differentiated by the inflection or tone. Thus there must be great accuracy of enunciation, and there must also be great accuracy of ear. Her Majesty had given an order to one of the eunuchs. The stupid fellow had misunderstood the inflection and had done the exact opposite. She was so amused and astonished, when she found that *her* tone had been misunderstood, that she did not reprove him for his stupidity.

===Chinese===
又有一日，我听见皇太后对宫中一位命妇（她的儿媳）说起一桩事——她说的中文竟被一名太监听岔了——这位命妇在言语一道上，也是位极讲求纯正的人。中文里有许多词，读音几乎完全相同，唯靠声调的抑扬轻重的分别来加以区分。因此吐字务必极其精准，听音也务必极其精准。皇太后曾向一名太监传下一道吩咐。那蠢才把声调听错了，所做之事恰与所传的相反。待她发觉连她自己的声调竟也被人听岔了，又是好笑又是惊异，竟不曾为他的愚笨而责斥于他。

===Original===
One day, she corrected one of the Princesses for the pronunciation of a word, and she said (in an aside) it was not strange this Princess did not speak better, for her father's Chinese was "execrable," thus showing that even Princes do not always speak the language correctly.

===Chinese===
有一日，她为某一个字的读音纠正了一位王妃，随后（在一旁）说道，这位王妃说话不肯再讲究些，也不足为怪，因为她父亲的中国话简直「糟糕透顶」——由此可见，即便是亲王，说的中国话也未必时时正确。

===Original===
One of the most precious gifts the Empress Dowager makes, and which is sacredly treasured by its recipients, is a scroll with a single great character written upon it by Her Majesty's own hand. This is considered one of the most difficult feats of a Chinese writer. These characters are sometimes four feet long. One day we were invited to go into the Throne-room to see Her Majesty make some of these characters. When I went into the Great Hall, Her Majesty and the Ladies were already there. She was stirring a great bowl of India ink, for she is very particular as to its consistency and fluidity. When the ink suited her, she took from a eunuch standing near, who held a number, a huge short-handled brush, which she could hardly clasp in her small hand. She tried two or three, before she found one that pleased her, and, turning to me, said, "You see I also have my choice in brushes." I asked Lady Yu-Keng to tell her that I thought her large brushes were more suitable for my hands and that my smaller ones would have been more appropriate for her. She laughingly replied she preferred the Chinese brush, and that her hands, small as they were, were able to wield it very satisfactorily, which was no vain boast.

===Chinese===
皇太后所赐之物中，最为珍贵的一种——受赐之人无不奉若神明般珍藏着——乃是一幅条幅，上头由她亲手题写一个大字。这在中国书写者看来，乃是最难的功夫之一。这些字有时长达四英尺。有一日，我们被请进宝座殿，去看皇太后写这些大字。我走进大殿之时，皇太后和几位命妇已在那里了。她正搅动着一一大碗墨汁，因为墨汁的浓稠稀薄、运笔的润涩流畅，她都极讲究。待墨调到她中意，她便从一名侍立身侧、手持数支毛笔的太监手里，接过一支粗大短柄的笔——那笔她那双小手几乎都握不过来。她试了两三支，才寻着一支合意的，转头向我说道：「你瞧，我选笔也是有讲究的。」我请裕庚夫人转告她：我觉得她那些大笔更适合我的手，而我那些小笔倒是更适合她。她笑着答道，她倒偏爱中国的毛笔，又说她的手虽小，挥起笔来却颇为顺手——这倒并非自夸之词。

===Original===
When all was ready, and the huge scroll spread out before her on a table, she dipped her brush into the bowl of ink, held by the eunuch, and began the first stroke of one of these famous characters, in which she is said to equal the most proficient writers in China. I was amazed to see the firmness of her wrist and the beautiful clearness of her stroke, which deviated not a hair's breadth from the line she wished to follow. She made six great characters on six of the scrolls. These characters meant "Peace," "Prosperity," "Longevity," etc. When she had finished these, she said she feared her hand had no longer the firmness necessary for doing another.

===Chinese===
一切齐备，那一大幅条幅已在她面前的案上铺展开来，她便把笔伸进太监捧着的墨碗里蘸了蘸，开始写下那些著名大字的第一笔——据说在这方面，她足以与中国最精熟的书法家并驾齐驱。我看着她手腕那般稳健、笔锋那般清丽，一丝一毫也不偏离她想走的笔路，真是不胜惊叹。她在六张条幅上写了六个大字。这些字是「寿」、「福」、「康」等等。写完这些，她说只怕她的手腕已不再有写下一个字所必需的那股稳健之力了。

===Original===
While she was writing, the young Empress, the Princesses, and the eunuchs stood around, watching her with intense interest. They seemed to take great pride in her firmness of touch and her accuracy of line.

===Chinese===
她挥毫之时，皇后、诸位王妃以及太监们都侍立四周，目不转睛地看着，兴致极浓。他们似乎为她那笔力的沉稳、线条的精准而深感自豪。

===Original===
The Chinese written character must be made in a certain way. It must begin at a given part. The strokes must follow a given direction. The transversal strokes must be placed with mathematical precision. Nothing is left to the caprice or individuality of the writer. Anyone, knowing the Chinese written characters, can tell you whether these complicated hieroglyphs were begun at the proper place or made in the proper way. They may look perfectly correct to the uninitiated observer who has a most accurate eye, and still not be so considered by the connoisseur.

===Chinese===
中国的方块字，必须依一定的法子写就。每一笔都须从一定之处起笔。笔画须沿一定的走向行去。横画的安排，须如数学一般精准。丝毫不能由书写者的兴致或个性而擅自更改。但凡识得中国字的人，都能告诉你这些繁复的象形文字是否从该起之处起笔、是否依该写之法写成。在外行人那双眼最老练的旁观者看来，它们也许显得毫无瑕疵，然而在鉴赏家眼中，却未必就无可挑剔。

===Original===
The Empress Dowager Writing a "Great Character"

===Chinese===
皇太后书写「大字」

===Original===
The firmness of Her Majesty's touch is also very apparent in her painting, for she is very artistic, and paints flowers in a charming way; in fact, she is remarkably clever with her fingers. She does not embroider now, as she formerly did, nor does she paint so much, for she says her eyes are not so good as they were, though she does not and has never worn glasses. There are a great number of artificial flowers made in the Palace, as no Manchu lady's coiffure is considered complete without flowers. Her Majesty is very particular about the way these flowers are made, and when they were brought to her for inspection, with a deft touch she would give a defective flower the required form.

===Chinese===
皇太后下笔之沉稳，在她作画时也极为明显，因为她极具艺术天分，所画花卉风姿绰约；说到底，她那一双手实在灵巧得很。她如今已不像从前那样做刺绣，作画也不像从前那样多了，因为她说眼力不济了——虽说她不曾戴过眼镜，也从不曾戴过。宫中所造的绢花、纸花为数极多，因为满族女子的头饰，若无花便算不得齐整。皇太后对这些花的扎制极为讲究，每逢花送上来给她过目，只要哪一朵略有瑕疵，她轻轻一拈，便能将那花拈出该有的模样。

===Original===
She often makes new designs for the flowers, having them woven into quaint figures, or having a number of small blooms made into a representation of some large flower. She sometimes had her diadem made of the snowy blooms of the fragrant jasmine, set with leaves and other small flowers, representing jewels, and she would wear this instead of her real jewels.

===Chinese===
她常为这些花样翻出新样，命人把花扎成古雅的图案，又或把许多小花合起来，做成一朵大花的模样。她有时竟教人用那芬芳茉莉的雪白花朵扎成凤冠，缀上叶片和别的小花以充珠玉，她便戴着这凤冠，以替代真正的珠翠。

===Original===
She is a great believer in one of the rules that Confucius lays down for the attainment of "Illustrious Virtue"; she "cultivates her person." She is always immaculately neat. She designs her own dresses, and has her jewels set according to her own directions. She is very artistic in the arrangement of her flowers and jewels, and sees that they harmonize with her toilet. She has excellent taste in the choice of colors, and I never saw her with an unbecoming color on, except the Imperial yellow. This was not becoming, but she was obliged to wear it on all official occasions. She used to modify it, as much as possible, by the trimmings, and would sometimes have it so heavily embroidered that the original color was hardly visible.

===Chinese===
孔子为求「明德」所立下的种种准则中，有一条她笃信不疑——她「修其身」。她总是纤尘不染、齐整洁净。衣裳由她亲自设计，首饰也照她自己的吩咐镶嵌。她对花与首饰的安排极具艺术匠心，务求与一身装束相得益彰。她在色彩的选择上品味极佳，我从未见她穿过不合宜的颜色，唯独那明黄除外。这颜色她穿着并不相宜，但凡遇大典礼，她又不得不穿。她便尽量用滚边、镶料来冲淡它，有时索性在黄缎上密密绣满花纹，绣到原本的底色几乎看不出来了。

===Original===
She is a great epicure, and often designs new and dainty dishes. She has perfumes and soaps for her own use, made in the Palace. Although there are quantities of French and German soaps and perfumes bought for the Palace, she prefers an almond paste that she has made and often uses the soap made in the Palace. The maids would make these under her supervision. I have frequently seen them bring the mortar in which they were stirring it to Her Majesty, that she might see its progress, and she would energetically stir it herself. She is also a great lover of perfumes, and herself combines the oils of different flowers so as to produce most subtle and delightful perfumes. The Chinese say "colors, odors, and perfumes are good for the soul." The Empress Dowager's soul was certainly well cared for in this respect.

===Chinese===
她是位极讲究口腹之欲的人，时常自出心裁，设计些新鲜而精致的小菜。她所用之香脂、香皂，皆由宫中自制。虽说宫中也采买大量的法国和德国香皂、香水，她却偏爱一种由她命人自制的杏仁膏，又常用宫中所制的香皂。这些物事都由宫女们在她的指点下亲手调制。我常常看见她们把搅和香料的乳钵端到皇太后跟前，让她看看进行的情形，她自己也兴致勃勃地动手搅上一番。她也极爱用香，并亲手将不同花卉的香精油加以调配，调出最为幽微妙曼、令人愉悦的香气。中国人说「色、香、味，皆有益于灵魂」。皇太后的灵魂在这一层上，无疑是被照料得极周全的。

===Original===
The Chinese are so near to nature, so simple in every way, that their influence over animals and birds is extraordinary, and seems to us almost magical. They are very fond of all animals, and especially so of birds. They train and teach these latter in wonderful ways. I have often seen a Chinese go near a singing bird's cage and tell it to sing, and it would pour forth its little heart in melody. Birds never seem to have any fear of them. In the afternoons, in early spring, or on a fine day in winter, one may see hundreds of well-dressed and dignified men each carrying a covered bird cage, taking the birds out for the air. When they arrive at some open space in the city, or beautiful spot in the environs, they uncover the cages and hold them aloft, or simply sit with them on their knees, and the bird will sing as if its little throat would burst. They have absolutely no fear, and, though caged, seem to have a perfect understanding with their owners and obey their voices. They are often let out of the cages when taken out for exercise, but they will return to them at the call of their owners; and these birds are not hatched in cages—they are taken from the forests and trained.

===Chinese===
中国人距自然如此之近，处事如此质朴，因此他们对鸟兽的影响之力，异乎寻常，在我们看来几乎是带着魔力的。他们极爱一切动物，尤其偏爱飞鸟。他们驯鸟、教鸟的法子，妙不可言。我常看见一个中国人走到一只能歌之鸟的笼边，吩咐它唱，那小鸟便会倾尽寸心，婉转地唱将起来。鸟儿似乎对他们毫无畏惧之心。早春的午后，或冬日里一个晴朗的日子，便可见成百衣着体面、举止庄重的男子，每人提着一只罩了布的鸟笼，带鸟儿出去吹吹风。待他们走到城中某处空旷之地，或郊外某处胜景，便掀开笼衣，把笼子高高举起，又或干脆把笼子搁在膝上坐着，那鸟便唱起来，仿佛那小小的喉管都要唱破了。它们全无惧意，虽在笼中，却仿佛与主人彼此心意相通，唯主人之命是从。带它们出来活动之时，常常把它们从笼中放出来，可主人一呼唤，它们便会回到笼中去；而这些鸟并非生在笼中——它们是从林间捉来，再加以训练的。

===Original===
Two of the religious precepts of the Chinese—"Hurt no living thing," "Protect all living things"—are carried so far, they will allow an animal to live in misery rather than put him out of it by a speedy death. They love all animals and fear none. They say if you do not attack an animal, however dangerous he is, he will not harm you.

===Chinese===
中国人的两条宗教训诫——「勿伤生」、「护众生」——被推到了这般地步：他们宁可任一只动物在痛苦中苟活，也不肯用一下痛快的了断来解脱它。他们爱一切动物，也不惧怕任何动物。他们说，你若不去攻击一只动物，无论它多么凶险，它也不会加害于你。

===Original===
The Empress Dowager seemed also to possess this almost magical power over animals. Her dogs never paid the slightest attention but to her voice, and would obey her slightest gesture; but, fond as she was of them, she rarely caressed them; and she was so particular about her hands that, when she did stroke or fondle one of her pets, she would immediately after have a cloth wrung out of hot water brought to wipe her fingers. I never saw a dog in her arms but once, and this was a puppy which she took a fancy to when visiting her kennels one day, and she brought him back to the Throne-room in her arms and played with him for some time.

===Chinese===
皇太后似乎也拥有这种对动物近乎魔力般的影响之力。她的狗从不理会别的任何人的嗓音，只听她一人之声，她最细微的一个手势，它们也无不听从；然而她虽疼爱它们，却极少去抚弄它们；她又极爱惜自己那双手，每有所抚弄，必立刻命人取一块绞干的热巾来，把手指一一擦净。我只见她抱过一次狗，那是一只小狗崽，有一日她去巡视狗舍之时看中了它，便抱回宝座殿，逗弄了好一会儿。

===Original===
On one of our promenades in the park I saw a curious instance of her wonderful personal magnetism and her power over animals. A bird had escaped from its cage, and some eunuchs were making efforts to catch it, when Her Majesty and suite came into that part of the grounds. The eunuchs had found it impossible to entice the bird back into its cage; nor would it come upon a long stick with a perch attached, which they held up near the tree where it rested. The eunuchs scattered at the approach of Her Majesty, and she inquired the cause of their being here. The Chief Eunuch explained what they were doing, and the Empress Dowager said, "I will call it down." I thought this was a vain boast, and in my heart I pitied her. She was so accustomed to have the whole world bow to her, she fancied even a bird in the grounds would obey her mandates, and I watched to see how she would take her defeat. She had a long, wand-like stick, which had been cut from a sapling and freshly stripped of its bark. She loved the faint forest odor of these freshly cut sticks, and in the spring often had one when she went out. They were long and slender, with a crook at the top. I used to think she looked like the pictures of fairies when she walked with these long, white wands. She would use them for pointing out a flower she wished the eunuchs to gather, or for tracing designs on the gravel when she sat down. Today she held the wand she carried aloft and made a low, birdlike sound with her lips, never taking her eyes off the bird. She had the most musical of voices, and its flute-like sound seemed like a magical magnet to the bird. He fluttered and began to descend from bough to bough until he lighted upon the crook of her wand, when she gently moved her other hand up nearer and nearer, until it finally rested on her finger!

===Chinese===
有一回我们在园中游赏，我亲眼目睹了一桩奇事，足见她那种惊人的人格魅力与对动物的驾驭之力。一只鸟从笼中逃了出来，几名太监正费力想捉住它，其时皇太后一行恰好来到这一处。太监们发觉，要把那鸟诱回笼中已是不可能；他们又举起一根长竿，竿头安着一个栖架，凑到那鸟栖息的树旁，它也不肯下来。皇太后一到，太监们便四散避开，她便问他们为何在此。总管太监回明了情由，皇太后便说：「我来把它叫下来。」我心中暗想，这未免是句大话，竟暗暗为她难过起来。她平日里惯受举世趋奉，便以为连园中的一只鸟也会听她的号令，我且看她这一番挫折如何受法。她手里有一根长长的、手杖似的树枝，是从一株小树上截下来的，新近才剥了皮。她爱这种新折之枝那股淡淡的林木清香，每到春日出门，常常执上一根。那枝又长又细，顶端弯成一个弯钩。我从前常想，她拄着这般又长又白的手杖款款而行，活像画中的仙女。她有时用这手杖指点一株花，命太监去采；又或坐下来时，用它在砂砾地上勾画图样。这一日，她把手中的杖高高举起，唇间发出一种低低的、如鸟鸣般的声音，眼睛一刻也不离那只鸟。她的嗓音本就是最为乐感十足的，那如笛般的声响，对那鸟而言，竟似一块带着魔力的磁石。它扑棱着翅膀，从一枝落到下一枝，一路下来，终于落在她杖端的弯钩之上；她又轻轻地、慢慢地把另一只手移近，移近，再移近——终于，那鸟停在了她的指尖！

===Original===
The Empress Dowager in the Gardens of the Summer Palace—Calling a Bird

===Chinese===
皇太后在颐和园中——唤鸟

===Original===
I had been watching with breathless attention, and so tense and absorbed had I become that the sudden cessation, when the bird finally came upon her finger, caused me a throb of almost pain. No one else, however, of her entourage seemed to think this anything extraordinary. After a few moments she handed the bird to one of the eunuchs, and we continued on our promenade.

===Chinese===
我屏息凝神地看着，全神贯注到了这般地步，乃至那鸟终于落到她指尖之时，这戛然而止的一刹，竟叫我心头几乎痛楚地一悸。然而她随行众人之中，似乎并无一人觉得这事有何稀奇。过了一会儿，她便把鸟交给一名太监，我们便继续向前游赏去了。

===Original===
I saw another instance of her magnetic power, this time with a katydid. One of the Princesses, seeing one on a bush, tried to catch it, but in vain. Her Majesty held out her hand toward the beautiful insect, made a peculiar sound like their own cry, and advanced her outstretched finger until it rested upon it. She stroked it gently for a few moments, and then removed her fingers, and the katydid made no effort to fly until she put it down!

===Chinese===
我又见过一桩她那种魅力之力的奇事，这一回是与一只蝈蝈。一位王妃见灌木上有一只，便去捉，却捉了个空。皇太后朝着那美丽的虫子伸出手去，发出一种与它们自己的鸣声相仿的奇特声响，又把伸出的手指慢慢凑近，凑近——终于那虫子落在她指上了！她轻轻抚弄了片刻，随后收回手指，那蝈蝈竟一动也不动，直到她把它放下，它才肯飞走！

## XIV The Palace Eunuchs / 十四 宫中太监

===Original===
The internal affairs of the Palace are managed by eunuchs, among whom there are all grades, all sorts and conditions. Some are clever literati given to study; some have the polished, insinuating manners of the courtier; some have a Mandarin rank of high degree; some are menials. There are actors and singers, cooks and gardeners, teachers and pupils, writers and readers. They occupy all sorts of positions, from Their Majesties’ bodyguard to gatekeepers. In this hierarchy, Their Majesties’ Chief Eunuchs held the first place. Under each of these there are six eunuchs of high rank, all exceptionally clever, who have raised themselves to the positions they occupy in the Palace by their own efforts or by some special qualification.

Each of the hundreds of pavilions and palaces in the enclosures has a corps of eunuchs, presided over by a head eunuch. These act as guards to the premises, as well as servants, and keep things in readiness for a visit from Their Majesties. There is a head eunuch who directs the large corps of Palace gardeners; another who presides over the dozens of cooks in the Imperial kitchen; one is at the head of each of the departments, and each of these head eunuchs, chiefs of the different departments, is under the jurisdiction of the Chief Eunuch, for Her Majesty’s Chief Eunuch may be called the real Chief Eunuch of the Palace. He is not only older than the Emperor’s Chief Eunuch, but is more capable. The two Chief Eunuchs, from their position near the sacred persons of Their Majesties, have unusual power. They may make or mar the career of the eunuchs beneath them; and they not only have this power inside the Palace, but from their exceptionally fine opportunities to present petitions, to speak for or against certain people, they also have a great deal of power with people outside the Palace. Her Majesty’s Chief Eunuch has almost the power in Peking, among officials and courtiers, that “Son Eminence Grise” had at the Court of Louis XIII of France. He is courted and fawned upon, receives magnificent presents, and nobles of high degree wait upon his pleasure; but while he occupies this high position with outsiders, in the Palace I saw no evidence of his having any unusual power with Her Majesty, beyond that of one who has been in the lifelong service of his master and who has the privileges resulting therefrom.

===Chinese===
宫禁内务，概由太监掌理。其间品级繁多，三教九流，无所不有：有的是潜心学问的聪明文士，有的是廷臣那种温雅圆滑的风度，有的官居高位，有的则是做粗活的厮役。其中有伶人、有歌者，有厨子、有花匠，也有师生、读者与写手。他们充任各样的差事，上至两位陛下的随身侍卫，下至看门把守，无不各有其人。在这等级森严的序列之中，两位陛下身边的首领太监位列最前。每一位首领太监之下，各有六名高阶太监，皆是聪明绝顶之辈，他们或凭一己之力，或靠某种专长，才挣得宫中如今这般的位子。

禁苑之中数以百计的亭台殿阁，每一处都有一班太监，由一名首领太监统辖。他们既是该处的护军，又是听差的厮役，须得时刻打理妥帖，以备两位陛下临幸。有一名首领太监统带宫中那一大群花匠；另有一名首领太监，统带御膳房里数十名厨子；各色衙门各有一名首领太监执掌。这些分掌各处的首领太监，皆归大总管统辖——因为皇太后的首领太监，实可称为宫中真正的大总管。他不仅比皇上的首领太监年长，也更干练。这两位首领太监因终日侍奉两位陛下圣驾左右，权势非同寻常。宫中那些小太监的功名前程，成之毁之皆在他们一念之间；而且他们不止在宫内如此，因有呈递折子、为人进言或构陷的绝佳便利，对宫外之人亦颇具权势。在北京的官员与廷臣之间，皇太后的首领太监其权势之盛，几乎与当年法国路易十三朝廷里那位「灰衣大老」（Son Eminence Grise）相埒。他为人奉承趋附，受着厚礼，连高贵的王公也不得不仰其鼻息；然而他虽对外人处此高位，我在宫中却未见他对皇太后有何出格的势力，不过是终身侍奉主子的一位老仆所享的那点体面罢了。

===Original===
The peculiar position of a Chinese Emperor, which shuts him in his Palace like a Buddha in a temple, makes some sort of confidential private messenger an absolute necessity. There is much business of an unofficial kind, which must be transacted in a private way. The Chief Eunuchs are naturally called upon in such cases. When the Ruler of the Celestial Empire is a woman, the Palace becomes more of a gilded prison, a shut-in shrine, than even in the case of an Emperor. She cannot see officials, or even members of the Imperial clan, except in the Audience Halls. Thus a Chief Eunuch under an Empress would have even greater power than under an Emperor; and in this instance, Her Majesty’s Chief Eunuch, Li Lien Ying, is really of exceptional ability!

===Chinese===
中国皇上的处境殊为特殊：他如供奉在庙中的佛像一般，深居于宫禁之内，是以某种贴心的私人信使，便成了断不可少之物。有许多非官方性质的事务，须得私下里悄悄办理。此等场合，自然便轮到几位首领太监出面。当这天朝的君主是位女子之时，宫禁便比男皇之时更像一座镀金的牢狱、一座与世隔绝的神龛。她不能在觐见的殿宇之外接见臣工，甚至宗室至亲也不可得见。是以一位女皇之下的首领太监，其权势比在男皇之下还要来得大；而在本朝，皇太后的首领太监李莲英（Li Lien Ying），其才干实属非同寻常！

===Original===
In person he is tall and thin. His head is, in type, like Savonarola’s. He has a Roman nose, a massive lean jaw, a protruding lower lip, and very shrewd eyes, full of intelligence, that shine out of sunken orbits. His face is much wrinkled and his skin like old parchment. Though only sixty years old, he looks seventy-five, and is the oldest eunuch in the Palace. He has been there since the age of ten. He has elegant, insinuating manners, speaks excellent Chinese﻿—having a fine enunciation, a good choice of words, and a low, pleasant voice. If one may judge from appearances, he possesses ability in a marked degree. Of His Majesty’s Chief Eunuch I can say nothing. I only saw him on the days of the Theater, or some festival, when His Majesty passed the day with the Empress Dowager and the Ladies, when he was always accompanied by his suite.

Her Majesty’s second eunuch, Sui, who is of equal rank with Li Lien Ying, is as unlike him as two people could possibly be, both as to person, character, mental and moral nature. This one has none of the qualities of the intriguer﻿—no Machiavellian schemes would be forwarded by him. He is almost a giant in size, tall and heavy. He is forty-six years old, and has a round, full face, without a line﻿—a typical Chinese face, as we know it from pictures, benevolent and kind. He, also, is a good Chinese scholar, and, of course, speaks it elegantly. Her Majesty will have no one around her person who does not speak it well. If it be true that Her Majesty, in choosing her ministers, tries to have them the opposites of each other, so that she may thus hear the different sides of a question and arrive at more just conclusions, her two Chief Eunuchs seem to have been chosen in the same way.

===Chinese===
他身量高大而瘦削。头颅的形状，与萨沃纳罗拉（Savonarola）相类。鼻梁作鹰钩状，下颌瘦削而宽阔，下唇微微前突，一双精明的眼睛从深陷的眼窝里放出光来，目光里满是聪明。脸上皱纹极深，皮肤也像旧的羊皮纸一般。他实际年纪不过六十，看去却像七十五岁上下，是宫中最年长的太监。他从十岁起便在宫中当差。他举止温雅圆滑，说得一口极好的中国话——咬字清晰，措辞典雅，嗓音低沉悦耳。若可从外貌论人，他实是一位才能出众之人。至于皇上的首领太监，我无可奉告。我只有在戏台开演之日，或逢节庆、皇上整日与皇太后及宫眷同在之时，才见过他——那时他身后总跟着他那一班随从。

皇太后的二总管隋（Sui），品级与李莲英相同，但二人无论是相貌、性情、心地与品性，都相差之远，无以复加。这位身上全无半点阴谋家的气质——马基雅维利式（Machiavellian）的诡计，断不会出自他手。他身量近乎巨汉，高大壮硕。他年四十六岁，生得一张圆润丰满的脸，不见一丝皱纹——这是图画里我们所习见的那种典型的中国人脸庞，仁厚和善。他也是一位中国学问的好手，自然也说得一口雅致的中国话。皇太后身边，从不容一个说话不漂亮的人。若说皇太后拣选大臣之时，故意取性情相反之人，好借此兼听两面、得出较为公允的结论，那么她这两位首领太监，似乎也是按着同一法子挑选出来的。

===Original===
There is a eunuch appointed to administer the punishment, ordered by Their Majesties for the eunuchs around their persons. For the higher eunuchs, this is generally the deprivation of a certain amount of their annual wages, or the loss of their buttons, for the buttons on the hats of Chinese denote their rank, and to be deprived of a button, or to have one of lower rank given, is considered a disgrace. I once saw Her Majesty very angry over the failure to carry out one of her orders, by two of the high eunuchs, and she ordered them to be deprived of two months’ pay. The head eunuchs of the different departments administer whatever punishment they see fit, to those over whom they are placed. This punishment is generally corporal. Sometimes they abuse their authority and are very cruel in administering this, but, as a rule, the eunuchs seem to be of a mild and peace-loving nature, rather than cruel and vindictive﻿—inclined to condone the faults of their inferiors rather than punish them to the full extent of their authority. There seemed to be a feeling of esprit de corps among them﻿—a spirit of mutual helpfulness.

Each of the higher eunuchs has a number of pupils among the lower grades, who call him “Master,” and whom he trains in manners and teaches his own specialties. The higher eunuchs seemed to take the liveliest interest in the good conduct, and literary, or other, advancement of these pupils, and they push their interests with Their Majesties in every way possible﻿—each one, of course, trying to advance his pupils beyond those of some other eunuch.

===Chinese===
宫中还专设一名太监，奉两位陛下之命，掌管对御前近身太监的责罚。对高等太监而言，责罚多半是扣发若干年俸，或是褫夺顶珠——因为中国人帽子上的顶珠表示品级，被夺去顶珠，或换上一颗较低品级的顶珠，乃是一桩耻辱。我曾见皇太后因两名大太监未能照办她的一道谕旨而大发雷霆，当即下旨扣二人两月俸禄。各处的首领太监，对所辖之人可视情形自行施罚。这种责罚多半是体罚。有时他们滥用权柄、下手极狠；然而总的说来，太监们的心性倒多是温和爱和平的，并不残暴好报复——他们宁可宽宥下属的过失，也不肯尽其权柄予以严惩。他们彼此之间，似有一种同袍之情（esprit de corps）——一种守望相助的精神。

每一位高等太监，手下都有若干低阶的徒弟。徒弟唤他作「师父」，他便教他们规矩，又传授自己的看家本领。高等太监对徒弟的品行进退、学问上或其他方面的长进，似乎都极其上心，想尽千方百计在两位陛下跟前为他们说项——当然，每位师父都恨不得把自己的徒弟举荐到别个太监的徒弟前头去。

===Original===
Her Majesty has a great horror of opium smoking. If a eunuch, however high his position, indulged in it, the severest punishments she ever ordered were administered. They were not only deprived of so many months’ pay and loss of their buttons, but were sometimes banished from the Palace for a certain length of time, and even severe corporal punishment would be ordered. These stringent measures did not prevent some of them, however, from indulging surreptitiously in the narcotic, but they took the most extreme precautions to prevent its being found out. Her Majesty has unusually acute olfactories, especially for opium. This, it seems, can be detected by its odor, which hangs around the clothes, and, like the odor of the rose, one “can break the vase, it lingers there still.” But it seems the eunuchs have special linen clothes, which they put on for smoking, and these are given to be washed, immediately the fascinating pipe is finished. Unless one is an habitual smoker, the drug has very little outward effect and, except by the odor, it cannot be detected.

The eunuchs are very fond of all sorts of pets, and have in their quarters dogs without number, cats and birds. While the younger eunuchs generally depend for their advancement upon their teachers, who report favorably on them to Their Majesties, they sometimes attract the attention of Their Majesties, and may be raised out of their places by Imperial favor. Among the eunuchs assigned to my service in the Palace, was one who was fortunate enough to attract the Emperor’s notice. His Majesty had happened to notice him, carrying my wraps on one of the promenades with Her Majesty. He liked his face and manners and took him into his own service. The eunuch had a “button” bestowed on him and promised to mount very fast in grade. This eunuch had been in the Palace about fifteen years; and had His Majesty not happened to notice him, he might have lived and died in oblivion, and never had a button, for his “master” was dead and he had no protector to push his interests!

===Chinese===
皇太后深恶抽大烟。若有太监纵情于此，不论位分多高，皆处以她所下过的最严厉的刑罚。不止是扣几个月俸禄、夺去顶珠而已，有时甚至逐出宫禁若干时日，还会下旨施以重重的体罚。然而这般严厉的法子，仍不能禁绝其中若干人偷偷享用那麻醉之物；他们只是千方百计、极尽小心，免得被人发觉。皇太后的嗅觉异常敏锐，尤善于嗅出鸦片。据说这物事的气味会沾在衣裳上久久不散，恰如玫瑰的香气一般——「纵然打碎了花瓶，香气仍萦绕不散」。但太监们似乎另有专用的麻布衣裳，抽烟时换上，一筒过完了瘾，便立即拿去洗濯。人若非惯抽之客，此物在外的形迹极微，除了气味之外，再无从辨认。

太监们极爱养各样的玩物，他们住处所养的狗不计其数，还有猫、有鸟。年轻的太监虽然多半仰仗师父提携——师父在两位陛下跟前为他们说好话——但有时他们自己也能引起两位陛下的注目，蒙恩典拔擢，跳出原来的差事。派来伺候我的太监里头，便有一位运气极好，得了皇上的青眼。原来有一回皇太后领众人散步，皇上偶然瞧见他替我捧着斗篷，便看中了他。皇上喜欢他的相貌举止，便将他调入自己跟前当差。皇上赏了他一颗顶珠，看光景品级升得极快。这太监在宫里当差已有十五年上下；若不是皇上偶然瞧见他，他恐怕一辈子默默无闻地生老病死，到死也挣不到一颗顶珠——因为他的「师父」早已去世，再无人替他撑腰说项！

===Original===
When one realizes that the Palaces of the Chinese Emperor are like towns, that their affairs are administered principally by the eunuchs, one can see there must be a good deal of intelligence among them, as well as great opportunities to add to their personal wealth.

I heard, before I went into the Palace, of the great power and unscrupulousness of the Chief Eunuchs; that it would be necessary to be very conciliatory toward them and make them many handsome presents. I did not find it so. I never made an effort to conciliate any of them, nor gave any handsome presents, and I found them all respectful, and I had every consideration shown me by them, and found them, on the whole, pleasant enough to deal with. Some of them were clever and interesting even, and they all had very good manners. In fact, I cannot too highly praise the manners of the Chinese, as a race. I quite concur in the opinion of a clever Frenchman, who said of China, “*Aujourd’hui c’est là où les bonnes manières se sont refugiées.*”

===Chinese===
中国皇上的宫殿大如城镇，其内部事务又主要由太监掌理，由此便可想见：他们之间必定颇有几个聪明人，也必有大把的机会中饱私囊。

我未入宫之前，曾听闻首领太监们权势熏天、又毫无顾忌，须得百般笼络他们、送他们许多厚礼才是。我却并未见得如此。我从未刻意笼络过他们中任何一位，也未送过什么厚礼，而他们待我都彬彬有礼，我处处都蒙他们照应；与他们打交道，总的说来倒也相当愉快。其中几位甚至算得上聪明有趣，他们的举止也都十分得体。说句实话，中国人作为一个种族，其礼仪之好，我纵极力称许亦不为过。我十分赞同一位聪明的法国人对中国所下的评语——他说：「时至今日，礼仪之所在，便是中国。」（Aujourd’hui c’est là où les bonnes manières se sont refugiées.）

## III The Palace of the Emperor’s Father (Prince Ch’un, the Seventh Prince) / 第三章 皇上生父的府邸（醇亲王，即七王爷）

===Original===
The Palace of the Emperor’s Father, which the Empress Dowager had set aside for me to live in while I was at work on her portrait, was a splendid demesne, with a noble park and spacious buildings. It had been much injured by the foreign troops in 1900 and had been unoccupied since, until Her Majesty decided it would be a suitable dwelling-place for her “Portrait Painter.” She had it hastily restored and refurnished for our occupation, but many of the pavilions and summerhouses in the grounds were in ruins, and the stables but partly rebuilt. Except the grounds immediately surrounding the buildings in which the Yu-Kengs and I lived, which were well kept and garnished, the greater part of the extensive park was in a fascinating state of natural wildness. The Palace, like all others in China, consisted of a network of verandahed pavilions built around spacious courts. There was a small Theater with the Prince’s loge and stalls for his guests, and numerous tea and summer houses were scattered over different parts of the grounds.

===Chinese===
皇太后将这座皇上生父的府邸拨出来给我作绘像时居住之所，这是一处辉煌的宅邸，有气派的园林和宽敞的屋宇。1900 年它曾被外国军队大肆毁坏，此后一直无人居住，直到皇太后觉得这里对她这位「御用画像师」来说是个合适的居所。她命人匆匆将其修缮、重新布置供我们居住，但园中的亭台别业多有坍圮，马厩也只是部分修复。除了裕庚一家和我所住屋宇周围紧邻的园地修整得齐整、点缀得宜之外，那大片园林大多处于一种迷人的天然荒野之态。这座府邸与中国所有的府第一样，由一连串带有游廊的亭阁组成，环抱在宽敞的庭院四周。园中有一座小戏台，设有王爷的专座与宾客席，还有许多茶室和凉亭散落在园中各处。

===Original===
I selected, as my abiding-place, a charming group of buildings in a walled-in garden, fronting on a lotus-covered lake, with a winding stream at the back, spanned by a picturesque bridge. The principal pavilion of this group had a lofty central hall, out of which opened, on one side, bedrooms and dressing-rooms, and on the other dining-room and dependencies. Great doors in the center of the hall, which I had decided to use as my living-room, opened on a wide verandah which ran the whole length of the building. Marble steps led from this into a court filled with flowering shrubs. Two sides of the charming court had smaller pavilions similar to the central hall, and opposite this latter was a quaint stone wall, the upper part of tiled latticework, with curiously shaped openings at irregular intervals. In the center of this wall, massive wooden doors opened out on a beautiful terrace, shaded by fine old elms, over the lake. It was a charming dwelling-place, and this group of buildings soon came to be known as the “Ker-Gunia Fu,” “Ker-Gunia” being “Miss Carl” rendered into Chinese, and “Fu” meaning “Palace,” for the Chinese are very fond of nicknames. I learned later that these pavilions had been the dwelling-place of the Seventh Prince’s son, the present Emperor Kwang-Hsu, after he had been chosen as Heir to the Throne and until he went to live regularly at the Imperial Palace.

===Chinese===
我选作居所的，是围墙花园内一组宜人的屋宇，前临一方荷花掩映的湖泊，背后一道蜿蜒溪流，上架一座如画的石桥。这组建筑的主亭阁有一间高敞的正厅，一侧通向卧室和梳妆间，另一侧通向饭厅和附属各房。我决定把正厅用作起居室，厅中央的大门通向一道宽阔的游廊，游廊贯穿整座建筑。由游廊而下，几级大理石台阶引入一座繁花烂漫的庭院。这可爱庭院的两侧，各有一组与正厅相仿的较小亭阁，正厅对面则是一堵古雅的石墙，上半部是瓦砌的花格，其间错落开着形状各异的窗洞。墙的中央，两扇厚重的木门开启，通向一方秀美的台榭——台下便是湖，台上则有古榆遮阴。这真是一处宜人的居所，不久之后，这组建筑便被人称作「克姑娘府」——「克姑娘」是「Miss Carl」的中文译音，「府」即「府邸」之意，因为中国人极爱给人取绰号。后来我才得知，这几座亭阁曾是七王爷之子——即当今皇上光绪——被选为储君之后、正式入居大内之前所住的所在。

===Original===
As Her Majesty gave me my morning sittings after the Audience was finished (which lasted from eight a.m. to ten or eleven), I had plenty of time, after my cup of tea, to explore the grounds of our Palace, and I discovered new beauties each day. The Park was enclosed by high walls, for the Chinese are jealous of their privacy. Parts of the grounds were gently undulating, and all the eminences, where views could be had, were surmounted by charming summerhouses and belvederes. In one of these, where I loved to go in the early morning to refresh myself by the contemplation of the calm and peaceful lake beneath, and drink in the faint perfume of the stately lotus flowers, which grew in rich profusion on its bosom, I found an inscription on a large flat stone at the left of the entrance. I had seen enough of Chinese characters to know the inscription looked like a “poem.” The Chinese poem is rarely more than a phrase: the expression, in elegant and concise form, of some dainty fancy, some bit of philosophy, and is more properly a “verse” than a poem.

===Chinese===
皇太后给我安排的晨间坐班都在觐见结束后进行（觐见自上午八时持续至十时或十一时），因此喝过茶之后，我有充裕的时间去探访府中园地，每日都能发现新的景致。园外围以高墙，因为中国人最重私密。园中有些地方地势微微起伏，凡可凭眺之处，山顶上都建有雅致的凉亭和眺望台。其中有一处，我尤爱在清晨前往——在那里静观下方那片宁静的湖水，吮吸湖上盛开的端庄荷花所散发的淡淡幽香，身心都为之一爽。就在这处亭子入口左侧，我发现一块大平石上刻着铭文。中国字我也见过不少，一看便知这铭文是一首「诗」。中国人的诗，往往不过片言只语：以典雅简洁的形式，抒发一点精致的想象、一缕哲思，与其称作「诗」，不如称作「短句」更为恰当。

===Original===
I found, later, the inscription on the stone at the entrance of the summerhouse was really a “poem,” and had been written by no less a personage than the Seventh Prince himself! This had been his favorite place for rest and contemplation, and one day, as he reclined upon a cushion at the entrance, he had written this poem on the flat stone which lay conveniently near. The Chinese write with a brush well charged with liquid India ink, and their writing accommodates itself to almost any surface. Their characters, one for each word, take up less space than our combination of letters, and are infinitely more picturesque! Chinese gentlemen, or some attendant, generally carry about with them tablets of writing-ink and a brush, and they thus have the means at hand for jotting down a thought as it comes to them.

===Chinese===
后来我才得知，那亭子入口处石上的铭文，果真是一首「诗」，而且出自七王爷之手！这里原是他最爱的休憩与冥想之所，某日，他倚着入口处的坐垫闲坐，便在身旁这块平石上挥就了这首诗。中国人写字用毛笔，蘸饱了墨汁，几乎无论何处都能落笔。他们的字，一字即一义，比我们以字母拼合的字占的地方更小，却要生动好看得多！中国的文人——或其随从——身边常带着墨锭和毛笔，因此一有所感，便可随时记下。

===Original===
This little poem had been written with a brush, and some of the Prince’s followers had afterward cut the characters in the stone, so that it became a permanent record of a fleeting thought. It had evidently been inspired by the lotus flowers growing beneath; so gloriously beautiful today, and tomorrow shorn of their splendor. It was a plaint on the transience of worldly glory—

> … Which today, like the lotus fair,

 Lifts its head in pride;

 But tomorrow lies low,

 Bathed in the stagnant waters of oblivion.

===Chinese===
这首小诗是用毛笔写的，后来王爷的随从将字刻入石中，于是这一缕稍纵即逝的思绪便成了永久的纪念。诗显然是受了下方那片荷花的启发而作——今日这般灿烂夺目，明日便香消玉殒。全诗乃是叹人世荣华之无常——

> ……今日，宛如那娇美的莲花，

 昂首怒放，意气洋洋；

 明日却已凋零委地，

 沉沐于湮没无闻的死水之中。

===Original===
One day I came upon a number of small tombstones, in a beautiful shady corner, near the stables. I learned that these marked the last resting-places of the Prince’s favorite dogs and horses. Each stone had an inscription with the name, and extolled the virtues of the favorite, whose bones lay beneath it. The Prince was a great lover of animals, and is said to have had the best kennels and stables of any of the Imperial Princes.

===Chinese===
一日，我在马厩附近一处秀美的阴凉角落里，看到若干小小的墓碑。后来得知，这些墓碑所标的，是王爷生前的爱犬爱马的埋骨之所。每块石上都刻着它们的名字，并颂扬其长处——它们的骸骨便埋在碑下。王爷一生酷爱禽兽，据说在诸王爷之中，他的犬舍马厩是最好的。

===Original===
In my morning rambles, I also often came upon stones engraved with some character or a phrase from the classics. The ideographic Chinese characters, always picturesque, are doubly so when deeply engraved, or standing out in high relief on some rugged stone in a charming spot in the landscape. The picturesque form of the characters is sometimes heightened by being painted in vermilion or gilded; and the glowing color makes a delightful contrast with the cool gray of the stone. Even though I could not decipher the characters, nor read the phrases, I loved to come upon them in my morning walks. How much more interesting they must have been to the scholarly Chinese who understood them! How fine, when out for rest and contemplation, to come upon some thought of their great Sages cut in the living rock, or to see some character meaning “Peace” or “Prosperity” standing out, in bold relief or glowing color, from some shady nook, as if to bless him!

===Chinese===
晨间漫步之时，我也常会遇见刻着某个字或某句经典文句的石头。中国字本就生动如画，若是深深镌刻，或以高浮雕凸现于山野胜处某块粗粝的石头上，便更觉栩栩如生。字形之美，有时因涂以朱红或贴以金箔而更显鲜明；那温暖的颜色与石头清冷的灰调相映成趣，煞是悦目。即便我并不识得这些字，也读不懂这些句子，清晨散步时遇见它们，我总觉欢喜。对于那些能够读懂它们、博学的中国人而言，这些刻字想必更加意味深长罢！出外休憩冥想之时，忽然看见某位先圣先贤的哲思刻在嶙峋的山石之上；或是在某处幽阴的角落，看见一个意为「平安」或「福寿」的字，以雄浑的浮雕或鲜艳的色彩凸现其上，仿佛在为他祈福——这是何等风雅之事！

===Original===
From another of the summerhouses in the Park I could see the stone-paved highway leading from the Capital to the Summer Palace. During Their Majesties’ residence at the Summer Palace, this is a busy thoroughfare. When I did not care for peaceful contemplation or quiet rambles over the grounds, I would go to this summerhouse, whence I could see the carts and “chairs” of the officials, with their outriders, going to and from the Palace; messengers galloping past, bearing despatches; all sorts of itinerant venders, with their wares; heavily laden wagons, with small yellow banners flying, which showed they carried supplies to the Palace. Sometimes a group of horsemen would dash gaily past, the retainers of some splendidly attired young Prince, who rode in their midst on a red-saddled, handsomely caparisoned horse with silver trappings. Anon, the cumbersome, red, fringe-bedecked cart of some Princess, preceded and followed by from fifteen to thirty outriders, according to her rank in the Princely hierarchy, the black carts of her women bringing up the rear.

===Chinese===
从园中另一座凉亭望出去，可以看见那条从京城通往颐和园的石板大路。皇上和皇太后驻跸颐和园时，这条路便是一条热闹的要道。当我不愿静静沉思或安步园中之时，便会登上这座凉亭，从这里可以看见大小官员的车马和轿子，前后带着骑马的扈从，往来于颐和园；传递公文的信使策马飞驰而过；还有形形色色的流动小贩挑着货品；以及载重的货车，车上插着小黄旗，表明是为宫中运送物资。有时一队骑者会欢快地疾驰而过——那是某位服饰华美的年轻王爷的扈从，王爷本人骑着一匹配着红鞍、饰以银质辔头、鞍韂讲究的骏马，居于队伍之中。不一会儿，又会驶过某位王妃那辆笨重的红车——车周缘饰以流苏——按她在王爷品级中的位次，前后有十五至三十名骑马的扈从开道和殿后，最后面是她女眷们乘坐的黑车。

===Original===
One can tell the rank of the Chinese from the outsides of their chairs or carts. Only a reigning Emperor and Empress can go abroad in yellow chairs. The Emperor’s secondary wives ride in orange-colored chairs. The relicts of an Emperor, first or secondary, go in yellow or orange-colored carts. Princesses go abroad in red carts. Mandarins of the first and second degrees ride in green chairs; those of the third and fourth in blue chairs; and there is still another shape and style of chair for the ordinary individual, who may prefer a chair to a cart. The rank and file go in carts. These carts, peculiar to Peking, curious two-wheeled vehicles with heavy, iron-studded wheels, are uniformly covered in blue cloth. The wealth and standing of their occupants are discernible from the quality of the cloth and its trimmings, and the richness of the harness and trappings of the mule which is always used in the Peking carts. The mule in North China is a magnificent animal, much finer than the Chinese horse, which is only a pony.

===Chinese===
从轿子和车的外表，便可看出中国人的品级。唯有在位的皇上和皇后才能坐明黄轿出行。皇上的侧室乘橘黄轿。先帝——无论正配或侧室——的遗孀则乘明黄或橘黄色的车。王妃格格乘红车出行。一品、二品大员乘绿轿；三品、四品乘蓝轿；此外还有一种形制和式样不同的轿，是给寻常人乘坐的——他们也许更爱坐轿而非坐车。寻常百姓则乘车。这种车为北京所特有，是一种奇特的两轮车，轮子笨重，钉着铁钉，外面一律蒙着蓝布。乘车之人的富足与身分，可从布料的质地与缘饰，以及骡子——北京的车一律用骡子——的辔头鞍韂的讲究程度辨认出来。中国北方的骡子是极壮健的牲口，比中国的马（不过是一种小马）要好得多。

===Original===
The Seventh Prince (Prince Ch’un) must have been a most interesting personality. He was brother to the Emperor Hsien-Feng, the husband of the present Empress Dowager; and his wife, the mother of the present Emperor, was Her Majesty’s sister. This Prince was a valued friend of the two Empresses, the present Empress Dowager and She of the Eastern Palace, while they were Co-Regents during the minority of the late and a part of that of the present Emperor, and he remained, up to the time of his death, one of the most trusted advisers of the Regency. He was recognized by foreigners, as well as by the Chinese, to be an enlightened Prince as well as a man of fine character. The esteem in which he was held may have had something to do with the choice of his second son as the Successor of the late Emperor Tung-Chih, who died childless. The Chinese Emperors and their Council may choose the Successor to the Throne. If there be but one son, he is chosen as the next Heir; if there be a number, a selection may be made from them of the one seeming to be most suited for the exalted position. If there be no sons, the Successor is chosen from the nephews without reference to their age or to their being the sons of an elder or younger brother. The present Emperor’s Father, Prince Ch’un, was the seventh brother of the Emperor Hsien-Feng, hence his Chinese name of “Seventh Prince.”

===Chinese===
七王爷（醇亲王）想必是个极有趣的人物。他是咸丰帝的兄弟——咸丰帝即当今皇太后的丈夫；而他的妻子，也就是当今皇上的生母，则是皇太后的妹妹。在先帝以及当今皇上幼年的部分时日里，两宫太后——即当今皇太后与东宫太后——共同垂帘听政，这位王爷是两位太后极为倚重的挚友；直至他临终之前，他仍是摄政体制中最受信任的谋臣之一。无论外国人还是中国人，都公认他是一位开明的王爷，品行端方。他所享有的声望，或许与他的次子被选为先帝同治——同治帝无嗣而崩——的继嗣有些关联。中国的皇上及其枢臣可以择定皇储。若仅有一子，便以此子为嗣；若有数子，则从中择一最宜此大位者。若无子嗣，则从侄辈中选立储君，不拘年齿长幼，亦不论是兄长之子还是幼弟之子。当今皇上之父醇亲王，是咸丰帝的第七个兄弟，因此中国人称他为「七王爷」。

## XIII The Steam-Launch—Semiannual Sacrifices to Confucius / 第十三章 汽船——祭孔的春秋两祭

===Original===
We began now to go out on the lake in the steam-launches, instead of the picturesque Imperial barge. The Empress Dowager is artistic and conservative enough to like the old-fashioned barge; but she is also intelligent enough to appreciate the advantages of other modes of locomotion, and has no prejudices; in fact, she rather likes trying new things. When the days were long, the air soft, and the bosom of the lake engirdled with its chain of blooming lotus, she preferred the barge; but when the shorter and cooler days came, when the lotus were no longer in bloom, she ordered the steam-launch for our promenades. She seemed now to like its swift and noisy progress as much as she had before enjoyed the softly gliding motion of the barge. Her Throne on the launch was on the prow, just outside and above the cabin, where the Princesses and Ladies sat. Her Majesty always wanted the fresh air and the view, and never went inside. The young Empress and the Ladies sat within the luxuriously fitted up cabin with its lounges and tables.

The first day we went out in the launch the engineer seemed not to have it quite under control, and we soon ran aground in a field of water-plants near the island. There was great consternation among the eunuchs when it was found the launch could not advance, even by putting on full steam. The engineer didn't seem to know what to do. Her Majesty ordered the engines reversed, and this was tried, but it was some time before the launch moved. The Princesses and eunuchs became quite excited, but Her Majesty was perfectly unconcerned, and laughed at their fears for her safety. She said it would be no great matter for her to walk over to the island. It would only mean one pair of shoes the less! When the launch finally moved, the Chief Eunuch, not wishing to run the risk of another mishap, wanted to give word to the engineer to return; but Her Majesty would not hear of this, and insisted upon completing the excursion as she had at first planned it. We had several other mishaps, and the launch finally ran aground; and no effort of the engineers, no putting on of extra steam, was able to get us off again. Her Majesty kept her good humor, ordered her barge brought alongside, and we were all transshipped. We finished our tour on the lake as she had planned it, but in the barge instead of the launch. She is too intelligent not to use any means at hand to attain her ends, and she is intelligent enough to see that these ends can be attained, by some means or other, before she fixes upon them.

===Chinese===
我们如今出游湖上，不再乘那如画般的御船，改乘汽船了。皇太后既有艺术家的性情、又保守得足以偏爱旧式的御船；可她也聪慧得足以赏识别种行船方式的便利，且全无成见；说实在的，她倒颇爱尝新。白昼长、风日柔和、湖面环着一圈盛开的荷花之时，她偏爱御船；可等到日短天凉、荷花不再之时，她便吩咐汽船，供我们出游。如今她似乎十分中意汽船那又快又喧闹的行进，一如她先前享受御船那轻柔滑行的妙处。她在汽船上的宝座设在船头，正在舱外、舱顶之上，王妃与命妇们则坐在舱内。皇太后总贪着新鲜空气与开阔的景致，从不进舱里去。皇后和命妇们坐在那陈设奢华的舱内，里有躺椅与桌案。

头一回乘汽船出门那一日，轮机手似乎操纵不大灵便，不多时我们便在岛边一片水草丛中搁了浅。待发觉即便开足马力汽船也不能前进，太监们大惊失色。轮机手似乎也不知所措。皇太后吩咐倒车，试了一试，可过了好一阵子汽船才动起来。王妃和太监们都慌作一团，皇太后却泰然自若，反笑他们为她的安危担惊受怕。她说她大可以步行上岛去，至多不过少一双鞋罢了！汽船终于动了之后，总管太监怕再出岔子，想吩咐轮机手回去；皇太后却不依，定要照她原定的计划把这趟出游走完。其后我们又遇了几回岔子，汽船到底还是搁了浅；任轮机手们怎样设法，任怎样额外加足马力，也再不得脱身。皇太后神色自若，吩咐将她的御船驶来靠在旁边，我们一行人都换过了船。我们终如她原先所定那般游完了湖，只不过乘的是御船，而非汽船。她太聪明，不会不善用一切手边可用的法子来成事；她又足够聪明，能看出这些事总可以如此那般地办成，方才将它们定下。

===Original===
The Emperor of China, with the usual Chinese tolerance—and the Chinese are the most tolerant people in the world as to religious faith—is not only the head of one church, but of all the churches in China. He is, as Emperor, the Great High Priest of Heaven, the High Priest of Buddhism and Taoism, and is, of course, a Confucian; though this is a philosophy rather than a religion. But though a philosophy, there are certain rites and ceremonies observed by the Confucians. All the great ceremonies of the different cults are celebrated in the Palace temples with rigid impartiality and equal pomp. Whatever may be the individual leanings of the Emperor, and, of course, he must have his own preferences, he participates in each of these celebrations. But his official, public exercise of religion, is limited to the worship of Heaven and Earth, to which he makes annual public sacrifices in the Great Temple of Heaven at Peking.

===Chinese===
中国的皇上，依着中国人素来的宽容——而在宗教信仰一事上，中国人实是世上最宽容的国民——不只是一教之首，而是中国诸教之首。他既为皇上，便是祭天的大祭司，是佛教与道教的大祭司，自然也是孔门之徒；虽说孔门之学与其说是宗教，不如说是哲学。然而既是哲学，孔门也有其一定的礼仪与典礼。各教所有的大典，都在宫中的庙宇里，以同样一丝不苟的公允、同样隆重的排场举行。不论皇上本人的心性如何倾向——他自然也有自己的偏好——这些庆典他每一项都要与祭。但他那正式的、公开的宗教仪轨，却仅限于天地之祀，每年在北京的天坛，向天地作公开的祭祀。

===Original===
The afternoon of our first steam-launch excursion, finished in Her Majesty's barge, there was a splendid ceremony in the chapel at the foot of the hill crowned with the Temple of the Ten Thousand Buddhas, to the memory of Confucius, the great Sage, whose philosophy has directed the lives and laws of the Chinese people for nearly twenty-five hundred years. Though a philosopher like Plato, he is appreciated and his teachings followed by the masses, as well as the classes, in China. He is not a religious leader but an ethical teacher, and though many temples have been erected to his memory, they are like Halls of Science and not temples to a divinity. There are no images either of Confucius or the Sages in these temples. They are classic halls, bare of all church-like ornamentation. Quotations from the "analects," painted on scrolls, cut into wood, and carved out of stone, adorn the walls, not only of the interiors of the temples, but of the courts and verandahs of the buildings. At the place where the altar would be in a temple, there is a plain niche, painted in red with a tablet bearing an inscription in gold, "The Seat of the Perfect One." On either side are similar niches, containing the tablets of four other great Sages, among whom was Mencius. These semiannual sacrifices are in commemoration of Confucius as an ethical teacher, a wise philosopher, a Sage. At this service in the Palace, the participants and celebrants were all in full Court dress. There was an address to the memory of the great Sage, with music and hymns; the latter were rhythmic verses, containing some truth inculcated by the Sage. There was an altar with a dragon table in front for offerings. There were sacrifices, incense, and music. The altar was rich with splendid vases, rare old bronze bowls, and incense-burners, and sweet with flowers and fruit. On the dragon table, which stood in front, were offerings of millet, meat, and wine. Tall cressets of open ironwork containing huge, burning pine-knots were placed in front of the raised platform, on which stood the altar, which was beautifully illuminated with tall candles in square, silver candelabra. The court in front of this temple, as well as the surrounding buildings, were hung with charming painted lanterns.

===Chinese===
我们头一回乘汽船出游——末了改在皇太后的御船里收场——那日的下半天，在那座山顶冠以万佛寺的山脚下，庙堂之中正举行着一场盛大的典礼，为的是追念大圣人孔子，他的哲学引导中国人的生活与律法，已近两千五百年。他虽是与柏拉图一样的哲学家，在中国却为大众所推崇、所遵从，并不分贵贱上下。他并非宗教领袖，而是伦理之师，虽则有许多庙宇是为追念他而建，这些庙宇却更近于学问之堂，而非供奉神祇的庙宇。这些庙里既没有孔子的塑像，也没有诸位贤人的塑像。它们是典雅的殿宇，不带一丝教堂似的装饰。从《论语》中摘出的语录，或绘于卷轴，或刻于木上，或雕于石上，装点着墙壁——不仅在庙宇之内，也在这些建筑的庭院与游廊之上。庙中本该设神坛之处，只有一个素朴的神龛，漆作红色，内供一牌位，上书金字的题铭：「至圣先师之位」。两侧亦有相似的神龛，奉着另外四位大贤的牌位，其中之一便是孟子。这春秋两祭，乃是为追念孔子之为伦理之师、睿智之哲、一代圣人。宫中举行此仪之时，与祭者和主祭者皆着朝服全套。典礼中有向大圣人追念之辞，并伴有乐与颂歌；颂歌是有韵的诗句，内中含有圣人所教诲的某番道理。设有神坛，坛前有一张龙案用以陈设祭品。有祭祀、有香、有乐。坛上陈列华美的花瓶、罕见的古铜碗与香炉，富丽堂皇，又有鲜花鲜果，芬芳袭人。陈在前面的龙案上，供奉着黍、肉、酒。露铁制成的高大灯架，里头燃着粗大的松木疙瘩，分立在祭坛所凭的高台之前；台上所设的祭坛，由方形的银烛台里高高的蜡烛照得一片辉煌。庙前的庭院，以及四围的建筑，都悬挂着玲珑可爱的彩绘灯笼。

===Original===
Their Majesties, with the Empress and Ladies, preceded and surrounded by eunuchs and officials, in full Court dress, went in ceremonious procession through the verandahed corridors, from Her Majesty's Throne-room to the temple. Their approach was accompanied by the slow beating of drums. When they reached the temple, three yellow cushions were placed on the paved floor for Their Majesties and the Empress, and red cushions for the Ladies. The music was played in rhythmic strains, while Their Majesties knelt and prostrated themselves three times; the Empress and Ladies doing likewise. The officials and other participants knelt outside in the court. When the prostrations were finished, a yellow chair was brought for the Empress Dowager. She sat during the rest of the service, but the Emperor, the Empress, and Ladies remained standing during the whole celebration. This consisted of a number of genuflections and prostrations by the celebrants, and a moving about of the offerings on the dragon table in a ceremonious and reverent manner. The chief officiant read the address from a long scroll. After finishing it, he placed it on a casket on the altar. The first part of the ceremony took place inside the temple, then the celebrants went out into the court and intoned the six hymns and made renewed prostrations. I was not able to understand enough of the hymns, or to get them sufficiently translated to make out their meaning. They were all of uniform length. They were in praise of Confucius and were called "Odes to Peace." When all the verses were intoned, the scroll with the address, some of each of the offerings, were placed in the huge iron incense-burner, that stood in the center of the outer court, and set on fire by the chief celebrant, while one of the several flagons of wine that had made part of the offerings was poured over the blaze.

===Chinese===
皇上皇太后，连同皇后与命妇们，前后左右由太监和官员们开道簇拥，俱着朝服全套，从皇太后的宝座殿出发，经那有游廊的过道，排开仪仗，迤逦走向庙堂。他们一行将近时，伴有缓慢的鼓声。到了庙前，便在铺砖的地上铺下三个明黄的拜垫，给皇上皇太后和皇后跪用，又给命妇们铺下红的拜垫。奏起有节拍的乐声，皇上皇太后跪下，行三跪之礼；皇后和命妇们也随同行礼。官员和其余与祭之人都在外头庭院中跪下。拜毕，便为皇太后取来一把明黄的椅子。余下的仪程里她都坐着，而皇上、皇后和命妇们在整场庆典中始终站立。这庆典乃是由主祭者们行若干屈膝与叩拜之礼，并以庄敬恭谨的仪态，将龙案上的祭品挪移搬动一番。主祭的司仪从一卷长长的卷轴上宣读祭文。读完之后，便将它供在坛上的一个匣子里。仪程的前半在庙内举行，随后主祭者们走到外头庭院中，吟诵六首颂歌，再行叩拜。那些颂歌我听懂的太少，也未能一一译出，故而无从尽得其义。它们都是同一长度，乃是赞颂孔子之作，名为《和平颂》。待所有诗句吟诵完毕，那卷写着祭文的卷轴，连同每样祭品各取一些，都放进外院正中那只硕大的铁香炉里，由主祭者点火焚化；同时将那作为祭品的几壶酒之一，倾浇在火焰之上。

===Original===
I had not expected to enter the temple with Their Majesties and the Ladies, but when we reached the door, the Empress drew me in with her. They seemed to realize that I enjoyed seeing these celebrations and to perfectly understand my not taking any active part in them. I always remained standing, but I listened reverently to the intoning of the hymns and the reading of the address. I conducted myself as I would at any religious ceremony, and they seemed to appreciate it.

===Chinese===
我原未曾指望随皇上皇太后和命妇们进到庙里去，可我们到了门口时，皇后把我拉了进去，与她一同入内。她们似乎明白我乐于观看这些庆典，也完全体谅我不亲自参与其事。我始终立在一旁，却恭恭敬敬地倾听颂歌的吟诵与祭文的宣读。我举止一如在任一宗教仪式上那样，她们似乎颇以为然。

===Original===
When all was finished, Her Majesty told me to go up to the altar and examine the rare, old, bronze ornaments, the candelabra, etc. They explained to me that the address, which had been read, was burned, as it had filled its mission when it was read; that the ashes of a literary essay were a most fitting offering to the memory of Confucius, the great philosopher. When all was over, Their Majesties ordered the boats to come to the foot of the terrace, where the last part of the celebration had been made, and we returned to the Palace by way of the lake.

===Chinese===
待一切都完毕，皇太后叫我走上坛去，细看那些罕见的古老铜制陈设、银烛台等等。她们向我讲解说，方才所读的祭文所以要焚化，乃因它既已宣读，便算功成身退了；又说一篇辞章的灰烬，正是献给大哲孔子最为贴切的祭品。一切既毕，皇上皇太后吩咐将船唤到那举行仪程最后一节的露台脚下，我们便由湖上回到宫里去。

## XVII The Summer Palace and Its Grounds / 第十七章 颐和园及其苑囿

===Original===
The Summer Palace, the Empress Dowager’s favorite residence, is really a superb domain. Its naturally picturesque situation among the beautiful Western Hills, sixteen miles from Peking, has been improved wherever possible, by the devices of art. The many buildings that constitute an Oriental Palace have been most picturesquely grouped on the banks of its great lake. The eminences and natural undulations have all been made the most of as sites for Palaces and temples, and the grounds are laid out with all the art the Chinese landscape artist has at his command.

===Chinese===
颐和园是皇太后最为钟爱的行宫，实为一处极壮丽的苑囿。其地坐落於秀美的西山之中，距北京十六英里，天然形胜本已绝胜，复经匠心巧思，凡可增益之处无不锦上添花。构成一座东方宫苑的众多殿宇楼阁，错落有致地布列于其大湖之畔。山丘高地、地势起伏之处，皆被尽善利用，以为宫苑寺观之基址；而整座苑囿的布局，则尽显中国造园家胸中的全部匠心。

===Original===
The buildings of the Palace proper, where Their Majesties and their suites live, are all massed in one great town-like group at the southeastern end of the lake. In this group are the Theater, with its courts, and the Great Audience Hall. Palaces, temples, summerhouses, tea-booths, dot the whole surface of the great park, and all the vantage-points have been utilized for constructions.

===Chinese===
正宫的一群建筑——即皇上、皇太后及随扈人等所居之处——悉数集中成一片城镇般的庞大建筑群，坐落於湖的东南端。这片建筑群之中，有戏楼及其院落，亦有正大光明殿。宫苑、寺观、凉亭、茶肆，点缀着整座广阔御园的各处，凡属形胜之地，皆已用以兴筑。

===Original===
A beautiful white marble terrace runs the length of the southern side of the lake. Pavilions at intervals vary the monotony of this line or give accent to the natural indentations of the banks. Picturesque landing-places, with their marble steps lapped by the waters of the lake, also lend their variety to this terrace surmounted by its beautiful lotus balustrade.

===Chinese===
一道秀美的汉白玉石台沿湖的南岸绵延。台之上每隔一段便有一座亭阁，或打破这道线条的单调，或恰好烘托出湖岸天然凹入的轮廓。一座座如画的码头，其大理石的阶矶被湖水轻轻拍打，也为这道石台平添了几分变化，台之上更环以秀美的莲花栏杆。

===Original===
The highest of the hills in the park of the Summer Palace is crowned by the Great Temple of the Ten Thousand Buddhas. This is approached by hundreds of steps, which lead up from the broadest part of the marble terrace over the lake. Beautiful Palaces are built along this terraced height. Picturesque *pai-lou* (memorial arches) are built at such beautiful points, that Nature herself seems to have designed these positions for them.

===Chinese===
颐和园园内最高的那座山，山顶上坐落着大万佛寺。由大理石台最宽处跨湖而上，需登数百级石阶方能抵达。沿着这一带层叠的高地，建有秀美的宫苑。那如画的牌楼（纪念性的牌坊）便建在这些绝胜之处，仿佛大自然亲手为它们择定了位置。

===Original===
A fair, verdure-clad island lies peacefully on the bosom of the lake, and the Palace and temple built thereon seem a part of the natural formation of stone out of which they rise. A graceful seventeen-arched bridge of white marble connects this island with the northern bank of the lake.

===Chinese===
一座苍翠欲滴的秀丽小岛静谧地偎依於湖上，岛上所建的宫苑与寺观，仿佛便是其下岩层自然生长而出的一部分。一座姿态优雅的十七孔汉白玉桥，将这座小岛与湖的北岸连接起来。

===Original===
The canal from Peking, which feeds the lake, winds in and out of the grounds in such graceful meanderings as to seem some fair mountain stream. The outlets to the lakes are spanned by the graceful camel-backed bridges that only the Chinese architects build. Nature and art are everywhere so blended, so harmonized, it is difficult to tell which is which. The simple lines and beautiful proportions and harmonious colors of the one-storied Chinese buildings make even these seem almost a part of the landscape.

===Chinese===
自北京而来的运河——湖水即由其补给——蜿蜒穿行於苑囿之间，那柔和的回环曲折，竟仿佛一道秀美的山涧。各处湖水出口，皆有姿态优美的驼背石桥飞架其上，此种桥唯中国的匠人方能造得。自然与人工处处交融，处处和谐，竟令人难以分辨孰为天成、孰为人巧。中国的一层建筑，线条简洁，比例秀美，色彩调和，便使这些殿宇也几乎成了这片山水的一部分。

===Original===
